Open Suminagashi — The Marbling Tray ↗
001

Suminagashi — The Marbling Tray

A quiet marbling bench seen from above: a shallow tray of paper-coloured water set in a dark wooden rim, lit by a soft band of morning light. On load seven drops of indigo, ox-gall, vermilion, celadon and ochre swell one after another into two sets of nested rings that press against each other, and a five-tine comb then draws itself across them, leaving feathered chevrons. The viewer keeps working the same water: every new drop shoves the existing bands outward, every drag pulls sharp feathers through them, and the fan stirs the whole surface in one slow gyre. Type stays tiny and deliberate, on a torn paper slip and a hanging vertical colophon strip stamped with the red seal of the invented Ukigumo Workshop. The tools are drawn objects resting on the wood. Pulling a print is the reward: a sheet unrolls over the water, takes the ink, turns over and flies to the drying rack, leaving a pale ghost behind for the next layer.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A full-bleed marbling tray seen from directly above: the viewer practises suminagashi / ebru paper marbling. Concept: ink floats on still water; every touch displaces what is already there. Implement real mathematical marbling on Canvas 2D: each ink drop is a closed polyline (a circle of several hundred vertices); a new drop at point C with radius r pushes every existing vertex P outward by P' = C + (P − C)·sqrt(1 + r²/|P − C|²), so earlier rings deform into nested, squeezed bands. Dragging pulls a "tine" through the ink: vertices are displaced along the stroke direction with exponential falloff by perpendicular distance (the classic tine-line formula), producing feathered combs and chevrons. Resample polylines when segments stretch so curves stay smooth. Mood: calm, tactile, a quiet workshop in morning light. Palette: water/paper #F3ECDD, indigo #1B2A49, vermilion #C4452D, celadon teal #2F7F79, ochre #E0A526, plus clear "ox-gall" drops in the paper colour that open white holes. The tray has a thin dark wooden rim (#3A2A1E) with a soft inner shadow, and the water carries faint drifting caustic light and paper grain. The rim widens into a wooden ledge on one side to hold the tools. Typography: tiny refined type. Title set small in letterspaced Georgia small caps on a paper slip at the tray's corner, with a hanging paper strip carrying a vertical line of Japanese-style colophon text (English words, vertical writing mode) and a hand-drawn red seal (SVG) reading the invented studio name "Ukigumo Workshop". Tools sit on the tray rim as drawn objects, not UI chrome: five ink bowls (choose colour), a single stylus, a five-tine comb, a fan (gently swirls everything with a curl field), a stack of paper ("Pull a print"), a strip of paper ("Skim the tray") and a small brass bowl that switches optional sound on (off by default). Labels in 10–11px small caps. Entrance: on load the tray is not empty: seven drops fall one after another in a slow rhythm (ease-out expansion of each ring, 600ms, cubic-bezier(.2,.8,.2,1)), then one comb pass draws itself, so the first three seconds already show a finished-looking marbled pattern forming. Interaction: click/tap drops ink (hold longer = bigger drop, ring grows while held); drag with stylus or comb to pull tines; keyboard 1–5 selects inks (S and C pick stylus and comb, F fans, P pulls a print, K skims; with the tray focused, arrow keys move a cursor and Space drops ink). Signature moment: the "Pull a print" control lays a sheet over the tray: a paper rectangle unrolls across the canvas with a soft shadow line, the ink soaks faintly through its back, then it lifts off and turns over carrying the pattern (mirrored, as a real print is), shrinks and lands in a strip of finished prints along the bottom (or side on wide screens), each slightly rotated with a deckled edge and a small red chop; the tray is left with only a pale ghost of ink. Clicking a print enlarges it with a short caption naming the inks used and the time it was pulled. "Skim the tray" clears it with a paper strip dragged across. Reduced motion: no intro animation, pattern appears pre-made, no caustics, prints appear on the rack without the flight. Copy is sparse and poetic: one line under the title, "Ink floats. Water remembers everything you do to it."
001-suminagashi-marbling-tray.txt

Open Aerodrome Lisbeth — Airship Departures ↗
002

Aerodrome Lisbeth — Airship Departures

A dark terminal hall with one object in it: an enamel-framed Solari board for an airship aerodrome that never existed. The page opens on blank flaps; within three seconds a wave of clattering characters rolls from the top-left to the bottom-right and resolves into a full schedule of airships whose times are anchored to the viewer's own clock. From then on the board lives by itself: the flap clock ticks every second, statuses creep from ON TIME through GAS CHECK and BALLASTING to BOARDING and DEPARTED, rows expire and the whole board shuffles up in another cascade, a yellow dot-matrix notice crawls along the bottom with deadpan announcements, and a windsock below drifts with an invented wind. Brass hardware on the frame (a toggle switch for the clatter, a pull lever, a maker's plate) invites touching, and every row opens into a small flap-built manifest with its captain, gas and a purser's remark.

↗ Open

Original prompt

The main departures board of an invented airship terminal, "Aerodrome Lisbeth, Mast Field East", built as a mechanical split-flap (Solari) display that fills the screen. Concept: a dashboard that is physically believable; every character is a real two-part flap that flips through intermediate characters to reach its target. Palette: board black #0F0F0E, flap face #1A1A18 with a hairline split, character white #F2EFE4, signal yellow #F5C400 for status words and the header rule, alarm red-orange #D4401F for "CANCELLED/STORM HOLD". Dark page. Subtle texture: flaps have a faint top-light gradient, the hinge line casts a thin shadow, the board has a brushed enamel frame with small rivets and a brass maker's plate ("Lisbeth Signalwerk, Type 4"). Typography: flap characters in a condensed bold sans stack ("Arial Narrow", "Helvetica Neue Condensed", Impact-like fallback, squeezed horizontally to fit a flap if the fallback is wide) in caps; header "DEPARTURES · ABFLUG · PARTENZE" in wide-tracked small caps under the station name and a yellow airship pictogram; a large clock built from flaps showing the viewer's actual local time (HH:MM:SS, seconds flip every second) with the local weekday and date beside it. Content: 11 rows (10 on phones, with fewer columns): TIME, DESTINATION, SHIP, MAST, STATUS, plus a small signal lamp per row (blinking yellow while boarding, steady red for holds and cancellations). Invented destinations and ships with a consistent world: e.g. 06:40 SALT MERIDIAN / LZ HALCYON / MAST 3 / BOARDING; CAPE VERRAT; THE UPPER LANTERNS; NEW ODESSA SKYPORT; PORT ISOLDE; statuses: ON TIME, MOORED, GAS CHECK, BALLASTING, BOARDING, DEPARTED, DELAYED, STORM HOLD, CANCELLED. The schedule is derived from the real clock: departures are generated deterministically one to three minutes apart around now; each ship's status steps forward as its real departure time approaches, departed rows expire about half a minute later and the board shifts up, so on average something changes roughly every 20 seconds and whenever a departure time passes. Signature moment: the cascade. When the board updates, every changed cell flips forward through the drum in order (A→B→C…, like a real Solari, ~55ms per flap). Each flap is drawn as a 3D fold: the top half swings down about the hinge, foreshortening and darkening as it turns from the light, and its back face, carrying the lower half of the next character, lands on the bottom half with a small bounce on the final flap. Flips are staggered left-to-right and top-to-bottom so a wave of motion rolls down the board. The flaps are painted on small canvases from pre-rendered sprite sheets so hundreds can turn at once without dropping frames. On load the entire board starts blank and resolves in a roughly 3-second cascade. Optional sound (off by default; a designed toggle shaped like a small brass switch): each flap makes a tiny filtered-noise tick via Web Audio, so a cascade becomes a clatter. Interaction: clicking/tapping a row (or Enter/Space on it) expands a flap-built detail strip under it (captain, expected time, lifting gas, cruise altitude, passengers aboard, and a wry purser's note like "Cabin band: the Ostend Trio"); filter chips ALL / BOARDING / DELAYED, with live counts, re-sort the board with a cascade, bringing matching rows to the top and dimming the rest; pressing R or pulling the brass "Next announcement" lever forces an update (the head row is cleared, everything shifts up and the next announcement begins). Pressing the maker's plate runs a self-test in which every flap makes one full revolution. A scrolling single-line announcement in a yellow 5×7 dot-matrix, stepping one dot column at a time over a lattice of unlit dots, sits at the bottom ("Passengers for Cape Verrat are reminded that kites are not permitted on the promenade deck."). Below the board: a small fictional wind and pressure panel with an animated windsock drawn in SVG, a compass dial, flap readouts and a barograph trace. Phone layout: fewer characters per column, abbreviated destinations and statuses, SHIP column folded into the detail strip. Reduced motion: flaps change with a quick crossfade instead of cycling, the announcement becomes static wrapped text and the windsock holds still.
002-airship-terminal-split-flap.txt

Open The Anatomy of a Croissant ↗
003

The Anatomy of a Croissant

A long-read feature from the invented magazine Crumb Quarterly, No. 14, laid out like a Sunday supplement crossed with a draughtsman's sheet: flour-coloured paper with grain and stray crumbs, a towering Didone headline with a swashed italic "of a", a drop cap, small-caps captions and monospaced measurements. A hand-drawn croissant sits on the title spread; below it, five chapters pair Odile Marchetti's wry essay in the left column with a sticky technical figure on the right that is driven by the reader's scroll. Butter is posted into an envelope of dough, a slab folds in thirds while a counter ticks 1, 3, 9, 27, a triangle winds into a spiral in plan and elevation, and finally the cross-section proofs and bakes: butter lines open into a honeycomb of alveoli, the outline swells by forty percent, the crust browns, steam escapes and a thermometer climbs to 200 °C. The piece closes with tasting notes and a troubleshooting table of the author's own mistakes.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A food-magazine editorial feature that explains lamination, built around one large SVG cross-section of a croissant that transforms as the reader scrolls. Concept: geometry you can eat: three letter folds make 27 layers of butter (3×3×3), steam inflates them into a honeycomb. Mood: warm, generous, precise; a Sunday supplement with the rigour of a technical drawing. Palette: flour paper #FBF3E0, butter #F7D98B, baked gold #E9B44C, crust brown #8A4B14, espresso ink #3A2412. Light page with a faint paper grain (SVG turbulence filter at low opacity) and occasional scattered crumbs/flour dust drawn as tiny irregular dots. Typography: classic serif editorial. A huge Didot/Bodoni-style display headline ("The Anatomy of a Croissant") with extreme thick–thin contrast, set in two lines with a swash-like italic "of a"; a drop cap; body in Georgia/Palatino at comfortable measure in a two-column asymmetrical grid; captions and measurements in small caps and a monospace for numbers (27 layers, a 1:3 butter-to-dough ratio by weight — 280 g of butter to 840 g of dough made from 500 g of flour — 16 °C butter, a 2 h proof at 26 °C and a 200 °C oven for 18 minutes are generic baking facts; quantities are plausible and described as the author's recipe, summarised in a small "recipe in numbers" card beside the introduction). Margin notes with thin leader lines pointing into the diagram. Pull quote: "A croissant is mostly a decision not to rush." Structure (long vertical scroll with a sticky diagram column on desktop, inline diagrams on phone): 1) Title spread with a whole croissant drawn in SVG (crescent built from overlapping segments with gradient shading and flaky stroke marks). 2) "The Block": a butter slab enclosed in dough. 3) "Three Turns": an animated fold diagram: the dough folds in thirds three times, a counter ticks 3 → 9 → 27 as stripes multiply. 4) "The Roll": a triangle rolls up into a spiral. 5) "The Oven": the signature moment. 6) "The Shatter": tasting notes and a small "troubleshooting" table (butter leaked: too warm; bready crumb: under-proofed; etc.). Signature moment: the sticky cross-section. It begins as a tight spiral of 27 thin alternating dough/butter lines; as the reader scrolls through "The Oven", scroll progress drives proofing and baking: the layers separate, air cells open between them into an irregular honeycomb (alveoli drawn as morphing paths), the outline swells by about 40%, colour moves from pale dough to deep gold, and wisps of steam (animated stroke-dashoffset paths) escape. A thermometer in the margin climbs with the scroll. Hovering or tapping any layer in the cross-section highlights it and names it with a leader-line tag and a one-line definition (crust, membrane, alveolus, butter line; before baking the same parts answer to skin, dough sheet and butter line). Three legend chips under the figure (crust, membrane, butter line/alveolus) highlight every layer of that kind at once and make the naming keyboard-operable. A readout row under the figure reports stage, clock, air temperature and width gained. Motion: scroll-linked with eased interpolation (lerp towards target so it never jumps), section headings slide in from the left with a clip-path wipe rather than fade-up. Small surprise: clicking the whole croissant on the title spread tears off its end with a scatter of crumbs that fall with gravity; a second click tears the other end and a third bakes a fresh one. Reduced motion: diagrams show their final states with a stepper instead of scroll-linked morphing. Writing voice: a knowledgeable baker-essayist, specific and a little wry; about 500–700 words of real copy, bylined to the invented writer Odile Marchetti for the invented magazine "Crumb Quarterly, No. 14".
003-anatomy-of-a-croissant.txt

Open Night Watch at Sorrow Rock ↗
004

Night Watch at Sorrow Rock

A night sea in near-black ink strokes, a red-banded lighthouse on its rock, and one heavy, warm beam that is the whole game. The page opens on darkness with the title lettered across the water in tall capitals that can only be read where the slowly turning lamp falls on them. Lighting the lamp starts a four-minute night from 21:00 to 05:00: trawlers, schooners and steamers come out of the dark edges with only a lantern to show for themselves, steering for the harbour mouth, and they will avoid only the rocks the viewer has held the beam on. Everything outside the cone is guesswork and foam glints; everything inside it turns to true colour, with gold wave-crests, sparkling motes, lit sails and long shadows thrown away from the tower. Fog banks swallow the beam, a storm brings rain and swell, and at the end the sea pales to dawn and the whole reef stands revealed. Beside the sea runs the keeper's ledger, where the hour, three lanterns for remaining chances and every arrival and wreck are written in Georgia italic as they happen; the night closes on the finished log page with the keeper's own verdict on the watch.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A small, atmospheric arcade game: you are the keeper of a lighthouse on a reef and you steer the beam. Top-down-ish view of a night sea on a full-bleed Canvas 2D. The lighthouse sits on a rock near the bottom centre (on phones) or lower left third (desktop); ships enter from the dark edges heading for the harbour mouth (two breakwaters with a red and a green light and a few lit windows, in the bottom-left corner), and can only see the rocks your beam has recently lit. Concept: light as the only game mechanic. Palette: night sky/sea #07131F, deeper water bands #0E2A3A, beam and lamp #F6E7A8, danger/wreck red #E2543C, sea-foam grey-green #7FA7A6. Dark page, hand-drawn feel: the sea is rendered as layered horizontal wave strokes that drift slowly; rocks are jagged dark silhouettes with a foam ring; ships are small detailed silhouettes (trawler, schooner with sails, steamer with smoke) with a faint lantern of their own, a lantern reflection and a short dotted heading tick. Mechanics: the beam is a cone (about 18° wide) that follows the pointer/finger angle with smooth inertia (spring-damped rotation with a capped turning speed so it feels like a heavy lens), or A/D and arrow keys. Rocks and fog banks are invisible in the dark apart from occasional foam glints, which come faster when an unwarned ship is bearing down on that rock. A rock must be held in the beam for a moment (about 0.4 s; a dashed outline draws itself round it) to be revealed; it then stays "remembered" for a few seconds, shown as a fading dashed outline with the rock's name in small italics (the Sisters, Widow's Teeth, Black Maggie…). A ship that comes within sight of a remembered rock charts it for good (her lantern answers with an expanding ring) and steers around it; an unlit ship sails straight on and wrecks (red flare rocket, hull breaks in two and sinks, a bell tolls, a broken red mast is left on the rock). Each ship safely in harbour adds to the log. Three wrecks end the watch. Difficulty ramps through the night: more ships, drifting fog banks that swallow the beam beyond them and slow the revealing of rocks behind, a storm phase with rain streaks (brightest inside the beam), heavier swell, shorter memory and occasional lightning that silhouettes the reef for an instant. A night lasts roughly 4 minutes, shown as a clock from 21:00 to 05:00 with the sky paling to dawn (#3B5368) at the end, when the reef becomes visible by daylight and the lamp is put out. Signature moment: the beam itself. Render it volumetrically: additive gradient cone with soft edges and faint lens shafts, dust/moisture motes sparkling inside it, the lit patch of sea showing wave highlights and true colours while everything else is near-black; when the beam crosses a ship its sails and wake light up and it casts a long shadow across the water away from the lighthouse (rocks cast shorter ones, fog banks a wide one). The lens flashes with a starburst and a long horizontal streak when it sweeps past the viewer's direction (straight down the screen). Typography and UI: a keeper's log rather than a HUD: a narrow paper-coloured (#E9E2CF) ruled ledger strip (right side on desktop, top on portrait phones) in Georgia italic with entries written as they happen ("02:14 — Schooner Mary Elspeth brought in. Wind rising."), a tally of ships home / ships lost in small caps, and three small lantern icons for remaining chances. Title screen: the lighthouse standing in darkness, the title "Night Watch at Sorrow Rock" in tall letterspaced serif capitals that are only readable where the idle rotating beam passes over them (text revealed by the light, each letter keeping a brief afterglow; the beam lingers over the title and can also be steered by the viewer), a schooner drifting through the dark that becomes the first ship of the watch, the keeper's standing orders already written in the log, and "Light the lamp" as the start control. End screen: the completed log page with a rating written in the keeper's voice, a signature and a Lights Board stamp, and "Keep another watch". Optional sound toggle (off by default): low sea noise, rain, a bell for wrecks, a soft horn for arrivals, distant thunder, synthesised with Web Audio. Touch: drag anywhere to aim. Reduced motion: slower sea drift, no rain streaks, no flashes. Pause when the tab is hidden; P (or the pause button) holds the watch.
004-lighthouse-keeper-night-watch.txt

Open The Slide Rule ↗
005

The Slide Rule

A ten-inch ivory celluloid slide rule by the fictional makers Halloran & Voss lies on pale green-grey drafting paper, pushed in from the left before its scales engrave themselves in a left-to-right wipe and the glass cursor glides to 1. Every tick on K, A, B, CI, C, D and L is placed by logarithm, so the instrument genuinely computes: drag the slide and cursor and a strip of ruled notepaper reports the seven values under the red hairline and says in plain words what the setting works out. Index tabs on the notepaper run guided lessons in which the parts move with mechanical ease while blue pencil notes and dotted arrows write themselves on and the answer is ringed in red pencil. Below, a pencil sketch on the table restates the same setting as two hatched lengths added along a logarithmic line, next to a folded instruction leaflet that explains why it works and how to read a scale.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A fully working, accurate ten-inch logarithmic slide rule, drawn in SVG, presented like a prized engineering instrument on a drafting table. Concept: a tool that computes by sliding lengths; the page teaches the viewer to multiply with it in under a minute. Palette: ivory celluloid #F4EBD3, engraved black #1C1B19, red scale numerals #B3261E, blueprint blue accent #2B5C8A, aged edge/brass and table tones #C9B98F; the surface behind is a pale drafting-paper green-grey (#DCE3DA) with a faint 5mm grid and a few pencil construction marks. Light page. The instrument: a body (stock) with fixed scales, a central sliding strip, and a glass cursor with a red hairline. Scales, all computed from logarithms and engraved with correctly graded tick marks (long/medium/short ticks, finer subdivisions between 1 and 2 than between 9 and 10): on the stock A (two decades) and D (one decade), K (three decades) on the top edge, L (linear, for log10) on the bottom; on the slide B, CI (inverted, in red), C. Numerals in a small engineering-style caps face (a monospace or "Gill Sans"-like stack), with "π" gauge marks on C and D. Maker's mark engraved at left: "Halloran & Voss · No. 4081 · Precision Rule". Ends of the stock have small metal braces with screws. The cursor glass has a subtle highlight and magnifies nothing but casts a faint shadow. Interaction: drag the slide left/right (pointer or touch, with momentum and soft end stops); drag the cursor independently; arrow keys nudge the focused part (Shift for fine). A live readout panel styled as a strip of engineer's notepaper shows the values under the hairline on every scale to 3 significant figures, and states in words what the current setting computes: "C index over 2.00 on D. Cursor at 3.00 on C reads 6.00 on D → 2 × 3 = 6". Because the SVG is wide, on phones the rule is shown zoomed with horizontal panning inside its own frame (no page overflow), and a mini-map of the whole rule above. Signature moment: guided demonstrations. Three buttons styled as index tabs, "Multiply 2 × 3", "Divide 8.4 ÷ 2.1", "√ of 49 (A and D)", and a free "Try your own: a × b" input, animate the slide and cursor with eased, mechanical motion (cubic-bezier(.3,.0,.2,1) with a tiny overshoot and settle) while dotted blue annotation arrows and handwritten-style notes draw themselves on to explain each step, ending with the answer circled in red pencil. If a product runs off the scale, the demo correctly uses the other index. Typography: headline "The Slide Rule" small and confident in a slab or Georgia bold, with a subhead "Addition of lengths is multiplication of numbers", and a short, well-written three-paragraph explanation set in a narrow column like an instruction leaflet (log a + log b = log ab), plus a small "How to read a scale" inset. Entrance: the rule slides in from the left as if pushed across the table, the cursor glides to 1, the scales engrave themselves with a quick left-to-right wipe. Detail: a pencil and eraser crumbs lie on the table; hovering (or focusing, or tapping) scale letters shows what each scale is for. Beside the pencil, a live pencil sketch drawn on the drafting paper lays the two lengths currently set on the rule end to end along a two-decade logarithmic line (log a + log b = log ab, with the actual values), updating as the slide and cursor move. Reduced motion: demos jump stepwise with highlights instead of sliding.
005-working-slide-rule.txt

Open Volcán Chico Hot Sauce ↗
006

Volcán Chico Hot Sauce

A loud, flat-colour Memphis-Milano landing page for an invented hot sauce made by two cousins in a bus garage. Six colour fields (yellow, pink, teal, terrazzo cream, lava red, yellow) are separated by thick black rules and scrolling marquee strips; every block is strewn with outlined squiggles, triangles, dotted circles and striped slabs that slap on like stickers and then jitter on stepped loops. The wordmark is set letter by letter, each glyph rotated, outlined and hard-shadowed, next to a squat wax-capped flask with a smiling volcano on its label. The centrepiece is a chili-shaped thermometer: dragging its little face from 1 to 10 heats the whole page (faster jitter, wobbling sweating headlines, a rattling cap, steam at the edges, colour fields flipping to red and black) until the bottle blows its cap, confetti-lava rains across the viewport, a starburst shouts "¡AY!" and the slider springs back down on its own. Trading-card sauces, sauce-able pairings, a wonky origin note and an order pad that gets rubber-stamped round it out, all in a funny bilingual voice.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A landing page for an invented small-batch hot sauce brand, "Volcán Chico", designed in a loud, joyful Memphis-Milano manner: squiggles, confetti triangles, terrazzo dots, thick black outlines, hard drop shadows, clashing colour blocks. Deliberately maximal; the opposite of a tasteful dark SaaS page. Palette (exact): lava orange-red #FF4B1F, banana yellow #FFD60A, teal #00A6A6, bubblegum pink #FF7BAC, ink black #111111, cream #FFF8E7. Light, saturated colour-field sections that change colour block by block. Flat colour only: no gradients except on the sauce liquid, no blur. Typography: enormous chunky display type in a heavy sans stack (Impact, "Arial Black", "Helvetica Neue" black fallback), all caps, tightly leaded, with letters individually rotated a few degrees and offset black shadows; secondary type in a geometric sans (Futura/"Century Gothic"/"Trebuchet MS"); tiny labels in monospace like a price sticker. Headline: "VOLCÁN CHICO" with "small volcano, big opinions" beneath. Content and sections: 1) Hero: a big SVG bottle (squat flask, wax-dipped cap, label with a smiling little volcano) surrounded by animated Memphis shapes; hard-edged sticker "FERMENTED 90 DAYS". 2) The three sauces as trading-card style panels: "No. 1 Verde Tranquilo" (tomatillo, serrano, lime leaf, 2,000 SHU-ish, described as "a firm handshake"), "No. 2 Mango Disturbio" (habanero, mango, allspice, "a small civil disturbance"), "No. 3 La Erupción" (ghost pepper, smoked pineapple, black garlic, "call someone first"). 3) "How hot is hot?": the signature heat slider, with a second copy of the bottle beside it so the reaction and the eruption are in view, and a price-sticker readout naming each level. 4) "Put it on": a grid of quick pairings with tiny SVG food icons (eggs, oysters, pizza slice, vanilla ice cream "trust us", tacos, elote, mango, popcorn); tapping a tile splats sauce on its icon. 5) Origin story in a wonky text block: made in a converted bus garage in Oaxaca-inspired invented town "San Lázaro del Humo" by two cousins, Itzel and Beto. 6) Footer with a mock order form whose button says "Send me the trouble" and responds with a stamped "PEDIDO RECIBIDO" animation (no network). Signature moment: the heat slider, a custom-drawn thermometer-chili control from 1 to 10. As it rises the entire page reacts progressively: background shapes jitter faster, headline letters begin to wobble and sweat (SVG droplets slide down them), the bottle shakes and its cap rattles, colours invert towards red/black at 8+, steam squiggles rise from the edges, and at 10 the bottle erupts: a burst of confetti-lava shapes with ballistic physics fills the screen, the heading reads "¡AY!" for a moment, then the slider springs itself back down to level 2 and everything settles with a springy ease. Black marquee strips with bilingual one-liners divide the colour fields. Motion elsewhere: shapes drift and rotate on staggered loops with steps() and springy cubic-bezier(.34,1.56,.64,1) pops; cards tilt toward the cursor with a hard black shadow that shifts opposite; buttons depress into their shadow. Entrance: shapes slap onto the page one after another like stickers (scale from 1.6 with rotation, 40ms stagger), then the bottle drops in and bounces. The cursor on desktop is a small chili drawn in SVG. Reduced motion: static composition, slider still recolours but no shaking or burst. Voice: funny, bilingual sprinkles of Spanish, confident, never corporate.
006-volcan-chico-hot-sauce.txt

Open Core 7B — Eight Hundred Metres of Winter ↗
007

Core 7B — Eight Hundred Metres of Winter

A white, laboratory-quiet logging sheet built around a single continuous ice core. The reader scrolls from a line-art drill tower and summit camp at the snow surface down through 800 metres of procedurally generated ice: snowy, visibly banded firn; bubbly ice; a fractured brittle zone; glass-clear clathrate ice; dusty ice-age ice; gravel-laden basal ice; and finally brown bedrock. An enormous hairline depth numeral in the margin counts every tenth of a metre against an orange reading line, with an estimated age beneath it, while annotation cards and field-log entries by Dr. Ingrid Solheim-Okafor are pinned to exact depths by leader lines that draw themselves. Three live traces, a thin-section light-box and a depth minimap stay in view; the page itself cools slightly with depth. Any point of the core can be cut: a disc slides out, turns face-on and lands in the light-box as a mosaic of crystal grains under rotating crossed polarisers, while the air released from the cut bubbles up the page.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A scroll-driven story: the reader descends an ice core drilled on an invented ice cap ("Halvard Dome, core 7B"), from fresh snow at the surface to bedrock 800 metres down. The page is one tall, continuous vertical cylinder of ice running down the centre-left of a white laboratory-clean layout, with annotations pinned beside it like a core-logging sheet. Clearly fictional expedition data, but the science described is generic and true: annual layers thin with depth, firn compacts to ice, ash bands mark eruptions, bubbles hold old air, dust marks cold dry periods. Mood: clinical, quiet, vast. Palette: paper white #F7FBFD, ice pale #DCEAF2, glacier blue-grey #9DBBD0, deep navy ink #17324A, a single warm accent, ash/annotation orange-red #E4572E. Light page; the core itself gets subtly bluer and clearer with depth, and the page background cools by a few percent as you go down. Typography: tiny refined technical type. A monospace (Menlo/Consolas/Courier) for depths, ages and sample IDs; a clean grotesk (Helvetica/Arial) light weight for annotations; one enormous thin numeral: the current depth in metres, fixed in the margin, drawn as custom hairline digits and counting as you scroll (e.g. "412.6 m") with the estimated age below it ("≈ 5,100 years before drilling"), the current zone name and the local annual-layer thickness. Title at the top in wide-tracked caps: "CORE 7B" with a subtitle "Eight hundred metres of winter, read from the top". The core: drawn on canvas (or layered SVG) as a cylinder with soft side shading and a specular strip; inside, procedurally generated annual layers (thick and snowy-white near the top, compressing to hair-thin bands), firn granularity that fades out at about 70 m ("pore close-off"), scattered bubbles that shrink and elongate with depth and vanish into clathrate below ~600 m, dust bands, three dark ash layers, a melt layer ("one warm summer"), a brittle zone with fracture lines, and gravel-laden basal ice above brown bedrock. A depth ruler runs alongside with ticks every metre and labels every 10 m. Sticky side panel: three tiny live strip charts (isotope temperature proxy, dust, trapped CO2-like trace) whose current position follows the scroll, generated from smooth noise. Story beats as annotation cards connected to the core by thin leader lines (each appears with a line drawing itself, not a fade-up): the surface camp and last summer's snow; a layer the team could date by a known ash band; "the year without a summer" melt layer; the transition into ice-age ice where dust thickens; and field-log entries scattered down the core, ending with the last drill run, written by the invented glaciologist Dr. Ingrid Solheim-Okafor ("Day 41. The drill came up smelling of nothing, which is what twelve thousand years smells like."). Signature moment: tap or click any section of the core and it is "sampled": a thin disc is cut out and slides sideways into a light-box inset where it is shown in cross-polarised light: a mosaic of crystal grains in shifting pastel interference colours (Voronoi cells that recolour as the virtual polariser rotates), larger grains the deeper the sample, while bubbles released from the cut rise up the page and pop. On wide screens the light-box lives in the sticky side panel and one demonstration disc is cut automatically at the end of the entrance; on phones it opens as a sheet above a bottom bar that carries the depth numeral and the strip charts. Each cut leaves a visible gap and a sample tag on the core. Also: a drill-depth minimap at the right edge shows where you are in the 800 m and lets you jump. Small extra earth tones (a muted tan for dust, brown for basal debris and bedrock) are allowed beside the palette. An optional sound switch, off by default, adds a saw tick and bubble pops. Entrance: the drill tower in thin line art at the top, and the first metre of core extrudes downward. Reduced motion: no rising bubbles, static crystal mosaic.

Open Cabinet of Impossible Minerals ↗
008

Cabinet of Impossible Minerals

A hushed, single-lamp mineral gallery for seven specimens that cannot exist. A brass-framed vitrine holds one real-time raymarched crystal floating above a plum velvet plinth, lit by a lamp that slowly orbits so highlights slide from facet to facet; beside it hangs an ivory museum label set in Didot-style italics with a brass catalogue number, a small property table and a paragraph of deadpan curatorial fiction, and below runs a brass rail of seven numbered crystal-habit silhouettes. The page opens in darkness and the lamp warms up over a second and a half. Changing specimen dissolves the crystal facet by facet into glints while a reflection sweeps across the case glass, then the next mineral resolves as the label re-sets itself letter by letter in glowing brass. Switching the lamp to ultraviolet drops the room into violet, makes every mineral fluoresce in a colour it has no business being, and reveals one extra line on the label written in fluorescent ink.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A museum display case holding seven invented minerals, each a real-time raymarched crystal rendered with hand-written WebGL fragment shaders. Concept: a mineralogy gallery for specimens that cannot exist; the label copy is deadpan curatorial fiction, the rendering is seductive. Mood: hushed, luxurious, a dark gallery with one lamp. Palette: velvet black-plum #0E0B10, case interior aubergine #2A1E2E, brass #C9A45C for rules, frames and numbers, label card ivory #E8E2D6 with ink #1B1518; gem hues are specific to each specimen (e.g. a deep absinthe green #5FD08A, arterial garnet #A3122B, glacier blue #7FD6F2, uranium yellow #E6E04A, smoky violet #7A5CC2, black with rainbow sheen, milky opal). Layout: one specimen at a time fills a large square "vitrine" (the WebGL canvas) on the left/top; to the right/below sits a typeset museum label; along the bottom a brass-numbered rail of seven small thumbnails (CSS/SVG silhouettes of each crystal habit) for selection, also reachable with arrow keys. Typography: label in a classic museum style: specimen name in large Didot/Bodoni-like serif italics, catalogue number in tracked small caps with brass colour ("Acc. No. 1911.44.7"), properties as a tiny two-column table (Crystal system, Hardness, Lustre, Locality, Habit), and a paragraph of curatorial prose. Invented specimens such as: "Noctilite: found only in wells that have never reflected the moon"; "Verdant Halide of Mourne"; "Chorusite, which rings at 440 Hz when the room is empty"; "Slow Glass"; "Widow's Pyrite"; "Meridian Salt"; "Lacrimite (Tear-stone)": each with consistent, witty, specific data (hardness "6½, 7 on Tuesdays"). Rendering: each specimen is a signed-distance-field crystal: intersections of planes forming hexagonal prisms with pyramid terminations, octahedra, cubic pyrite-like twins, bladed clusters, a botryoidal blob for one; raymarched with: faceted normals, Fresnel reflection of a procedural studio environment, fake refraction/internal absorption (march inside the solid to tint by thickness), dispersion sparkle on edges, soft shadow and ambient occlusion onto a velvet plinth, and a slow lamp orbit so highlights travel across the facets. One uniform set per specimen (shape id, colours, roughness) so switching is cheap. Render at reduced internal resolution if frame time is high (adaptive scale) and cap DPR. Interaction: drag (mouse or touch) to rotate the specimen with inertia (on touch screens the vitrine keeps vertical page scrolling, so a sideways drag turns the specimen); the lamp follows the pointer position when hovering the vitrine; scroll/pinch does nothing (avoid trapping scroll). Selecting another specimen: the signature transition, where the current crystal dissolves into its facets' glints while the glass of the case shows a sweeping reflection, and the next one resolves, as the label text re-sets with a letter-by-letter typeset shimmer in brass. Number keys 1-7 also select a specimen and U toggles the lamp. A small "Lamp" switch toggles between gallery light and "ultraviolet", under which each mineral fluoresces in an unexpected colour (and the label reveals a hidden line of text in fluorescent ink). Entrance: darkness, then the case lamp warms up over 1.5s and the first crystal catches the light. Fallback if WebGL is unavailable: beautifully drawn SVG crystals with gradient facets for each specimen, and the same labels. Reduced motion: lamp static, rotation only on drag, cross-fade transitions.

Open Koi Pond, Noon ↗
009

Koi Pond, Noon

A bright, full-bleed garden pond seen from directly above at midday. The water runs from pale jade shallows at the mossy corner stones through teal to a deep centre, with pebbles on the floor and a slow net of caustic light sliding over everything, fish included. Nine to thirteen koi, each a flexing spine with its own painted pattern (kohaku, showa, ogon, tancho), cruise in bursts and glides, paddle their pectoral fins and drag soft shadows across the floor that lengthen over deeper water. Lily pads drift on tethers, two lotus flowers breathe, maple leaves turn, water striders dart, and now and then a dragonfly and its shadow cross the frame. The page opens in white glare that settles like eyes adjusting to the sun while the fish swim in from the edges. There is nothing to achieve: tap for ripples, hold to feed, and watch the whole pond turn towards one spot.

↗ Open

Original prompt

An ambient, full-bleed top-down view of a garden koi pond at midday, on Canvas 2D. Nothing to achieve; a place to rest your eyes. Concept: procedural animals that feel alive: each koi is a chain of spine segments driven by a travelling sine wave, with body width profile, pectoral fins that paddle, a fanning tail, and a unique painted pattern. Mood: calm, bright, tender. Palette: pond teal #2C7A7B shading to deep #14323A in the centre, pale shallow jade #BFE3D0 at the stone edge, koi orange #F26B21, white #FFFFFF, plus sparing koi black #1B1B1B and yolk yellow #F2B33D; lily pads in two greens (#3E8E5A, #6DB37C) with one or two pink-white lotus flowers (#F7C6D0). Light page (bright water, not a dark pond). Scene: 9–13 koi (fewer on small screens) of varying sizes and named varieties (kohaku: white with orange patches; showa: black, red, white; ogon: solid gold-yellow; tancho: white with a single red crown spot), patterns generated per fish by seeded noise blobs clipped to the body shape. Fish wander using steering behaviours (wander, mild separation, avoid edges and lily pads), accelerate with a tail burst and glide. Each casts a soft offset shadow on the pond floor, which sells the depth; the floor shows faint pebbles and animated caustic light (a cheap layered-sine or Voronoi-like caustic pattern drawn at low resolution and scaled up). Surface: lily pads drift on slow tethers and bob when ripples pass; occasional water striders skate in short darts leaving tiny dimples; a few fallen maple leaves rotate slowly. Stone edging frames the pond irregularly at the corners with moss. Interaction: click or tap the water to make concentric ripples (expanding rings with refraction-like highlight/shadow pairs that distort nothing but look right) which startle nearby fish (they dart away, then return); press-and-hold or double-tap scatters a pinch of food pellets: the signature moment, where all the koi turn and converge in a swirling, jostling knot, mouths opening at the surface with little "bloop" rings, then disperse lazily once it is eaten. Moving the pointer slowly makes the nearest curious fish follow it for a while. A dragonfly occasionally crosses and its shadow passes over the pond. The mouse cursor is replaced by a small drawn ring that fills as an arc while holding. Keyboard: with the pond focused, Enter makes a ripple and Space scatters food. Typography: almost no text. A tiny vertical caption in the lower corner in refined small type (Georgia italic, 11–12px, colour #14323A at 70%): "Koi pond, noon." and a discreet designed hint with two tiny drawn glyphs, "tap the water · hold to feed", each half of which fades once that gesture has been used. A small unobtrusive readout names the fish nearest the cursor ("Kohaku, about eleven years old") in the same quiet style. Optional sound toggle (off by default): soft water trickle and plips synthesised with filtered noise. Entrance: the water surface fades up from white glare like eyes adjusting to sunlight, and fish swim in from the edges. Performance: cap fish at 14, caustics at low-res offscreen canvas, DPR capped at 2. Reduced motion: fish glide at a third of the speed, no caustic animation, ripples still appear gently.

Open Six Rooms of Optical Pressure ↗
010

Six Rooms of Optical Pressure

A white-cube gallery reduced to two values. The visitor arrives at a title wall where enormous condensed capitals are sliced by horizontal hairlines that beat against each other, then walks down through six snap-aligned rooms that alternate between black and white. Each room holds one square work made from nothing but a repeated line, ring, check or spoke laid over a copy of itself: rings that produce sweeping hyperbolas, a checkerboard that swells into a dome, upright stripes that ripple, spokes that bloom into counter-rotating petals, ruled paper that folds into a pleated curtain, and finally a black disc that the viewer stares at until it disappears and leaves a glowing ghost on a blank wall. Signage is tiny, uppercase and widely spaced, with a large roman numeral and a dry wall note per room. The mood is exacting, quiet and slightly dizzying; everything that seems soft, grey or three-dimensional is produced by the viewer's own vision.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A CSS-only (no JavaScript at all) gallery of six kinetic op-art works in strict black and white, in the lineage of 1960s perceptual abstraction (bulging grids, moire, vibrating stripes), but new compositions rather than copies of any existing painting. Concept: the eye does the animating; CSS only nudges it. Palette: pure black #000000 and pure white #FFFFFF only. No greys except those the eye invents, no colour, no shadows, no opacity fades, no gradients other than hard-stop repeating gradients used as line generators. The title wall is white; the rooms then alternate: black rooms (I, III, V) holding predominantly white works, white rooms (II, IV, VI) holding black works. Layout: a vertical sequence of a title wall followed by six full-viewport "rooms", each holding one large square work centred with generous margin (min(86vw, 86vh)), with a museum caption beneath in tiny type. Each room also carries its roman numeral set large in condensed caps (in the left margin on wide screens, above the work on narrow ones) and, on wide screens only, a short wall note at lower left under a hairline rule. Use CSS scroll-snap so each room lands cleanly. A fixed, minimal index on the right edge (a short dash for the title wall, then six small squares, all anchor links; the room name wipes out to the left on hover or focus) drawn in difference blend so it reads on both black and white rooms. The current room's square is filled using scroll-driven view timelines where supported, falling back to :target. The six works (each built from repeating-linear/radial/conic gradients, masks, blend modes like mix-blend-mode: difference, transforms and keyframes): I. "Slow Interference": two identical layers of fine concentric rings (repeating-radial-gradient, 4px pitch, 3px on phones), the second in difference mode. Their centres drift around each other very slowly on sine-eased translations (one wanders near the middle, the other travels a wide ellipse), producing sweeping moire hyperbolas and ellipses. Pressing adds a slow sway and swell to the second field. II. "Bulge": a checkerboard that appears to swell into a dome: twenty-six concentric circular lenses of the same checker pattern, geometrically spaced in radius and each scaled a little more than the last along a logarithmic profile so the seams stay small, with a breathing animation. Pressing rolls the whole board slowly. III. "Tremor": perfectly vertical stripes shown in five phase-shifted copies, each masked to diagonal bands of sinusoidally varying width so the stripes read as rippling waves; the masks slide with steps() timing (four steps a second) so the surface shimmers rather than scrolls. Pressing makes the whole field lean from side to side. IV. "Current": a circular work. Sixty conic-gradient spokes turn one way; a difference-mode layer turns the other way with seventy-two spokes in the outer annulus and fifty-four in the inner disc, so twelve interference petals sweep round the outside while six sweep the opposite way inside, around a black hub. Pressing quickens the counter-rotation. V. "Fold": a sheet of parallel horizontal lines cut into twelve tall slices, each slice skewed alternately so the plane reads as a pleated curtain with a zigzag hem; alternate slices use a heavier or lighter line so the pleats take light and shade; pleat depth animates like breathing. Pressing lets the lines run down the folds. VI. "Afterimage": a large black disc on white with a tiny fixation cross, a ring of twenty tick marks and the instruction to stare for twenty seconds. Tapping the disc (or the Ready / Staring switch in the caption) starts a 20s pure-CSS progress ring that drains around the disc while a two-digit countdown in the caption steps from 20 to 00; then the disc vanishes and the room flips to blank white with only a tiny black cross left to hold the gaze, so the viewer sees the glowing afterimage. The instruction line changes at each stage. Restartable by flipping the same checkbox/label control back. Interaction without JS: each work is itself a label for a hidden checkbox, so hovering it, or tapping/clicking it, or toggling it from the keyboard "presses" on the work and switches on an extra layer of motion (animation-play-state on a paused booster animation, so nothing jumps); the caption's second line states the hint and the current pressed state. Each caption for rooms I to V has a "Moving / Still" switch built from a styled checkbox so viewers can freeze a piece mid-motion. Custom ring cursor over the works. Signature moment: Room I's moire: two trivially simple ring layers yielding huge, liquid, three-dimensional-seeming sweeps. Typography: tiny refined grotesk (Helvetica/Arial) at 11px, uppercase, wide letterspacing, in the manner of museum signage: "I — SLOW INTERFERENCE, synthetic polymer on screen, dimensions variable". An opening title wall before Room I: a hairline-ruled signage strip at the top, then the title "SIX ROOMS OF OPTICAL PRESSURE" set enormous in black condensed caps (Impact/"Arial Narrow" stack) filling the width (two justified lines on landscape screens, three on portrait), the letters knocked out by a slowly drifting line pattern (background-clip: text): two layers of horizontal hairlines at 10px and 11px pitch blended in difference and drifting in opposite directions, so bands of solid black and bands of split lines roll through the letters. Below it a short curatorial paragraph in small type, a boxed advisory with a flickering stripe glyph: "These works flicker. A calm version is shown if your device asks for reduced motion.", a ruled floor plan listing the six rooms as links, and an "Enter Room I" link with a drawn arrow. Entrance: the title appears solid black and the line pattern sweeps across it once from left to right, the top rule draws itself, and the lower blocks wipe in with a stagger. Under prefers-reduced-motion all animations stop and each work rests in its most striking static frame, the Moving / Still switches are replaced by "Held at rest", and only Room VI keeps its twenty-second clock (advancing in whole-second steps) because the afterimage depends on it. Must stay crisp on high-DPI and not overflow on a 360px phone.

Open The Hours in Words ↗
011

The Hours in Words

A clock that is only a sentence. The viewer's local time is written out in lowercase English and set as a wood-type broadside: every line is measured to its ink and scaled to the full width of the screen, the minute phrase in heavy italic, the whole block packed into a solid rectangle on a single flat field of colour that drifts through twenty-four hand-picked pairs across the day, from aubergine small hours through dawn peach, white-hot noon and terracotta evening back to deep blue. When the minute turns the poster re-typesets itself in place: only the letters that differ roll out and in, and the lines swell or shrink to stay flush with both margins. In the corners, tracked monospace capitals carry the date, year, time zone and day of the year, all in words, while a hairline along the bottom edge counts the seconds. The effect is of a printed sheet that happens to be alive, calm at rest and briefly mechanical once a minute.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A typographic clock with no numerals and no dial: the page states the viewer's actual local time as one enormous sentence that fills the whole viewport, e.g. "It is / twenty / past four / in the / afternoon." Concept: time as language, set like a poster that re-typesets itself every minute. Rounded to the minute in natural English ("just gone half past nine", "nearly noon", "a quarter to midnight", "one minute to six in the morning"); write a careful phrase generator covering every minute, with o'clock, past/to forms, noon and midnight special cases, and day-part suffixes (in the small hours / in the morning / in the afternoon / in the evening / at night). Palette: one flat colour field per hour, 24 hand-picked pairs of background and ink that follow the day (examples: 05h dawn peach #F6C6A8 with ink #3B1F2B; 09h morning yellow #F2C14E with ink #1A1A1A; 13h white-hot #FDF8EC with #E4391B; 17h terracotta #D9663A with #FFF3DC; 21h deep blue #1D3557 with #F1E3C6; 02h near-black aubergine #1A1220 with #C9B8E8). The field blends continuously between hour colours using the minutes, so the page is never the same colour twice in a day. No gradients on screen: flat colour only, plus a very fine paper grain. Typography: the hero. A heavy Didone/serif display stack (Didot, "Bodoni MT", "Playfair Display" if present, Georgia and Century Schoolbook fallbacks) set in tight leading, lowercase, with the key word (the minute phrase) in huge italic; lines are fitted to the viewport width by measuring and scaling each line individually (justified block, each line its own font-size, like a wood-type broadside), so the sentence always forms a solid rectangle of type edge to edge at any screen size. Line breaks are chosen by trying every possible breaking of the sentence and keeping the one whose fitted lines best fill the stage (a slight vertical condensing or extending of the whole block, like condensed and extended cuts of wood type, closes the remainder), with a preference for keeping lines that have not changed. Tiny counterpoint details in tracked monospace caps at the corners: the date written out ("Monday the ninth of November"), the date written out with the year in words, the seconds as a thin progress hairline along the bottom edge that fills over 60 seconds, the time zone name with its offset from Greenwich in words, and the day number of the year, also in words ("Day three hundred and thirteen of the year / fifty-two remain"). No numerals appear anywhere except in the hover translations. Signature moment: the minute change. When the minute turns, the lines that changed do not swap: each letter that differs rolls out upward and the new one rolls in from below with a staggered spring (30ms per letter, cubic-bezier(.2,.9,.2,1.1)), unchanged letters hold perfectly still, and lines re-fit their size with an eased scale so the block re-justifies itself like liquid. On load the sentence assembles the same way from blank. On the hour the field flashes to the ink colour and back in a slow inversion and a line of small text notes the hour ("That was four in the afternoon. It will not happen again today."). Interaction: real time by default. Dragging horizontally anywhere (with a little inertia on release), the mouse wheel, or the arrow keys (left/right one minute, shift for ten, up/down one hour) scrub time forward/back to preview other moments, with the colour field following, and a small designed "now" marker that snaps back when clicked; a subtle toggle switches between plain and "elaborate" phrasing ("it is nine twenty-two" vs "twenty-two minutes past nine in the evening"); pressing F or a corner control goes fullscreen. Hovering a word tilts it slightly and shows its numeric translation in tiny mono ("twenty past = :20"). Reduced motion: letters cross-fade instead of rolling. Must look perfect and fill the screen on a 360px phone in portrait (more, shorter lines) and on ultrawide monitors.

Open Murmuration at Dusk ↗
012

Murmuration at Dusk

A wide marsh sky in the last minutes of light: plum-ink overhead sinking through mauve and coral to a pale apricot horizon, a low sun and its broken reflection in a thin strip of water, pylons and poplars tiny on the far bank, and a black reed bed swaying along the bottom edge. Into the empty sky a long ribbon of starlings streams from the right and gathers into a single breathing shape made of thousands of tiny flapping chevrons, lighter where the birds are far and darker where the flock folds over itself, stretching into ribbons and hourglasses and rippling with sudden dark waves when it turns. The viewer's pointer is a peregrine; the flock parts around it, wraps behind it and closes again, and a click sends the falcon into a tucked dive that tears a hole straight through the birds. Left alone, the falcon hunts by itself, the light slowly fails, and the whole flock drains through a narrow neck into the reeds; after a few seconds of stillness a new evening begins. The only words are a tiny italic title, a live count of starlings, a hint that dissolves once the hunt starts, and two quiet controls.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A full-bleed simulation of a starling murmuration over a reed marsh at dusk, on Canvas 2D. Concept: thousands of simple birds becoming one dark, folding fabric in the sky; the viewer is the falcon. Mood: awe, hush, the last ten minutes of light. Palette: sky as a tall vertical gradient from pale apricot #F6C9A8 at the horizon through coral #E58F7B and mauve #6B4E71 to deep plum-ink #2A2438 at the top; birds, reeds and far treeline in near-black #0D0B12; a low sun disc and its reflection in a thin strip of marsh water in a paler apricot. This dusk gradient is the concept, not a default; keep it painterly with faint horizontal washes, a faint grain and a few long thin horizontal clouds that drift very slowly. Simulation: 2,500–3,600 boids on desktop (scale down automatically on small or slow devices, never below 1,200), using a uniform spatial grid for neighbour lookup, classic separation / alignment / cohesion with topological-ish neighbour limits (nearest ~7 sampled from the surrounding cells), plus a wider neighbourhood term taken from per-cell sums (shared heading, a pull to the local centre of mass, and a soft pressure that pushes birds down the density gradient when a region gets too crowded) so the flock holds together as one fabric without collapsing into a ball. A soft roosting attractor orbits slowly above the reed bed so the flock stays on screen; it periodically splits into two lobes along a rotating axis so the flock stretches into ribbons and hourglasses and then rejoins. Add a little coherent noise turbulence. Add fake depth: each bird has a z value (part its own, part a slow sheet that drifts through the flock) that scales its size (roughly 1–3px strokes) and darkness, and a soft density haze under the flock, so it reads as a volume whose density makes darker folds where it overlaps itself. Draw birds as tiny oriented chevrons (two short strokes that flap by alternating length), not dots. "Waves of agitation": when birds turn sharply they briefly thicken and lengthen their strokes, and an excitable wave passes from bird to neighbour with a short delay and a refractory period, so alarms ripple visibly through the flock; now and then something unseen spooks one edge of it. Interaction: the pointer/finger is a peregrine: a small falcon silhouette glides toward the pointer position with inertia (a faint reticle marks the pointer itself, and the falcon circles it rather than stopping); birds within a radius flee and dodge sideways off its line of flight, so the flock splits, wraps and re-forms around it, the signature moment. A click/tap (or the space bar) makes the falcon stoop: wings tucked, a fast dive toward the pointer, or along its heading if the pointer is already beneath it, which punches a hole through the flock and leaves a thin streak and a faint ring. If the pointer is idle the falcon loiters high and makes occasional autonomous passes, some with a stoop, so the page is alive without input. Over about three minutes the light fades one step (sky darkens ~15%), then the flock gathers above the roost, funnels down through a narrow neck and pours into the reeds like liquid (the reeds shiver where birds land), the screen rests for a few seconds, and a new evening begins; a small control lets the viewer "call them down" or "send them up" at will, the latter erupting the whole flock out of the reed bed. Foreground: a reed bed silhouette along the bottom in two layers with individually swaying stems, drooping plumes, bulrush heads and leaves (sine sway with phase by x), a leaning fence post with sagging wire, two distant pylons with their wires and a clump of poplars on the far treeline for scale, and glints flickering in the sun's reflection. Typography: almost none. Bottom-left in tiny letterspaced Georgia italic, cream at 80%: "Murmuration at dusk" and a live, quietly updating count line "3,200 starlings · one falcon" (it counts birds in view, and reads "3,200 starlings in the reeds · one falcon, hungry" while the flock roosts); a designed hint with a small falcon glyph, "move to hunt · click to stoop" ("drag to hunt · tap to stoop" on touch), that fades after first interaction. Sound toggle bottom-right (off by default): a soft rush of wings via band-passed noise with a fast flutter, whose volume and filter follow the flock's average speed, its presence on screen, its proximity to the falcon and how many birds are fleeing. Entrance: the empty sky, then the flock streams in from the right as a long ribbon and balls up. Performance: typed arrays, batched paths per depth band, the static sky, the drifting clouds, the birds and the reeds on separate stacked layers, DPR capped at 2, pause when hidden, flock thinned automatically if frames run long. Reduced motion: 1,200 birds, everything at well under half speed, no stoop streak or ring and no autonomous attacks; a still composition with a gently drifting flock.

Open Iron Filings ↗
013

Iron Filings

A top-down view of a classroom demonstration: a sheet of warm white paper, fibres catching soft daylight, dusted with fourteen thousand black iron slivers. Two red-and-blue bar magnets glide in from opposite edges and the random dust visibly combs itself into the textbook pattern, pulling a dense bridge of filings across the gap between north and south. From there it is a toy: slide, spin and flip the magnets and watch the alignment wave chase them, see tufts of filings cling to the poles and get dragged along leaving swept trails, flick the paper to tidy the lines, bring like poles together to find the pencil-circled null point, and drop a horseshoe or disc magnet, a compass, or a fresh shake of filings from the apparatus tray. A taped typewritten label and a pencilled note are the only words on the sheet.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A desk toy: a sheet of white paper dusted with iron filings, with magnets you slide around underneath. Full-bleed Canvas 2D (a static paper layer plus a live layer for magnets, filings and instruments). Concept: make an invisible field visible; the delight is how thousands of tiny slivers swing into alignment as a magnet passes. Mood: clinical-playful, a physics classroom demonstration photographed from above in soft daylight. Palette: paper #ECEBE6 with faint fibre texture, paper tooth and a soft vignette, filings near-black #1A1A1A (with slight grey variation), magnets painted classic red #D62828 (north) and blue #1D4E89 (south) with a white "N"/"S" stamped on them, soft grey drop shadows, brushed-steel greys for the compass and shaker, pencil blue #3A68AA for diagram lines. Light page. Printed worksheet details: corner registration marks and a small "0 - 5 CM" scale bar. Simulation: up to 14,000 filings on desktop (scaled to area; about 4,800 on phones), each a short line segment roughly 4-9px with a position and an angle, held in typed arrays. The field at any point is the sum of softened monopole fields from each magnet's poles (two per bar and horseshoe; the disc is a single upward-facing pole giving a radial pattern). Every frame each filing eases its angle toward the local field direction with its own lag, scaled by field strength, so alignment spreads outward as a visible wave; about a third of the filings also creep along the gradient of field strength toward the poles when the pull is strong (clumping into spiky tufts at the poles and leaving emptier halos, and being dragged along by a moving magnet), with friction so they settle. Filing darkness, weight and length are modulated by field strength (dithered so there are no visible bands) so lines of force stand out as denser streaks. A "tap the paper" action (tap any empty paper, the red TAP PAPER button, or T) sends a ring outward from the tap point that jitters every filing it passes and briefly lowers friction, so they re-settle into cleaner lines, exactly as you would flick the sheet. Objects: the entrance demonstrates the signature moment: on load two bar magnets glide in from opposite sides (left/right on wide screens, top/bottom on tall ones) with opposite poles facing, and the filings, initially random, snap into the classic pattern with a taut bridge of lines across the gap within the first two seconds; a compass then pops in. A moulded tray along the bottom edge (side on wide screens) holds the rest: the slot for the second bar magnet (press to return it or take it out again), a horseshoe magnet, a small disc magnet, and a "fresh shake" shaker (press to re-dust the whole sheet in a top-to-bottom sweep, or drag it over the paper to sprinkle filings where you want them). Magnets can be clicked or dragged out of the tray and dropped back on it. Drag magnets (pointer/touch) with a slight lag and a soft shadow lift; rotate with a small handle that appears beside the magnet, the mouse wheel, a two-finger twist, or Q/E; double-click/tap flips polarity with a little flip animation. Bringing like poles together shows the null point between them, which gets a hand-drawn pencil circle and the note "null point"; opposite poles pull a taut bridge of lines. Extras that reward attention: a small steel compass drawn on the canvas, with a red-white needle that swings with damped oscillation, can be dragged around and always points along the field; toggling "field lines" overlays thin pencil-blue streamlines traced from the poles, like a teacher's diagram, with tiny arrowheads, drawn in progressively; a label strip bottom-left reads out the filing count, pole count, and the field strength and bearing at the compass; an optional sound switch (off by default) adds a faint gritty hiss proportional to how much the filings are moving, a thump for taps and a click for flips. Typography: sparse, like a lab worksheet: top-left a slip of paper taped to the sheet carries a small label in a typewriter/monospace face (American Typewriter/Courier stack) reading "Demonstration 14 — Lines of Force" (typed on at load), with a handwritten-style pencil note beneath in italic ("drag the magnets. tap the paper to settle the filings." plus one line about turning and flipping suited to mouse or touch); tool labels in 10px tracked caps. Performance: typed arrays, batch line drawing in six paths bucketed by shade, DPR capped at 2, the loop sleeps when everything has settled and pauses when the tab is hidden. Reduced motion: filings re-orient instantly without creeping or jitter animation, and magnets start in place. Keyboard: Tab to a magnet or the compass, arrows to move, R to rotate, F to flip, Delete to return it to the tray; tray tools and switches are ordinary buttons.

Open Where the Primes Fall ↗
014

Where the Primes Fall

A white sheet set like a Swiss mathematics poster: an enormous lowercase grotesk headline runs up the left edge with one word in red-orange, a hairline-framed square field fills the height of the window, and a narrow monospace rail of rules, sliders and tabular figures sits to its right. The field holds every integer up to N as one small square. It opens by drawing all of them as a single black line, reeling that line into a solid black square spiral, and then sieving it from the centre outward behind a red front until only the primes remain, scattered along unmistakable diagonals. Choosing another arrangement sends every dot along its own path, centre first, so the square coils into the Sacks spiral, flares into the polar galaxy, or falls into rows whose columns stripe at widths like 30 and 210. Pointing at any dot produces a black label with its arithmetic and lights the diagonal, curve, column or arm it belongs to in red; zooming in far enough turns the dots into numbered tiles.

↗ Open

Original prompt

An interactive data visualization of prime numbers, all computed in the page with a sieve: the integers 1…N laid out in several arrangements that reveal hidden structure, with animated morphs between them. Concept: the same dots, re-arranged, suddenly line up into diagonals and curves; the wow is the morph. Mood: austere Swiss-poster mathematics; exact, black and white with one hot accent. Palette: paper white #FAFAF7, ink #111111, single accent red-orange #FF3B00 (used for highlighted primes, the cursor ring, twin-prime links and one headline word), light rule grey #D8D8D2. Light page. Layout: a large square canvas (the field) with an asymmetric Swiss grid around it: a narrow left column carrying an enormous rotated, stacked headline in a tight grotesk (Helvetica/Arial bold, lowercase: "where the primes fall", with "primes" in the accent), and a slim control rail and readout in small monospace. On phones the headline lies flat above a full-width canvas with the controls in a compact strip beneath. Strict baseline rhythm, thin rules, numerals in tabular mono. Arrangements (all for the same N, default 40,000 on desktop, 16,000 on phones, adjustable from 1,000 up to 250,000 with a custom slider): 1) "Line": numbers wrapped in rows of a chosen width like text (an adjustable wrap width, with presets 6, 30, 210 and √N, shows stripes appearing at multiples of 6, 30 …). 2) "Ulam": the square spiral, where primes cluster on diagonals. 3) "Sacks": the Archimedean spiral with squares on the east axis (r = √n, θ = 2π√n), where primes form sweeping curves. 4) "Polar": the point (n, n) in polar coordinates, i.e. radius n and angle n radians, which yields galaxy-like arms at different zoom levels. Each number is a small square: primes in ink, composites invisible by default or faint grey by toggle. Zoomed in far enough, every square carries its number. Signature moment: switching arrangement animates every point along its own eased path (staggered by n so the morph unspools from the centre outward, about 1.8s, cubic-bezier(.65,0,.2,1)); between the two spirals the points travel in polar coordinates, so the square spiral visibly coils into the Sacks spiral and the diagonals bend into curves. On load, the page plays the opening itself: a single ink line of all the numbers draws across the field, wraps into a solid black Ulam square, and a red front then sieves outward from the centre, dropping the composites and leaving only the primes in ink. Interaction: hover/touch a point to read it: a crisp tooltip in mono gives n, whether prime (and its rank, and its twin if it has one), its factorisation if composite, its gap to the next prime, and if it sits on a famous prime-rich polynomial (Euler's k² + k + 41 and k² + k + 17, Legendre's 2k² + 29); the accent highlights the structure through that point: in Ulam the richer of its two diagonals with the quadratic 4t² + bt + c it follows, in Sacks the curve k² + k + c, in Line its column (a residue class mod w), in Polar its arm or ray (mod 44 or mod 710), each with a count of how many of its numbers are prime. Scroll-wheel/pinch zooms and drag pans the canvas (contained, so the page itself does not get trapped: zoom only when the pointer is over the canvas, no modifier needed, and at full view wheeling outward scrolls the page on; + / − / fit buttons too). Arrow keys on the focused canvas step through the numbers and primes; keys 1–4 switch arrangement. Toggles: twin primes linked in red; colour by last digit; Euler's k² + k + 41 polynomial stitched as a red thread through the spiral; composites in grey. A live readout shows the count of primes ≤ N and N / ln N beside it, with the ratio, the number of twin pairs and the widest gap, plus a small bar chart of gaps between neighbouring primes, all updating as N changes. Copy: three short, precise paragraphs in small type explaining what one is seeing (Ulam doodled the spiral during a dull talk in 1963; diagonals correspond to quadratic polynomials; nobody has proved why some are so rich), written plainly. Performance: draw with ImageData, typed arrays, DPR capped at 2. Reduced motion: no opening sequence, and arrangements switch with a short cross-fade.

015

Maison Ombre — Parfums d'Intérieur

A slow, bilingual landing page for the invented Parisian house Maison Ombre and its single fragrance, No. 7 — Bibliothèque Brûlée. On warm ivory laid paper, crossed by a drifting casement of pale window-light, an enormous oxblood italic title runs behind one exquisitely drawn flask; the word seen through the glass is bent by it, and the amber-oxblood liquid inside tilts, sloshes, ripples and bubbles as the pointer passes or the flask is tipped. Scrolling carries the same flask into the olfactory pyramid, where its oak cap lifts off and the notes rise from the neck as fine wisps and then evaporate in order, top first, heart next, base lingering, before three quieter movements: a portrait of the perfumer beside a scorched, tea-ringed folio, the object's specifications with a dimensioned elevation and a discreet table of editions, and a one-line request for a sample card that is answered by an envelope closed with a wax seal.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A landing page for an invented Parisian perfume house, "Maison Ombre", presenting a single fragrance, "No. 7 — Bibliothèque Brûlée" (the smell of a library the morning after a small fire: smoked paper, cold tea, iris, wet stone). Concept: luxury through restraint and one exquisite object: a perfume bottle drawn entirely in SVG whose liquid behaves like liquid. Mood: luxurious, literary, slow. Palette: warm ivory #F5EEE4 page, oxblood #5B0F1B for display type and the liquid's shadow tone, antique gold #B8925A for hairlines, numerals and the bottle's collar and cap band, soft black #1E1A17 for text. Light page with enormous margins; a barely visible laid-paper texture (fine horizontal lines) and soft window-light: a slanted pale rectangle of light that drifts very slowly across the page as if from a casement. Typography: high-contrast Didone display (Didot, "Bodoni MT", Georgia fallback) used very large and very small: the name "Bibliothèque Brûlée" in huge oxblood italic that runs partly behind the bottle; everything else in tiny, wide-tracked small caps (10–11px, 0.3em) and a quiet serif text at narrow measure. French–English bilingual touches. Numerals in old style where available. Thin gold rules, a monogram "MO" drawn in SVG. Structure (vertical scroll, sparse; five movements separated by generous white): 1) Opening: the bottle centred low, the name, and one line: "A fragrance for rooms that have read too much." 2) The pyramid: top / heart / base notes as a vertical triptych: Tête: bergamot peel, cold Lapsang, struck match; Cœur: iris root, vellum, ink; Fond: smoked cedar, wet limestone, a trace of beeswax. 3) "The nose": a short portrait of the invented perfumer Séverine Aubrac, who "worked for eleven years as a paper conservator". 4) The object: bottle specifications (hand-blown in Meisenthal-like invented village "Val-d'Orne", 50 ml, cap turned from fire-blackened oak), editions and price in a discreet table (Extrait 50 ml · €240 · edition of 900). 5) A request form reduced to one line ("Request a sample card") with a custom-styled input and a wax-seal confirmation animation (no network). Signature moment: the bottle. A faceted rectangular flask with thick glass walls drawn with layered SVG gradients, inner reflections, a gold collar and a charred-oak cap; the part of the title that passes behind the flask is seen through the glass slightly magnified and displaced, as if refracted; dragging the flask sideways (or the arrow keys) tilts it while the liquid stays level with the world; the amber-oxblood liquid inside has a surface line that tilts and sloshes with pointer movement or device tilt-like drag (spring-damped angle and a travelling wave on the surface path), with tiny bubbles rising after a slosh; caustic light from the liquid falls on the page beneath as a soft coloured shadow that shifts with the window-light. When the notes section scrolls into view, the flask glides aside (shrinks to the top on phones), its cap lifts away, and the notes "evaporate" in olfactory order: top notes rise from the bottle's neck as fine drifting SVG wisps and their words fade first, then heart, then base lingers longest, driven by scroll progress with eased interpolation, with a small clock counting the hours since spraying. Hovering, focusing or tapping a note name breathes it back to full strength, releases a small wisp of its own and shows a one-line sensory description ("iris root: cold, powdery, like the inside of a cello"). Motion: slow and exact: 900–1400ms transitions with cubic-bezier(.16,1,.3,1); the title's letters settle in with a staggered blur-free tracking animation (letterspacing closing from wide to set). The cursor on desktop is a small gold ring that swells over interactive elements. Reduced motion: liquid static with a gentle shimmer disabled, notes simply visible. Voice: elegant, precise, lightly ironic; no superlatives.
