Open Descent — A Vector Lander ↗
016

Descent — A Vector Lander

A single cathode-ray tube sits in a dark cabinet above an engraved model plate. Everything on its face is light: mint-white beam cores wrapped in green bloom, vertices that burn a little brighter where the beam pauses, and a phosphor that takes a moment to forget what it was shown. The attract screen traces the word DESCENT one stroke at a time while a cratered wireframe moon turns behind it and an autopilot quietly demonstrates a landing below. Starting a mission hands over a small legged module drifting above jagged regolith; the camera eases in to two and a half times scale as the surface approaches, the engine plume fans out and flickers, dust streaks away along the ground in short glowing lines, and the whole tube brightens with the burn. A clean touchdown earns a dry remark from mission control and a harder site; anything else scatters the lander into its component segments, which tumble, bounce and fade while the phosphor remembers their arcs.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A playable lunar-landing game drawn as if on a 1970s vector CRT (an X-Y monitor, not a raster screen): everything is glowing line segments, with phosphor persistence and beam bloom. An original game in the lander genre with its own terrain, ship and mission texts. Concept: the display technology is the aesthetic; the lines must feel like light. Palette: tube black #020403, phosphor mint-white #B8FFD9 for the hot beam core, green glow #5CFFB0 for bloom and trails; nothing else except a faint dark-green reflection of the room on the curved glass and near-black green tints for the cabinet. Dark page; the "screen" is a rounded-rectangle tube with a subtle barrel vignette, a thin bezel and a small engraved model plate ("VECTRON 21 · X-Y DISPLAY") with two screws and a power lamp under it. Rendering: Canvas 2D with additive blending ('lighter'): each line drawn several times (wide dim glow, medium, thin bright core); brighter dots at vertices where the beam dwells; instead of clearing each frame, a persistence layer is faded with a low-alpha black fill so moving things leave short decaying trails, and a blurred quarter-resolution copy of the picture is screened over it as halation; slight per-frame beam jitter (sub-pixel), occasional flicker, and a gentle barrel warp applied to every endpoint. All text on the screen is drawn with a custom vector stroke font defined in the page (line-segment glyphs for A–Z, 0–9, punctuation and arrows), never with fillText. Game: the lander (a small original module shape with cabin, window, splayed legs, antenna dish and a descent engine bell) starts high with horizontal drift over a procedurally generated jagged lunar terrain that repeats endlessly sideways, with a dim parallax ridge behind it and several flat pads of different widths marked with multipliers (×2, ×3, ×5). Physics: lunar-ish gravity, rotation left/right, main thrust with limited fuel, velocity in two axes; land upright, slowly, on a pad to score (multiplier × remaining fuel bonus). Crash: the ship breaks into its line segments which tumble, bounce and fade with physics, with a spray of sparks and a shock ring. As the ship nears the ground the camera zooms in smoothly (scale 1 → 2.5) for the final approach. Each successful landing starts a harder site: thinner pads, crosswind, less fuel. A mission has three landers; a crash costs one and retries the same site. HUD in vector type: ALTITUDE, HORIZONTAL and VERTICAL SPEED with little arrows, FUEL as a segmented bar, SCORE, SITE number, remaining landers, crosswind, and a blinking DESCENT RATE warning. Messages in deadpan mission-control voice, typed out character by character: "CONTACT LIGHT. ENGINE STOP. THE EAGLE-EYED AMONG YOU MAY EXHALE.", "YOU HAVE CREATED A NEW CRATER 2 KM WIDE." Signature moment: thrust. The engine plume is a flickering fan of vector lines whose length follows throttle; it kicks up line-particle dust from the surface when close, and all of it smears with phosphor decay, lighting the tube. Also an attract mode: before start, the screen shows the title "DESCENT" drawing itself stroke by stroke as a beam trace, a slowly rotating wireframe moon with craters, and an autopilot demo landing in the background. Controls: keyboard (left/right or A/D rotate, up/W/space thrust, Enter start, R restart, P pause, M sound, Esc back to the title); touch: three large vector-outlined buttons along the bottom (rotate left, thrust, rotate right) that light up and hatch when pressed and support multi-touch; a vector-outlined start control and a pause control. Optional sound (off by default, vector-drawn speaker toggle): thrust rumble from filtered noise, low beeps for altitude warning and low fuel, a crunch for crashes. Reduced motion: no jitter or flicker, shorter trails, title shown without the trace animation. Responsive: the tube fits the viewport with correct aspect on phones in portrait (taller, narrower playfield).

Open Spore Print — A Two-Colour Zine ↗
017

Spore Print — A Two-Colour Zine

A six-spread stapled zine laid out on a cutting mat, printed in exactly two inks. Everything on the page, from the full-width cover title to the smallest weather icon, exists twice: once on a fluorescent pink plate and once on a teal plate, multiplied together over flecked recycled paper so that overlaps turn a deep violet nobody paid for. The plates never quite agree. As the viewer moves, scrolls or tilts, the pink plate slides a few pixels against the teal one with a soft spring, keylines drift off their fills, halftone screens beat against each other, and a printer's registration target in the corner reports the error and can be dragged to make it much worse or double-clicked to make it, briefly, perfect. Inside that process sits a warm, funny little publication by two damp people and a cat: instructions for taking a spore print (with a cap you can lift to watch one land), five mushrooms described like neighbours, a centrefold of roots and mycelium growing under the fold, letters, a month of rain, and a stamp telling you to pass it on.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A risograph-printed zine about looking for mushrooms, reproduced as a web page that behaves like cheap, beautiful two-colour printing. Invented publication: "SPORE PRINT, issue 3: The Wet Month", from an invented small press, "Damp Hands Press". Concept: the print process is the star: two spot inks, visible grain, slightly misregistered plates, overprint making a third colour. Palette (strict two inks on paper): paper warm off-white #F4EFE2 with recycled-stock flecks and fibres; fluorescent pink #FF48B0; teal #00838A; where they overlap, multiply blending yields a deep violet-indigo ink (in the region of #3B2F5C, darker where both inks are solid). No black anywhere: all text is teal or pink, shadows are violet-tinted. Light page; the spreads lie on a pale cutting-mat grid. Print simulation: every illustration is built as two separate SVG layers (a pink plate and a teal plate) with mix-blend-mode: multiply, each plate offset by a small registration error (1–3px, different per spread) and textured with an SVG turbulence noise mask so ink coverage is grainy and uneven; large flats use halftone dot or line screens (SVG patterns) at different angles for each ink; line work is pushed through a displacement filter so it wobbles like a drawn line. The signature moment: registration drifts with the pointer: moving the mouse (or dragging/tilting on touch, or scrolling) slides the pink plate against the teal plate by a few pixels across the whole page, with a springy settle, so overprints open and close and the viewer feels the two plates; a small "registration" crosshair mark on a paper tab in the corner (the printer's target) shows the current offset with a numeric x/y readout and can be dragged (or nudged with arrow keys) to misregister on purpose by up to 12px; double-click (or Enter) snaps everything, including each spread's own error, back into perfect register until the pointer moves away again. Typography: zine-like and confident: huge condensed sans headlines (Impact/"Arial Narrow" stack) in pink overprinted with teal keylines; the cover title is set as SVG text stretched to the full width of the spread, with teal line and dot screens clipped inside the letters; body in a typewriter face (Courier/"American Typewriter"); hand-drawn SVG arrows, underlines, asterisks and marginal notes; page numbers in scribbled circles; crooked paste-up blocks rotated 1–2° with torn tape; crop marks, a colour bar and a plate slug on every spread. Content: a vertical sequence of 6 "spreads" each sized like a folded A4 page with a visible centre fold and two staples drawn at the spine (on phones the pages stack and the fold turns horizontal): 1) Cover: giant "SPORE PRINT" with a big overprinted fly-agaric-like cap illustration crossing the fold, "issue 3 · the wet month · free or trade", a contents list, "No.3" and a starburst "free or trade" sticker. 2) "How to take a spore print": four steps with drawings: cut the stem, cap gills-down on paper, cover with a glass, wait overnight; with an interactive: press the cap (seen from above on a sheet of paper), a moon crosses for the night, the cap lifts aside and a spore print slowly blooms where it sat as radial lines of dots in teal (animated, each time a different pattern: different gill count, density lobes and draught smear); press again for a fresh sheet. 3) "Five you will meet": profile cards of five mushrooms described by general appearance only (a brown bolete-type with a spongy underside, a shaggy white cap that melts into ink, a bracket shelf on birch, a tiny orange cup on moss, a ring of pale caps on a lawn), with friendly drawings and field notes in the writer's voice; no edibility advice except the firm repeated house rule: "we don't eat anything from this zine. this is a zine about looking." 4) A centrefold poster: a procedurally drawn forest-floor cross-section showing rain, trunks, small mushrooms, a pair of boots, roots, and mycelium threads linking tree roots, in overprinted line work, with the line "the forest is mostly underneath" and pasted labels. 5) Letters (three, with replies) and a hand-drawn weather calendar for the wet month: 31 squares with drawn weather, a pink cap on days something was found, a diary line for each day, and totals computed from the calendar. 6) Back: colophon ("printed in two colours on a machine named Gerald; 200 copies; stapled by hand"), a copy number, a "pass it on" rubber stamp that can be pressed to leave further faint impressions, a "this copy has passed through" list, and the press mark. Motion: spreads arrive with a slight paper slap (rotate from 3° to resting angle with a fast overshooting ease-out), not fades; mushrooms nod on hover or tap; mycelium lines grow by stroke-dashoffset when the centrefold enters view. Reduced motion: plates fixed at a tasteful static misregistration (the mark can still be dragged), no slap, no nodding, no growth animation, the spore print appears at once. Voice: warm, damp, funny, first person plural.

Open Plotter No. 2 — Landscapes in One Pen ↗
018

Plotter No. 2 — Landscapes in One Pen

A bright, technical bench-top scene: a white A4 sheet lies on a pale grey gridded cutting mat inside a drawn pen plotter with two aluminium rails, a travelling gantry bar, a black carriage with a cable loop and a ballpoint whose red clip is the only warm colour besides the hold button. On load the carriage homes into the corner with a small bump, rules the border and registration crosses, then works through the sky, each ridge from far to near, the lake or pines, and finally letters its own title block. The landscape is a true plot file: every mark on the sheet is an ordered polyline that the pen actually travels, with travel moves, pen lifts, corner slow-downs and ink that is paler when the pen runs fast. The pleasure is in watching tone accumulate as the machine hatches a mountainside in hundreds of quick strokes, while a small LCD reports pen state, coordinates, percentage and time remaining, and a listing on the mat ticks off the layers. Every new sheet is a new seeded composition, and the finished ones pile up in the out tray.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A generative-art page in which a pen plotter draws an invented mountain landscape live, in a single blue pen, on a white sheet. The viewer watches the machine work: the gantry travels, the pen lifts and drops, the line appears exactly where the nib is. Each sheet is a new composition. Concept: the pleasure of watching a drawing being made by a patient machine. Palette: sheet white #FFFFFF on a pale grey cutting-mat surround #D9D9D9 with faint grid, a single ballpoint-blue ink #1F3FBF, machine parts in near-black #111111 and aluminium greys, a small red (#D93025) accent only on the pen clip and the emergency-stop style control. Light page, bright and technical. The drawing (generated per seed, then converted to an ordered list of polylines, i.e. a plot file): a stack of 5–8 receding ridgelines made from layered noise, each ridge hatched with a different technique so the tones separate: contour-following lines that wobble with the slope, vertical rain-like hatching, cross-hatching in shadow, stippled dashes for scree; occlusion handled by hidden-line removal (lines do not draw where a nearer ridge covers them: keep a running "horizon" height per x). Above: a sun as concentric circles or a spiral, or a crescent moon with a few stars, sometimes with horizontal cloud bands that break around it or a small flock of birds; below: a lake of horizontal dashes with the mirrored ridge broken into ripples, or a foreground of a few pine trees drawn as zigzag strokes. The machine: a drawn plotter around the sheet: two rails at the sides, a crossbar (gantry) that moves in Y, a pen carriage that moves in X with a cable loop, a pen that visibly lifts (shadow separates, carriage shows pen-up state) for travel moves and drops for draw moves. Motion follows the path at a constant feed rate with acceleration/deceleration at corners; travel moves are faster. Lines are rendered with slight ink variation: a tiny blob at pen-down, lighter ink at high speed, minute wobble. Paths are sorted to minimise travel, like a real plot. Signature moment: watching the pen race along a ridge then hatch a mountainside in hundreds of quick parallel strokes, the image building up tone. Entrance: the carriage homes to the corner with a small bump, draws the border and registration marks in the first two seconds, then starts the sky. Controls (styled as the plotter's physical control panel under the sheet: a knurled speed dial, a chunky lever toggle, two black keycaps, a red emergency-stop style mushroom button, a small monospace LCD): feed rate dial 1×–64× in seven detents (default 4×); pause/resume on the latching red HOLD button; "finish" (skip to finished) and "new sheet" keycaps (the finished sheet slides off to the right into a stack in the out tray and a blank feeds in from the left); the lever toggle switches the sound; pen colour is fixed. On wide screens the mat beside the machine carries a silk-screened "plot file" listing: every layer with its stroke count, ticked off as the machine completes it, plus pen-down and pen-up distances in metres. The LCD shows live status in mono: "PEN DOWN X 212.4 Y 88.0 37% ETA 01:12". Clicking anywhere on the sheet while it draws drops a "bird": the plotter will later add a tiny V-shaped bird stroke there. Optional sound (off by default): stepper-motor tones whose pitch follows axis speeds, via two oscillators, with a soft click on pen up/down. Typography: monospace technical (Menlo/Consolas/Courier), tiny, tracked; one small heading "Plotter No. 2". Responsive: on phones the sheet is portrait and the gantry simplified. Clicking the sheet after the plot is done wakes the machine to add the bird. Keyboard: Space hold, F finish, N new sheet, + and − feed. Reduced motion: the drawing appears finished, with a pair of layer-step keys on the panel to step through the layers.

019

Rhythmus — A Drum Machine in Primary Colours

Rhythmus is a poster that happens to be an instrument. On warm, toothy paper a wall of hand-drawn geometric capitals climbs the left edge, and a heavy black bar cuts across the sheet at seventeen degrees with the tempo reversed out of it like a ruler. Six flat-colour shapes balance on and hang from that bar: a blue circle resting on the incline, a red square standing on its edge, a yellow triangle, a black half-disc facing its outlined twin, a thin red mallet on a black pivot, a blue ring. Below, a six-by-sixteen grid repeats the same shapes in miniature as the score. Press the red circle and the composition starts to move: every drum hit is performed by its shape with spring physics, the playhead sweeps the grid, the title letters step along with the bar, and the whole print keeps time. Everything is drawn in code, with slightly ragged printed edges and off-register fills, and the layout re-composes for phones with the stage above a two-page grid.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A sixteen-step drum machine designed as a 1920s Bauhaus-school composition: the instrument and the poster are the same object. Circles, squares, triangles and bars in primary colours are the voices; when a voice plays, its shape performs. Concept: geometry as percussion. A new composition, not a copy of any existing poster. Palette: warm paper #F1E9D8, red #D62B1F, yellow #F2B705, blue #1F4E9E, black #141414. Flat colour, light page, with subtle paper tooth and slightly imperfect "printed" edges (tiny SVG displacement on large shapes). Layout: an asymmetric composition on a tilted axis (17 degrees): the title "RHYTHMUS" runs vertically up the left edge in enormous black uppercase geometric lettering, drawn in SVG from heavy bars and arcs so it never depends on an installed font; a diagonal black bar crosses the page carrying the tempo (numerals reversed out of the bar, a ruler of ticks along its edge, and a small lowercase instruction set inside the bar itself); the upper right holds the "stage" where six large shapes live (one per voice), all standing on, hanging from or leaning toward the tilted bar; the lower area is the sequencer grid: six rows × sixteen steps, where each step is a small shape matching its voice (circle for kick, square for snare, triangle for hi-hat, half-circle for clap, thin bar for rim, ring for tom), drawn as outlines when off and filled (slightly off-register, like a second print pass) when on, with every fourth step marked by a thicker baseline. Small labels in lowercase tracked sans (Futura/"Century Gothic"/"Avenir" stack), in the school's all-lowercase manner: "schlag / kick", "wirbel / snare", "becken / hat", "klatsch / clap", "rand / rim", "trommel / tom". The upper left carries the play control, a short bilingual statement, the pattern slots, the swing knob, clear/random and a row of keyboard hints. Sound (Web Audio, all synthesised, off until the viewer presses the large play control, a red circle with a black triangle that becomes a black square while playing): kick (sine with fast pitch drop), snare (noise burst + tone), closed hat (high-passed noise, short), clap (three quick noise bursts), rim (short filtered click), tom (pitched sine drop). Scheduler uses lookahead timing against AudioContext time for tight rhythm; swing control. Signature moment: when playing, a thin black playhead sweeps the grid and the big shapes on the stage perform each hit with distinct, physical choreography: the blue circle squashes against the bar and rebounds like a struck drum skin; the red square snaps a quarter turn, rolling over its own corner on the bar; the yellow triangle flicks and shivers; the black half-circle claps against its outlined mirror image with a small burst of sparks; the thin red bar strikes down onto the black bar like a mallet and recoils; the blue ring ripples outward as concentric rings. Springy easing with overshoot (a custom spring integrator, not CSS default ease), so the poster is visibly dancing in time. The letters of the title count along as a second playhead, one letter nudging out per eighth note, and the play control pulses on each beat. A loaded default pattern makes the page impressive the instant play is pressed, and on load (before sound) the bar wipes in, the title letters slide up, and the shapes assemble with a staggered slide-and-rotate from off-canvas along the diagonal axis. Interaction: click/tap steps to toggle (drag across to paint); click a big shape to audition it; tempo by dragging the numerals along the diagonal bar (60–180 bpm, numerals in big type that grow with the tempo and swell out of the bar while you drag); swing knob as a rotating semicircle; four pattern slots as small coloured squares (A B C D) with distinct preset grooves; "clear" and "random (tasteful)" controls; mute per row by clicking its label. Keyboard: space play/stop, 1–6 audition voices, arrow keys move a focus cursor on the grid, Enter toggles; tempo and swing respond to arrow keys when focused. Phone layout: title up the left edge, stage on top, then controls, then the grid in two pages of eight steps with "1 – 8 / 9 – 16" tabs that show playhead progress and follow the playhead unless the viewer has just been editing; all targets at least 28px. Reduced motion: shapes flash fill instead of springing, the playhead steps instead of sweeping, and the entrance is a simple fade. Visible focus rings in black with a yellow offset.

Open Aurora over Skjervøy Sound ↗
020

Aurora over Skjervøy Sound

A silent winter night at 69°N, seen from the shore of an invented fjord. The page opens on stars, black mountains and one lit cabin window; then a green curtain of light unrolls from the left and the sky comes alive. Three aurora curtains hang in true perspective: folded ribbons with crisp, bright lower borders, tall rays that shimmer sideways, pale cores and violet upper fringes, the nearest arching overhead while the farthest lies low over the mouth of the sound. Snow on the peaks picks up the green, the water below breaks the whole sky into long trembling streaks, and a thread of smoke rises from the cabin beside a jetty and a moored boat. A small plate of type in the corner names the place and slowly turns the pages of an observer's notebook. The viewer can simply watch, or stir the solar wind and call down a substorm that floods the fjord with light.

↗ Open

Original prompt

An ambient, full-bleed night scene: aurora borealis over an invented northern fjord, rendered with a hand-written WebGL fragment shader. Watch-only by default, quietly interactive. Concept: light that hangs like curtains: the shader must capture the real structure of aurora: tall vertical rays, folding curtain ribbons seen in perspective, a sharp bright lower edge fading upward, slow drift with sudden brightening surges. Mood: awe, cold, silence. Palette: sky near-black blue #030B14 deepening to #01040A, dark spruce-teal #0B3B3C in the glow haze, aurora green #39E6A0, pale core #A6F0C6, occasional magenta-violet upper fringe #C04BD9; snow on the mountains a dim blue-grey #2B3B4A; one warm point: a tiny cabin window #F2B56B. Dark page (this concept requires it). Shader design: for each pixel above the horizon, accumulate three curtain layers. Each curtain is a thin luminous sheet standing on a ribbon defined in a ground-plane coordinate, displaced by layered noise so it folds; the view ray is walked along the ground and every crossing of the sheet is located exactly (grazing passes along a fold are caught as near misses and glow as bright fold edges), so overlapping folds stack up in perspective. Intensity along the vertical falls off with a sharp lower edge and a long exponential upper tail whose length varies along the ribbon; fine vertical rays come from high-frequency 1D noise along the ribbon; colours shift from green at the base to violet at the top, with a pale core where the light is strongest; the layers sit at different depths and angles and animate at different speeds, and a slow global "substorm" envelope periodically makes them surge and ripple faster. Add stars (hashed points with twinkle, dimmed behind bright aurora), a faint Milky-Way band, thin high haze. Below the horizon: mountain silhouettes on both sides of the fjord (ridge noise, four depth layers with snow caps and gullies catching green light) and the water: a mirrored sample of the sky, taken three times with small moving ripple offsets and averaged, stretched vertically so the reflection becomes long shimmering streaks. A tiny cabin with a lit window and a thread of chimney smoke sits on the snowy, spruce-lined right shore, with its own warm reflection; a small boat rests at a jetty. Interaction: moving the pointer/finger horizontally (or the left/right arrow keys) acts as the solar wind: it bends and pulls the curtains toward it and locally brightens them (with slow easing, so it feels like stirring something vast); click/tap (or Space/Enter) triggers a substorm surge that sweeps outward across the sky over several seconds: the signature moment, when the whole fjord flushes green, violet fringes flare, the snow lights up and the reflection ignites. Rarely, a meteor streaks. If idle, everything continues on its own, including an occasional natural substorm. Typography: almost none: bottom-left, tiny wide-tracked small caps in pale green-white at 70% opacity: "SKJERVØY SOUND · 69°N" and a line that updates slowly with invented, time-stamped observer notes in italic serif ("23:41 — the dog has stopped barking"); after a surge the next note reacts to it. A one-line interaction hint in the same small caps appears after a few seconds and fades once the viewer has stirred the sky. A minimal designed toggle for optional sound (off by default): low wind and a faint high crackle from filtered noise, both swelling during a surge. A "hide text" key (H), also available as a small key-cap button that stays faintly visible so touch users can bring the text back. Technical: full-screen triangle, uniforms for time, resolution, pointer, surge, entrance and meteor; render at an adaptive internal resolution (start at DPR-capped 1.5, drop resolution and then sample count if frame time exceeds ~22ms) and upscale; pause when hidden; handle context loss. Fallback without WebGL: a layered CSS/SVG scene with animated gradient curtains (blurred vertical gradient bands with ray masks and slow keyframes, mirrored into the water) over SVG mountains, cabin, jetty and boat; pointer and click still lean and brighten the curtains. Reduced motion: time runs at one-fifth speed and surges and meteors are disabled. Entrance: starts with stars only; the first curtain unfurls from the left across the sky during the first three seconds, the second follows from the right, the distant third fades in.

Open Drawer 31 — Moths That Were Never Caught ↗
021

Drawer 31 — Moths That Were Never Caught

A walnut specimen drawer slides out from under a cabinet rail and fills, moth by moth, with two dozen species that never existed: emperors with ringed eyespots, swept hawk-moths with rose underwings, angular lichen-green geometers, stout owlets and tiny fringed micros, each painted procedurally from a seed, mirrored for symmetry and then slightly damaged the way real specimens are. Rows are grouped under printed genus headers, every pin in a row sits on one line, and beneath each moth is a small tilted label in uneven sepia handwriting. The lining paper is foxed at the corners, the glass carries a faint moving reflection, and the drawer front holds a brass label holder, a cup pull and a small brass switch. To one side a huge Didot numeral and a dry curatorial note explain the collection of E. M. Hollis, who pinned the moths he was sure of and never caught. The experience is quiet and studious: pass a brass magnifier over a specimen to read its scales at three times life size while its typed index card slides out, turn the whole drawer over to see the pale undersides, pull the next drawer, and eventually discover that one specimen in each drawer is still alive.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A museum entomology drawer seen from above: a glass-topped wooden tray of pinned moths, every one procedurally generated and unique, with handwritten data labels beneath. Concept: generative natural history; a taxonomist's drawer for species that do not exist. The page should feel like leaning over a real specimen drawer. Mood: tender, studious, slightly dusty. Palette: drawer lining paper #EFE8D8 (aged, foxed at the corners), ink sepia-black #2B2118, moth browns and rusts #8C5A3C, tan #C8B48A, lichen green #6E7F5C, with rare accents permitted on individual moths only (a dusty rose #C97B84 underwing, a pale eyespot blue #7FA3B8). Wooden frame in darker walnut #4A3424. Brass fittings and a red TYPE border are the only other colours. Light page. Generation (Canvas 2D, seeded): each moth is drawn on one side and mirrored for perfect bilateral symmetry, then given tiny asymmetric wear (a nick in a wing edge, a missing antenna tip) so it looks real. Forewing and hindwing outlines come from parametric curves with per-species parameters (aspect, apex pointedness, scalloped or smooth margin, tails on some hindwings); wing patterns are layered in order: base gradient, noise mottling, transverse bands that follow curves across the wing (antemedial, postmedial lines), a discal spot or a full eyespot with concentric rings and a highlight, veins as thin lines radiating from the wing base, a fringe of tiny hair strokes on the margin; bodies are furry (short radial strokes) with segmented abdomens; antennae either feathery (pectinate) or thread-like. Five families of body plan so the drawer has variety: hawk-moth-like with narrow swept wings, emperor-like with big eyespots, geometer-like thin and angular, stout owlet-like moths with kidney marks and bordered hindwings, and tiny micro-moths with long fringes. Each specimen has a pin (steel head with a specular dot) through the thorax and casts a soft shadow offset on the paper, larger for larger moths. Layout: a grid of 24 specimens on desktop (16 at tablet widths, 12 on phones, in fewer columns) arranged in four neat rows grouped under invented genus header labels, the pins of each row aligned on one line, each with a small rectangular label pinned beneath in handwriting-like italic (Georgia/"Palatino" italic, slightly rotated, ink of uneven darkness): invented binomial names generated from Latin-ish syllable sets ("Noctua vesperalis", "Selenia umbrata Hollis, 1887"), locality and date in tiny script ("Fenwick Carr, at light, 14.vi.1902"), and a red-bordered TYPE label on one specimen. The drawer has a brass label holder on the front: "Drawer 31 · HETEROCERA · Coll. E. M. Hollis". Beside the tray (below it on small screens), sparse museum text in small caps, a very large drawer numeral, a short curatorial note about E. M. Hollis and a tally of specimens, genera and types. Signature moments: (1) on load, the drawer slides open from under the cabinet rail at the top with a wooden ease and a small settling bump, and the moths are "set" one by one in quick succession (each pops in with its shadow, 35ms stagger); (2) hover or tap a moth and it lifts toward the viewer under a round brass-rimmed magnifier that follows the pointer across the specimen: a large crisp re-render at 3× with full pattern detail, while a typed catalogue card slides out beside it (wingspan in mm, flight season, locality, and an invented one-line observation typed out character by character: "Comes to sugar. Sits with wings tented, like a small roof."); (3) one moth in each drawer is not dead: now and then its wings quiver on the pin, a first click makes it stir and adds a note to its card, and when clicked a second time it detaches, flutters erratically across the page with a flapping animation, and settles somewhere else, leaving an empty pin and its label behind. Disturbing it where it rests sends it off again. Controls: a "Next drawer" brass cup pull on the drawer front that closes this drawer and opens a newly generated one (new seed, new species, drawer number increments); a small brass toggle "undersides" that flips every specimen with a card-turn to show paler, mirrored ventral patterns. Keyboard navigation between specimens with arrow keys and visible focus; N and U as shortcuts. Reduced motion: no fluttering or staggered setting; the magnifier appears without animation.

022

The Hotel Meridian — Going Up

The viewer stands inside the lift car of the Hotel Meridian, a sixty-storey Art Deco hotel of 1931, and scrolling is riding. The screen is fixed: two brushed-brass doors engraved with chevrons and a rising sunburst, a semicircular brass floor indicator above them, verdigris wall panels either side, a strip of veined black marble underfoot. As the page scrolls the indicator hand springs from floor to floor, shaft lines streak through the gap between the doors, and at ten floors the car stops, sways, and the doors slide apart on a small flat Deco scene with one looping motion each: a revolving door, a spinning globe, waltzing silhouettes under a mirror ball, a swinging bulb on bare brick, parrots on perches, dripping lost umbrellas, a cake being iced at 3 a.m., a lone swimmer under the moon, a tram crossing below a telescope, and finally the roof with the hotel's neon sign seen backwards against the dawn. Each scene is captioned in the dry, discreet voice of the attendant, Mr. Abernathy. The mood is luxurious, nocturnal and slightly haunted; the whole page is lit by brass against lacquer black.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A scroll-driven story set inside the elevator of an invented 1931 Art Deco skyscraper hotel, "The Hotel Meridian", sixty floors tall. Scrolling is riding: the page is fixed like the inside of the lift car, and the scroll position is the floor. Concept: a building told as a vertical sequence of doors opening on brief scenes; the elevator machinery is the interface. Mood: luxurious, nocturnal, a little haunted, witty. Palette: lacquer black #0B0B0C, brass gold #C8A04A (with a lighter highlight #E7CC84 for engraved lines), burgundy #7A1F2B, ivory #F0E6D2, verdigris green #1F3B3A. Dark page, lit by the gold. Texture: fine vertical brushed-metal lines on the doors, a subtle marble veining on the floor strip. Composition (fixed viewport): the elevator doors fill the screen: two tall brass panels engraved with a symmetrical Art Deco sunburst-and-chevron pattern drawn in SVG line work. Above the doors is the signature object: a semicircular floor indicator, a brass arc graduated with a tick and a small lamp for every floor from L to 60, with engraved numerals at the notable floors (L, 7, 12, 19, 27, 33, 41, 52, 60; floor 13 has neither numeral nor lamp), and a black arrow-hand that sweeps across an ivory dial face as you scroll, with eased, slightly springy motion and a soft mechanical tick as it passes each floor. On load the hand swings down to L as the car lights flicker on. To the right, a column of round brass call buttons for the notable floors (clicking one scrolls there smoothly with the doors held shut in between; the button lights ivory); where 13 would be there is only a blanked-off screw-head cover, which works anyway and glows burgundy. To the left, a wall panel carries the large current-floor numeral in a lacquer window, a status line (floor name, "Ascending", "Descending"), the sound knob and a brass capacity plate ("nine persons or one grand piano"). Motion cue: thin horizontal lines streak past in the gap between the doors while travelling, speed proportional to scroll velocity. Story: at each of about ten notable floors the car "stops" for a stretch of scroll: the doors slide apart (transform driven by scroll progress with easing; they open fully, hold, and close again as you continue) to reveal a vignette drawn in flat Deco illustration (SVG shapes in the palette, each with one piece of looping motion) with a short caption typeset below in the doorway: Lobby (a revolving door turning, the night porter's bell); 7, The Cartographers' Club (a wall of maps, a globe spinning); 12, the Ballroom where it has been 1934 since 1934 (silhouetted dancers rotating, a mirrored ball throwing dots); 13, which the hotel insists does not exist (the doors open on bare brick and a single swinging bulb; they shut quickly); 19, the Aviary Suite (parrots on brass perches); 27, Housekeeping, where lost umbrellas go; 33, the Night Kitchen (copper pans, steam, a cake being iced at 3 a.m.); 41, the Swimming Pool in the Sky (ripples, a lone swimmer's line); 52, the Observatory Bar (a telescope, a cocktail named "The Last Tram"); 60, the Roof (the doors open on the city far below, searchlights sweeping, the hotel's neon sign seen from behind, dawn starting). Captions are specific and literary, two or three sentences each, in the voice of a discreet lift attendant named Mr. Abernathy. Typography: Deco display capitals drawn with geometric proportions: tall, thin, widely tracked sans (Futura/"Century Gothic"/"Gill Sans" stack, uppercase, letterspacing 0.35em) in gold for floor names; large stylised numerals for the current floor; captions in an elegant serif italic (Georgia/Palatino) in ivory. The title screen at scroll zero: the closed doors with "THE HOTEL MERIDIAN" engraved across them and the instruction "scroll to ascend" shown as a small engraved plate with a downward chevron that bobs. Details: the car sways by a pixel or two when stopping; the indicator arc's lamp glows for the current floor; an optional sound toggle (off by default) adds a low hum, door rumbles and a bell "ding" on arrival via Web Audio; at the top, a "Return to lobby" brass lever sends the car down in one long accelerating glide with the hand sweeping back. The night porter's bell in the lobby can be rung by clicking it. Phone: buttons become a horizontal strip beneath the doors, the floor numeral and sound knob sit in the top corners beside the dial, and the wall panels are dropped; short landscape screens place the caption beside the vignette. Reduced motion: doors cross-fade open; no streak lines or sway.

023

Side A — A Turntable in CSS

A catalogue-style spread for an invented 1974 turntable, the Marlowe PL·7, playing an invented jazz LP, "Slow Weather" by the Marguerite Okonkwo Quintet. A walnut plinth with a cream deck plate sits tilted in real perspective under a translucent sage dust cover, in front of a burnt-orange supergraphic sun and sage shelf stripes; a fat italic "Side A" headline, a sleeve note, a leaning record sleeve and a track list sit to the left, and a brushed-aluminium amplifier with two VU meters and a pair of walnut speakers complete the system. The page looks like a still illustration until the viewer presses START: the lamp and strobe light up, the record gathers speed, the arm rises, crosses to the lead-in groove, settles, and begins a four-minute walk toward the label while the meters dance, the speakers breathe arcs and slanted window light slowly warms the page. Everything is drawn from HTML elements and gradients and everything moves without a line of script.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A CSS-only (no JavaScript, no SVG images; shapes made from HTML elements, gradients, borders, transforms, clip-path and masks) illustration of a 1970s walnut hi-fi turntable seen from above at a slight angle (a real 3-D transform with mild perspective), which actually operates: the viewer can start the platter, cue the tonearm and change speed using only styled checkbox/radio inputs and labels. Concept: an object so carefully drawn in CSS that people inspect the source. Mood: warm, nostalgic, Sunday afternoon. Palette: walnut #5A3A22 (with lighter grain streaks #7A5232 and dark #3E2716), cream #E8DCC4 for the deck plate and page, burnt orange #E2711D for the record label and one accent, soft black #1A1A1A for vinyl and rubber, sage-grey #9FB3A8 for the dust-cover tint and secondary shapes; brushed aluminium built from conic and repeating gradients. Warm light page: the page background is cream with a large orange quarter-circle (with concentric rings) in the top-right corner and sage stripes in the 70s supergraphic manner that run under the hi-fi like a shelf and turn upward through a rounded corner on wide screens. The object: a plinth with wood grain (layered repeating-linear and repeating-radial gradients plus subtle noise from gradients) and a visible front face with a maker's badge, a cream top plate with corner screws, a platter with two rows of strobe dots around its rim, a rubber mat, a 12-inch record with fine grooves (repeating-radial-gradient), track gaps and a rotating wedge of light reflection (conic-gradient highlights) so the spin is visible, an orange centre label whose tiny text ("SIDE A · 33⅓ · MARGUERITE OKONKWO QUINTET · SLOW WEATHER") is set letter by letter around the rim and rotates with the disc, a spindle; a straight tonearm with counterweight, pivot gimbal, angled headshell, finger lift and sage cartridge, plus an arm rest; a cue lever; a speed selector (33 / 45) as radio buttons styled as a rocker; a start/stop control as a chunky square button with an orange pilot lamp; a strobe lamp housing that glows onto the rim; a 45 adaptor in a well; four rubber feet; a hinged smoked dust cover standing open at the back, built from four 3-D faces. Operation (signature moment), all via the :checked state of inputs placed first in the document and general-sibling selectors (so nothing depends on :has(); :has() is used only for one enhancement, bars of afternoon window light that fade across the page while a record is on): pressing start lights the pilot lamp and strobe, the platter spins up over about 2.4 s into a steady linear rotation (1.8 s per revolution for 33, 1.333 s for 45; the spin-up and run-down are a decelerating counter-rotation on a wrapper so there is never a jump in angle), then the tonearm lifts (small scale and shadow change), swings over the lead-in groove with a custom cubic-bezier, lowers, and slowly tracks inward across the record over four minutes while "sound" appears visually: a pair of VU meters on a slim brushed-aluminium amplifier under the turntable begin to dance (two different keyframe programmes) and thin concentric arcs pulse gently from two small walnut speakers flanking the composition. Pressing stop lifts the arm, returns it to its rest, lowers it, and the platter runs down with ease-out. The cue lever (checkbox) lifts the arm mid-record, parks it exactly where it is, drops the VU needles to rest and silences the arcs; lowering it resumes. The speed switch changes rotation rate without a jump and swaps which strobe row stands still and which drifts. Details: hover (or press) the record to see the room's reflection slide round; hover the dust cover, or click/tap it to latch, and it opens further; hover the 45 adaptor and the amplifier knobs; a record sleeve leans at the left with a designed cover (abstract orange/sage/black shapes and the album title in a fat 70s serif: Cooper Black falling back to Georgia bold italic with tight tracking), an inner sleeve that slides out on hover, and a track list of invented tunes with durations (Side A on the wide layout; Sides A and B plus personnel on the stacked layout). Liner copy in small type on the cream page: two sentences about the album in an authentic sleeve-note voice. A numbered "To play" list and a short specification list act as catalogue annotations and interaction hints; a one-line colophon closes the page. Typography: fat, tight, rounded-serif display for "Side A" (Cooper Black/Georgia bold italic) with stacked orange and sage offset shadows, small tracked sans caps for hardware legends (START · STOP · CUE · SPEED · STROBE · 33 · 45). Responsive: the whole hi-fi is drawn in em units driven by one viewport-derived unit, a catalogue spread on wide screens and a single stacked column down to 360px without overflow; controls keep roughly 40px touch targets through enlarged invisible hit areas. All inputs keyboard-focusable with visible orange focus rings on their labels. Reduced motion: platter turns very slowly, meters hold still, arm changes position without sweeping, speaker arcs are static.

Open Everyone in Ostrava-under-Hill ↗
024

Everyone in Ostrava-under-Hill

A parish census drawn as a single sheet of cream paper: on the left, a small masthead and seven questions set as large serif sentences; on the right, one enormous numeral, a phrase that completes it, and three hundred coloured dots that are, each of them, somebody. The dots rain in and settle into a packed cluster of households; ask another question and every family lifts off together, arcs across the page and springs into place as a population pyramid, nineteen clusters of occupation, a hand-ruled map of six streets under a hill, a beeswarm of arrival years, a choir on its risers above a grey audience, or a ring of friendships. The headline counts to its new value, the rules and labels draw themselves in, and a sentence written from the data sits underneath, dry and affectionate. Hovering any dot produces a little card with a name, an age and a one-line life; pinning it lets you follow one person through every statistic they belong to.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A unit-chart data visualization of an invented village of exactly 300 people, "Ostrava-under-Hill". Every resident is one dot, generated in the page from a seeded random model with a name, age, household, occupation, street, how long they have lived there, whether they sing in the choir, and who their best friend is. The viewer asks the village questions and the same 300 dots rearrange to answer. Concept: statistics that never stop being people. Mood: warm, humane, lightly humorous; a parish newsletter drawn by an information designer. Palette: cream #FFF9F0 page, charcoal #2F2F2F text, and four pastel-strong category hues: coral #EF767A, slate blue #456990, mint teal #49BEAA, wheat gold #EEB868, with a pale grey #D9D4CC for de-emphasised dots. Light, pastel, with a faint paper grain. Data model (clearly fictional, internally consistent): age distribution with 75 children, 135 working-age adults and 90 elderly (one newborn only days old); 113 households on six named streets (Mill Lane, Upper Row, The Shambles, Chapel Street, Goose Green, Station Approach); occupations with charming specificity (11 farmers, 4 teachers, 1 bell-ringer, 2 bakers who are rivals, a retired lighthouse keeper far from any sea, 23 commuters, 61 schoolchildren, a vicar, the staff of the Drowned Goose, the creamery, etc., 19 groups in all); 34 choir members (10 sopranos, 9 altos, 7 tenors, 8 basses); arrival years with a bump in 1974 "when the creamery opened" and one family who arrived last month; friendships as links, biased towards the same street and similar age. Views (each a layout of the same dots, chosen from a list of plain-language questions set as large clickable sentences): "Who lives here?" (a loose organic cluster of packed circles, households kept together, with six hand-ruled call-outs: the oldest, the newest, the bell-ringer, the lighthouse keeper, a baker and, on the opposite side, the other baker); "How old are we?" (a population pyramid built from stacked dots in five-year bands, women left, men right, coloured by generation); "What do we do all day?" (grouped clusters with labels and counts, sorted by size); "Where do we live?" (a little schematic map of the six streets with households as tight clumps of dots along them, and the river, church, hill, mill, railway and goose green drawn in thin lines); "How long have we been here?" (a beeswarm along a timeline of arrival year, from born-here elders to last month's newcomers); "Who sings?" (choir members rise into four curved rows, soprano/alto/tenor/bass, everyone else greys out below as the audience in pews with an aisle); "Who is whose best friend?" (dots arranged on a ring grouped by street, with street names set along the arc and curved links; mutual best friends in coral, unrequited in grey, and a count of each). Signature moment: the transitions. On changing question, every dot travels to its new position along a slightly curved path with individual delays based on its household (so families move together, in a wave ordered by destination), spring-settling with a tiny overshoot, taking about 1.4s in total; the headline number counts up/down, and axis lines, map lines, links and labels draw in afterwards. Implemented on Canvas with a hit-test grid for hover. On load the dots rain into the first cluster. Interaction: hover/tap a dot to meet the person: the dot enlarges with a ring, and a small card gives a name and one-line story assembled from their attributes with wit ("Agnieszka Dvořák, 71. Mill Lane. Retired postmistress, alto. Has lived here 71 years and has opinions about all of them."), plus their best friend and whether it is mutual, while their household members and best friend are highlighted with thin connecting lines in any view. Click to pin a person and follow them through every view (their dot keeps a ring). Arrow keys on the focused chart step through residents; Enter pins, Escape lets go. A search-by-name field with custom styling (accent-insensitive, with a small results list; matches stay bright while everyone else fades). A small "re-roll the village" control regenerates with a new seed, shown as the village number. Typography: questions in a large friendly serif (Georgia/Palatino) with the active one in bold and underlined by a hand-drawn SVG stroke; big tabular numerals for the answer ("34 of us sing") with a supporting line beneath; annotations in a small humanist sans ("Gill Sans"/"Trebuchet MS" stack). Layout: left column of masthead, questions, search and re-roll; right the answer, legend, chart and insight; stacked on phones with questions as a horizontally scrollable strip inside its own container. Each view has one written insight sentence beneath it, generated from the data ("The oldest person on Goose Green is 94; the youngest is eleven days old. They are neighbours."). Reduced motion: dots cross-fade to new positions and numbers change at once.

Open The Wheel — Throw a Pot ↗
025

The Wheel — Throw a Pot

A quiet potter's studio on a single screen: linen wall with a patch of window light, a bench with a dusty blue water bucket and two spare balls of clay, and in the middle a charcoal splash pan with 900 g of wet terracotta turning on the wheel head. The page opens with the lump wobbling into centre and a pair of ghost fingers opening it and making one pull, so the clay rises in the first seconds; then the viewer takes over and the wall follows their fingers, getting taller as it is collared, squatter as it is bellied, thinner as it is pulled, and folding into a heap if it is rushed. Around the wheel the type is set like a craft catalogue: small-caps labels, potter's notes in tiny italic numerals, a hand-drawn tool rack and one large italic line, "Centre. Open. Pull. Breathe.", whose words light in turn. Wiring the pot off begins the second act: it is lifted on tongs, dipped into a bucket of glaze, watched through a glowing kiln peephole while the cones bend and the counter climbs to 1,240 °C, and comes out glossy to slide onto a wooden shelf that fills up over the session.

↗ Open

Original prompt

An interactive pottery wheel: a lump of wet clay spins on a wheel head and the viewer shapes it by dragging, then trims, glazes and fires it, and keeps the result on a shelf. Canvas 2D, rendered as a convincing lathe solid. Concept: a tool-toy with the tactile satisfaction of throwing; the form is a profile curve revolved, shaded so it reads as wet, spinning clay. Mood: quiet studio, earthy, absorbed. Palette: studio wall/canvas apron linen #E7DCCB, wet clay terracotta #B5653A, deep clay shadow #7A3E22, slip highlights #D99A6C, charcoal #2E2A26 for type and wheel, and a studio accent of dusty blue-grey #5F7F86 (water bucket, glaze default). Glazes offered: celadon #A9C5B0, tenmoku brown-black #2A1A14, cobalt #2B4C7E, oatmeal white #E9E1CF with iron speckle, ash green drip. Light page. Rendering: the pot is defined by a radius profile r(y) sampled at ~80 heights plus an inner wall thickness. Draw it as stacked horizontal ellipses/strips from bottom to top with Lambert-style shading across x (a lit left side, a core shadow, a reflected rim light on the right), the visible inner rim ellipse at the top showing the hollow with darker interior, and throwing rings: fine horizontal ridges whose phase scrolls sideways to convey rotation, plus a few drifting specular streaks and slip drips. The wheel head is an ellipse with concentric rings spinning beneath; splash pan around it; a small pool of slip. While the wheel spins, an off-centre pot visibly wobbles: if the viewer pushes too fast or too hard at speed, the wall develops a growing sinusoidal wobble and can collapse into a slumped, folded heap (a charming failure with a "wedge it and start again" prompt). Interaction: drag on the pot's silhouette (pointer or touch): pushing inward at a height narrows the form there, pulling outward bellies it, with a smooth Gaussian brush along height and volume loosely conserved (narrowing makes it taller); dragging upward along the wall "pulls" the clay taller and thinner; pressing on top opens/widens the rim. Fingers appear as two soft-shaded abstract finger shapes at the contact point with slip trails. A foot pedal slider controls wheel speed (affects ring motion, wobble sensitivity and sound). While the clay is still a solid lump, any press on it opens it. Tool rack (drawn tools, not generic buttons): hands (the default, to return to after a tool), sponge (smooths the profile), wooden rib (straightens a section), needle (trims the rim level), wire (cuts the pot off). Keyboard alternative: arrow keys move a contact point up/down and in/out; shift+up pulls the wall taller; space presses the top. A small "wedge & start over" link resets the clay at any time. Signature moment: after the wire cut, the sequence of making: choose a glaze by dipping (the pot is lowered on dipping tongs into a bucket, its foot waxed, and comes up coated in chalky raw glaze with a wavering glaze line and a bare foot ring), then "Fire": the view cross-fades into a kiln peephole glowing from dull red through orange to white-yellow with a pyrometric cone bending and a temperature counter climbing to 1,240 °C, then cools, and the finished pot emerges with glossy glaze shading (sharper specular, pooled darker colour in the throwing rings, speckle), and slides onto a wooden shelf of the viewer's finished pots at the bottom of the page (kept for the session and restored on reload within it, each with a tiny maker's mark and number). On load, the lump is already centring and a pair of ghost hands demonstrates one pull so the first seconds show the clay rising. Typography: restrained craft-catalogue style: small caps labels in a humanist sans ("Gill Sans"/"Optima"/"Trebuchet MS"), a single line of larger serif italic: "Centre. Open. Pull. Breathe." Live readouts in tiny type like a potter's notes: height in cm, widest point, wall thickness, clay weight 900 g (a little less after a needle trim), and an automatically named form (beaker, bowl, jar, bottle, vase, dish). The four words of the italic line light up in turn as the viewer centres, opens, pulls and rests. Optional sound toggle (off by default): low wheel hum varying with speed and a wet friction noise while shaping. Reduced motion: rings do not scroll, wobble disabled, kiln sequence shortened to a cross-fade.

Open Frost on the Window ↗
026

Frost on the Window

A cold room at 2 a.m., seen as one full-bleed window: four panes in a dark wooden frame, a sodium-lit street dissolved into heptagonal bokeh behind them, and frost drawing itself across the glass in real time. The first ferns shoot out of the lower-left corner, then everything slows to a patient creep: curved plumes with herringbone side branches at sixty degrees, six-armed frost flowers, lines of ice running along invisible scratches, all stopping where they meet older ice so the pane fills over about two minutes yet keeps clear islands. Where the ice is dense the street behind turns pale and diffuse, and crystals glint when a lamp or a passing car's headlights slide behind them. Hold a finger to the glass and a haze of breath blooms, the frost melts outward into beads, a few drops run down leaving clear tracks, and when you let go the patch quietly freezes over again with ferns that were never there before. The only words are "breathe here", fingertip-written in old condensation and already half frozen, a thermometer on the frame, and a tiny caption on the sill.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A night-time window pane on which frost grows in real time: feathered fern-like ice crystals creep in from the edges and from scratches in the glass; the viewer can breathe on the glass to melt a patch and watch it freeze over again. Full-bleed Canvas 2D simulation. Concept: slow crystalline growth as a living drawing, seen against the warm blur of a street outside. Mood: tender, wintry, intimate; the inside of a cold room at 2 a.m. Palette: night blue-black #0A1522, window-glass blue #16304A, ice pale #CFE8F5, crystal white #FFFFFF, and the warm sodium light of street lamps outside #F2B56B (with a few soft red and green points from a distant traffic light, used sparingly). Dark page. Layers: (1) Background "outside": out-of-focus bokeh discs (large, soft-edged, slightly polygonal, overlapping with additive blending) from street lamps and windows, a hint of a dark roofline and bare branches, slow drifting snow as blurred specks; bokeh discs gently pulse and one occasionally passes (a car's headlights sliding across). (2) The glass: faint condensation gradient, a wooden window frame and one muntin cross dividing the pane into four, drawn at the screen edges in dark silhouette with a thin highlight. (3) The frost: the simulation. Frost simulation: a growth model that produces dendritic, feathery "fern frost" rather than blobs: e.g. many growing tips that advance along a heading with slight curvature, spawn side branches at regular intervals at ±~60° (hexagonal symmetry bias) with decreasing length, stop when they meet existing ice (occupancy grid), and thicken over time; seeded along the frame edges, along a few invisible "scratch" curves across the glass, and at random nucleation points that create star-shaped flowers. Draw ice as fine bright strokes with a soft glow, with sparkle: a small fraction of crystals glint as the bokeh light passes behind them. The frost refracts what is behind it: where ice is dense, the background appears more diffuse and paler (composite a blurred, lightened copy of the background through the frost density mask). Growth is slow but always visible: tips creeping at a few pixels per second, the whole pane taking around two minutes to fill, never fully (clear islands remain). Interaction (signature moment): press and hold anywhere (mouse or touch): "breathing" on the glass: a round patch of fog blooms (soft white haze with tiny condensation droplets), the frost within it melts back from the centre outward into droplets, a few of which grow heavy and run down the pane in stick-slip jerks leaving clear trails, and some beads freeze where they stop; through the cleared patch the street outside is sharper. Release, and after a moment the fog fades and new frost begins to regrow into the patch from its rim, with fresh, different ferns. Dragging a finger draws a clear line (write a name on the window) which slowly frosts over. Moving the pointer without pressing leaves the faintest warmth trail. Keyboard: with the pane focused, arrow keys move a small ring and holding Space breathes there. Typography: nearly none. One small line set low on the glass as if written with a fingertip in the frost and then half frozen over: "breathe here". A discreet thermometer drawn on the frame (mercury column, −11 °C) whose hover shows "−11 °C outside · 14 °C inside". A small control for optional sound on the sill (off by default, drawn as a little radiator whose fins warm to sodium when on): a faint ticking radiator and muffled wind, plus a soft breath while holding. Corner caption in tiny tracked caps at low contrast but readable: "FROST ON THE WINDOW". Entrance: clear glass with bokeh for a second, then the first ferns shoot in from the lower-left corner quickly (fast at first, then slowing to the ambient rate) so the first three seconds show dramatic growth. Performance: incremental drawing to an offscreen frost canvas (only new segments drawn each frame), capped number of active tips, DPR capped at 2. Reduced motion: frost appears mostly grown and static, the street is still; melting works with simple fades and the frost fades back afterwards.

Open Five Calligrams ↗
027

Five Calligrams

A fine-press book of five pages on warm paper, in blue-black ink with one red mark per page. The cover sets "Five" in italic over an enormous "Calligrams." and types its subtitle onto a pale wave. Each page then performs its poem: Rain falls in slanted columns of words that splash letter by letter at the ground and part around one red, dry "umbrella"; River carries a long sentence down a meander, letters springing at the source, swelling where two small tributary sentences run into it; Heron stands as a bird made of type and, when ready, lunges its neck of words at the water as "not yet" becomes "now"; Stair lets the reader's scroll bounce a red full stop down seventeen word-steps to the floor; Breath opens and closes a ring of words at four seconds in and six out. Any poem can be un-shaped into plain stanzas and shaped again, so the reader can watch form become, and stop being, meaning.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A small anthology of five original concrete poems (calligrams) in which the text itself forms and performs its subject, typeset on SVG paths and animated. Concept: reading as watching; the shape of the words is the meaning. Original poems written for this page (short, good, unsentimental); the calligram form is the tradition, the poems are new. Mood: quiet, bookish, playful. Palette: book paper #FBFAF5, ink blue-black #14213D, one red #C1121F used for a single word or mark in each poem (and for the full stop after the cover title), and a pale grey-blue #C9D1DB for ghost paths. Light page with a faint paper grain. Layout: a vertical sequence of five full-viewport "pages", each a poem centred in generous margins like a fine-press book: a small folio number and title at the top in tracked small caps, the calligram in the middle, a hairline rule and a one-line note at the bottom. A cover at the start: a large title set in an elegant book face (Baskerville/Palatino/Georgia), "Five" in italic above a very large "Calligrams." with a red full stop, the subtitle "poems that do what they say" typed onto a long ghost wave that runs the width of the cover (with the aside "the shape of the words is the meaning" further along it on wide screens), and a slim table of contents in which each poem is paired with its verb (Rain falls, River forgets, Heron waits, Stair goes down, Breath comes back). A column of small ticks at the right edge marks the six pages. The poems (each uses SVG text on paths, with lettering sized to remain legible on phones; text is real selectable SVG text, and an accessible plain-text version of each poem is provided in a visually hidden paragraph): I. "Rain": the six lines of the poem fall as near-vertical columns of words, each line on its own path slanting with the wind, at different speeds, each column carrying its line twice so the page is always raining; the last word of each line travels as separate letters and, on reaching the ground line, the letters are thrown up in small arcs while two ghost ripple arcs spread. Fast hairline ghost streaks (and, on wide screens, faint ghost columns of "it rains") fall between the lines. The red element: the single word "umbrella" held still in the middle under a ghost canopy, with the rain parting around it. Text: "it has been raining since the word for it / it rains on the just and on the bus stop / on the roofers, who have all gone home / on the washing, which was nearly dry / on the sea, which has no use for more / it rains on everything except one umbrella". II. "River": one long sentence in eight clauses set on a meandering path that flows from the top left to the bottom right (each clause's stretch of the meander is re-sampled every frame, letters emerging one by one at the source and leaving one by one at the mouth), with two tributary sentences joining it, one from below at a trough and one from above at a crest; where they meet the type size swells (roughly 18, 23, 31px on desktop) and the ghost water line gains another strand. The text is about a river forgetting its source; the red word is "upstream". Hovering (or pressing on touch) slows the current. III. "Heron": the bird's standing silhouette is built from text: neck a single S-curved line of words, body a packed block of seven short lines of varying size in a tilted teardrop, legs two thin lines of widely spaced letters, the head the words "not yet" whose "o" is the red eye. Every so often (and when tapped) the neck coils, straightens and strikes down to the water in an arc, letterspacing stretching like sinew; at the moment of the strike the head reads "now" and ghost rings spread on the water; then it returns. The poem is about patience. IV. "Stair": the seventeen words of one sentence set as steps descending over a ghost stair profile ("a sentence is a staircase you go down carrying one full stop until you find the floor"); the page is pinned while scrolling makes a small red full stop bounce down the stairs, word by word, with squash and stretch, each word dipping and inking in as it is landed on and a ghost arc recording every hop, until the stop comes to rest after "floor". V. "Breath": a four-line poem set in a circle (four arcs of text on a circular path) that slowly expands and contracts at breathing pace (4s in, 6s out), letterspacing opening and closing; in the centre in tiny type "in" / "out" inside a ten-tick dial with a travelling dot. The red element: a single comma. Pressing and holding holds the breath. Signature moment: the River: a long, legible sentence visibly flowing along a meander, with tributaries merging in type. Motion throughout is requestAnimationFrame-driven path re-sampling and path morphing with eased curves; each poem "writes itself in" when its page enters view: letters appear sequentially along the path at reading speed, not a fade. Interaction: hover/tap effects per poem as described (in Rain the wind follows the pointer); clicking a poem's title toggles "read plainly", which gracefully un-shapes the text: every line's path morphs, staggered, into a straight line of an ordinary left-aligned stanza (and back); arrow keys move between poems with an eased page turn, and inside Stair the down arrow first carries the full stop to the floor. Typography: the poems in a classic serif italic and roman (Baskerville/"Palatino Linotype"/Georgia), sizes from 13px to about 40px with deliberate scale changes within a poem; notes in small sans caps. Phone: paths are recomputed for narrow viewports (a steeper portrait-shaped meander, no ghost rain columns and fewer streaks, a staircase that turns back on itself in switchback flights, a smaller heron and circle) so nothing is illegibly small or overflowing. Reduced motion: all poems hold a composed still frame, fully written; the River does not flow; breathing is paused with "in" and "out" both shown; the full stop moves step to step without bouncing.

Conservas Maré Alta — Tinned Fish Since 1923

A sunny, proud landing page for the invented cannery Conservas Maré Alta of Porto Salgado, drawn entirely as mid-century tinned-fish packaging: flat inks in cream, tomato red, sea teal, sun yellow and navy, halftone-dot shadows, off-register outlines, arched banners and numerals in ovals. Below sit a yellow shelf of six differently designed tins that turn over to show ingredients, a one-line serving suggestion and an escudo price sticker; a teal strip of six perforated stamps telling the process; a typed letter from fourth-generation owner Inês Carvalhal with a hand-drawn signature; and a footer shaped like the embossed bottom of a tin, with stockists, factory visits and a newsletter that answers with a rubber stamp.

Original prompt

A landing page for an invented Portuguese-style cannery, "Conservas Maré Alta" ("High Tide"), founded 1923 in the invented harbour town of Porto Salgado. The page is built from retro packaging illustration: flat inks, bold labels, decorative borders, the visual world of mid-century tinned-fish wrappers, all drawn in SVG and CSS. Concept: the tins are the heroes, and one of them opens. Mood: sunny, nostalgic, appetising, proud. Palette: label cream #F6EAD0, tomato red #D7372F, sea teal #0F6E78, sun yellow #F2B441, deep navy ink #1B2D3A. Flat colour with slight print texture (a fine halftone on shadows, off-register outlines by 1px on some illustrations). Light page. A tiny drop of oil rolls off the lid. Motion: illustrated elements animate like printed ephemera coming alive: sunrays rotate slowly behind the hero tin, the leaping sardine arcs once on load with a splash, waves in borders scroll as repeating patterns, sections enter with a label-being-pasted motion (slight rotation settling with a soft spring), tins on the shelf bob when the pointer runs along them. The cursor on desktop is a small fish-fork. Reduced motion: lid opens with a simple reveal, no bobbing or rotation. Voice: warm, dry-humoured, specific; proud without marketing clichés.

Open Holm Skerry Weather Station ↗
029

Holm Skerry Weather Station

A wall of Victorian instruments mounted on grained mahogany: a great aneroid barometer at the centre, ringed by an anemometer, a 32-point wind rose, a psychrometer, a rain gauge, a clockwork barograph in a glazed case, a sunshine recorder and a cluster of small dials for tide, sea and visibility, all under a brass plaque. Nothing is a number in a box; every value is a needle, a mercury column, a water level or an ink line. A week of invented weather runs on a fast clock, and a deep Atlantic low arrives a few seconds after the page opens: the barometer needle sinks heavily into the red sector, the vane backs and twitches, the anemometer surges with each gust, rain streaks cross the window reflected in every glass, the light dims, and a red storm cone climbs the signal mast while the observer's log writes its warning in a slanting hand. Then the front goes through, the vane flies round to the north-west, and the glass leaps. The experience is meant to be calm and exact, like standing in a quiet room while the weather happens outside.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A weather dashboard for an invented North Atlantic island, "Holm Skerry (Station 03-117)", presented as a mahogany instrument board of brass-and-enamel analog instruments drawn in SVG, each needle moving with real mechanical inertia. All data is clearly fictional, generated in the page from a smooth, plausible simulated weather system (a passing depression: pressure falls, wind backs and rises, rain arrives, front passes, pressure climbs, wind veers), looping over a simulated week and advancing in accelerated time (about 1.8 seconds per simulated hour). Concept: a dashboard with no rectangles of numbers: everything is a dial. Mood: steadfast, maritime, Victorian-scientific; calm even when the weather is not. Palette: enamel dial cream #EDE4CF, brass #B08D3C (with highlight #D9BC6A and shadow #6E5620), deep green-black #23312F for lettering, the barograph case lining, the signal-mast porthole and the baize desk under the log, signal red #A33B2B for warning sectors and one needle (the wind vane), sea grey-green #6F8F8C for secondary marks. Board in dark wood tones derived from #3B2A20 with procedural grain. Light instruments on a dark-wood board; overall the page reads warm and light because the dials dominate. Instruments (each with bezel, glass with a window-pane reflection, screws, engraved scale, maker's name "Tarrant & Lyle, Leith"): 1) Aneroid barometer (large, centre): 950–1050 hPa scale with the traditional words STORMY · RAIN · CHANGE · FAIR · VERY DRY, an inner inches-of-mercury scale, a black indicating needle and a brass set-hand the viewer can drag to mark the current pressure and see the trend. 2) Anemometer dial with wind speed in knots and a Beaufort ring, plus a small spinning cup-anemometer illustration whose rotation speed matches. 3) Wind direction rose with a 32-point compass and a red vane needle that hunts and gusts realistically. 4) Thermometer pair (dry and wet bulb) as glass tubes with rising mercury, and a derived humidity hand on a sub-dial. 5) Rain gauge: a graduated glass cylinder filling in mm, emptied daily at 09:00 with a little drain animation. 6) Barograph: a clockwork drum in a glazed case with a paper chart on which a pen, lifted by an aneroid stack, traces pressure in violet-ish ink; the drum visibly rotates and the trace extends: the signature object. About three days of the trace show on the curved face of the drum; the enlarged view unrolls all seven days. 7) Sunshine recorder card (burn trace under a glass sphere) and a small tide dial (high/low water hand). 8) Sea state indicator and visibility as two small subsidiary dials. In the header, flanking the plaque: a small 24-hour station clock with a day-of-week hand, and a signal mast in a baize-lined porthole. Signature moment: needle physics and the storm. Every needle is a damped spring following its target value with instrument-appropriate character (the barometer slow, heavy and slightly sticky, the wind vane nervous, the anemometer surging in gusts), with tiny tremble. When the depression deepens past 980 hPa, the board reacts: a red storm cone hoists on the signal mast (point down for a southerly gale, flipping point up when the wind veers north-west), the lighting dims as clouds pass (the glass highlights shift), rain streaks faintly across the window reflection in the dial glasses, and the log writes a gale warning in red ink. Tapping the barometer glass (click) makes the needle jump slightly, as with a real aneroid. Interaction: a brass time lever on a rail fixed to the foot of the board scrubs through the simulated week (drag or arrow keys; needles chase the values with inertia; the gale period is marked red in the slot), a hold/auto switch resumes; hovering any dial shows its exact reading in a small engraved-plate tooltip; clicking a dial (or its brass name plate; for the barometer the plate only, since its glass is for tapping) enlarges it to a detailed view beside a record card with the exact reading, a note, and a 24-hour history inked as a small chart. Below the board: the observer's log, handwritten-style italic entries generated from the state with the terse poetry of a shipping forecast ("Wednesday 0600. 980 falling quickly. S 8, rain, sea very rough. Shutters barred on the weather side."), and a typeset "general synopsis" with a twelve-hour forecast. Typography: engraved-style serif capitals for dial lettering (Georgia/"Times New Roman" with wide tracking), numerals rotated around the dials, title on a brass plaque: "HOLM SKERRY · METEOROLOGICAL STATION · EST. 1884". Responsive: instruments reflow from a 4-column board to two columns and then a single column of large dials on phones, barometer first. Reduced motion: needles move directly without oscillation; the drum does not rotate (the week's chart lies still and the pen travels across it).
