Open Rose Window ↗
030

Rose Window

A hushed stone nave seen from the floor: a wall of ashlar blocks, a pointed arch with carved mouldings, two dark columns, and high in the wall a rose window whose several hundred pieces of ruby, cobalt, gold, emerald, amethyst and pale glass are generated afresh by gothic rules of symmetry, nested rings, lancets, foils and roundels. The viewer holds the sun. As it moves, a hotspot of brilliance travels across the glass, bright pieces bloom over their leads, and the whole window is thrown down as a long, soft, skewed pool of colour across the flagstones, with tinted dust sparkling in the shaft between. Each new commission is drawn in lead from the centre outward and then glazed ring by ring, every piece flaring as it is set, while a carved inscription records its order and opus number.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A generative stained-glass rose window in a dark stone nave, on a full-bleed Canvas 2D. Each window is unique, built by an algorithm with gothic logic (radial symmetry, nested rings of tracery, petals and foils), and lit from behind by a sun whose position the viewer controls; the coloured light falls through the window onto the stone floor. Concept: generative art with architecture and light: the wow is the glowing glass and the pool of colour it throws. Mood: monumental, hushed, devotional without religious iconography (purely geometric and botanical motifs). Palette: stone darkness #08070A with cool grey masonry highlights (#2A2830, #3A3842), lead came lines near-black #0B0A0C, and glass jewel tones: ruby #B3202A, cobalt #1B4FA0, gold #E0A100, emerald #2E8B57, amethyst #6C2A7A, with small amounts of pale "white" glass #E9E2CF. Dark page. Generation: choose a symmetry order n from {8, 10, 12, 16} and build the window as concentric zones: a central oculus (a rosette or star), an inner ring of n petals (lancet or teardrop shapes with trefoil heads), a middle ring of 2n smaller lights, an outer ring of roundels and spandrel pieces filling the gaps, all bounded by a thick stone ring with carved mouldings. Each compartment is subdivided into individual glass pieces by leading (subdivide petals with curved bands, borders with small rectangular quarries, diamonds, a vine or wave motif), so there are several hundred pieces. Colour assignment follows rules, not noise: a dominant pair (e.g. ruby and cobalt as in medieval glass) alternating by ring and position, gold accents on borders and centres, greens in foliage bands, consistent across the n-fold symmetry. Each piece has slight hue/brightness variation, streaks and tiny bubbles (per-piece radial gradient and a few speckles), and painted grisaille detail lines on some pieces (thin dark strokes). Stone tracery between lights is drawn with a bevel (light and dark edges). Light: the sun position (pointer/finger position, or slow automatic drift from east to west if idle) determines: (1) each glass piece's brightness: a soft hotspot of brilliance moves across the window opposite the sun, with halation: bright pieces bloom slightly over the lead lines (additive blurred pass); (2) the projection: the signature moment: the whole window is cast as a skewed, stretched, blurred pool of coloured light onto the stone floor beneath, with a soft bounce of its average colour spilling up the foot of the wall (draw the window image row by row through the floor's perspective with shear/scale according to sun angle, with blur and additive blending, over visible flagstones so the joints show through), sliding and lengthening as the sun moves; dust motes drift and sparkle inside the invisible light shaft between window and floor, tinted by the colour they pass through. Architecture: the window sits high in a wall of large ashlar blocks with subtle mortar lines; a pointed arch moulding frames it; below, the floor recedes in perspective with flagstones; two column silhouettes at the sides for scale. Interaction: move to place the sun; click/tap or press Space to "commission a new window": the lead lines of the new design draw themselves in from the centre outward (animated strokes), then the glass is "glazed" in ring by ring, each piece lighting up like being set into place, 1.5–2.5s total with staggering; this also plays on load. A small control cycles time of day: dawn (warm low light, long projection), noon (shorter, intense), dusk (red-gold), night (moonlight: desaturated blue glass, faint projection). Keyboard: arrows move the sun, T also cycles the hour. A small sun (or crescent moon at night) glyph replaces the cursor and marks where the sun stands. Opus numbers count upward from 0231 and each number always yields the same window. Typography: minimal, inscription-like: tiny carved-looking capitals with wide tracking in pale stone colour at the base of the wall: "ROSE WINDOW · ORDER XII · OPUS 0231" updating with each window's order and number, in a classical serif (Trajan-like fallback: Georgia/"Times New Roman" caps). A designed hint, "move the sun · click for a new window", cut into a floor slab and fading after use. Reduced motion: no mote drift; new windows appear with a simple fade. Performance: render glass to an offscreen canvas once per window; per frame only composite lighting and projection.

Open Tangram Menagerie ↗
031

Tangram Menagerie

A bright, paper-coloured tangram table in the manner of a mid-century picture book. The title is drawn letter by letter in chunky geometric strokes so that every counter and arch can hold a small coloured triangle. Seven flat-coloured wooden pieces begin as the classic square, burst apart into a loose scatter, and a pale dotted silhouette draws itself onto the work mat. The viewer drags, turns and flips the pieces until the shape is covered; the seams then glow, the silhouette fades, and the finished figure wakes up while still visibly made of its seven pieces: a swan glides over ripples, a cat blinks and swishes its tail, a rabbit hops, a fish wags through bubbles, a dancer sways, a boat rocks on waves, a house puffs smoke, a bird flaps. A lettered caption reports the time, and a vermilion stamp is pressed into a row of eight.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A tangram puzzle game: the seven classic pieces (two large triangles, one medium triangle, two small triangles, a square, a parallelogram, all cut from one square) and a set of silhouettes to fill. Built in SVG with precise geometry. Concept: seven shapes, a whole zoo; the reward for solving is that the animal comes alive. Mood: playful, bright, mid-century children's-book modern. Palette: warm paper #F7F3E8; pieces in flat colours: vermilion #E4572E, turquoise #17BEBB, sun yellow #FFC914, leaf green #76B041, plus charcoal-aubergine #2E282A (used for silhouettes, type, and two of the pieces in lighter tints: #5A5257 and #8C8287, and one small triangle in the charcoal itself, so all seven pieces are distinguishable). Light page, flat colour, a faint paper grain, soft short shadows under lifted pieces. Layout: the work mat in the centre holds the target silhouette (solid charcoal at 18% tint with a subtle dotted outline); the seven pieces start in a loose scattered arrangement around or below it (on phones: in a tray beneath, scaled to fit); a puzzle selector on the left as a column of tiny silhouette thumbnails with names; a header with the title in big, chunky, friendly geometric type (Futura/"Century Gothic"/"Trebuchet MS", bold, lowercase: "tangram menagerie") where the letter counters are filled with little triangles. Puzzles (at least eight, each defined by exact piece placements on the classic unit grid so the silhouette is a true tangram union and the solution check is exact): swan, cat, rabbit, fish, dancer, sailing boat, house, bird in flight. Solution detection must be shape-based, not piece-identity-based: compare the covered area with the silhouette (e.g. rasterise/sample points or polygon tests with tolerance), so interchangeable pieces and alternative solutions count; the two orientations of the parallelogram are handled with a flip. Interaction: drag a piece (pointer events with capture; the piece lifts with scale 1.04 and a deeper shadow); rotate in 45° steps by tapping/clicking a selected piece's rotate handle, mouse wheel while dragging, the R key, or a two-finger twist; flip the parallelogram with F or a flip handle. Pieces snap magnetically when a vertex or edge comes within a few pixels of the silhouette's valid positions or of other pieces' vertices (snap with a quick spring settle and a tiny "click" squash). Keyboard: Tab cycles pieces, arrows move, R rotates, F flips. A "hint" control shows the outline of one unplaced piece in its place for two seconds (dotted line drawing itself); a "start over" control scatters the pieces with a playful toss. Signature moment: completion. When the silhouette is fully covered, the seams between pieces glow briefly, then the assembled animal animates as a creature while still made of its seven pieces: the swan glides across the mat with its neck piece bobbing and ripple lines beneath; the cat's tail triangle swishes and it blinks a drawn eye; the rabbit hops with ears flicking; the fish swims with tail wagging and bubbles; the boat rocks on drawn waves; the dancer sways with arms waving and notes drifting up; the house pops its roof like a lid, lights its windows and puffs smoke; the bird flaps past speed lines. A hand-lettered style caption appears ("a swan, from seven pieces, in 1 minute 12") and a small stamp is added to a progress row of eight animal stamps. Then "next" slides the pieces apart with a staggered spring and loads the next silhouette. Entrance: the seven pieces begin as the original square in the centre, hold for half a second, then burst apart into the scatter and the first silhouette draws on: a strong first three seconds. Details: a move counter and timer in tiny tabular numerals; the unused-thumbnail silhouettes wiggle on hover; pieces show a subtle grain direction so rotations are legible. Optional sound toggle (off by default): soft wooden clicks on snap and a short pentatonic flourish on completion. Reduced motion: no burst or creature animation; solved state shown with a simple colour flash and caption.

Open The Night Typist ↗
032

The Night Typist

A Halden Portable, Model 5, sits on a lamp-lit wooden desk in 1958. A sheet of warm paper rises out of its black rubber platen and fills the upper part of the view; below it are the grey-green enamel deck with its fan of steel type bars, two ribbon spools, a nickel nameplate, a strike counter, and a bank of round glass-topped keys. The machine types its own first line with a human rhythm, returns the carriage, and then waits with a pulsing red caret at the printing point. From there every key the viewer presses is a physical event: the key sinks, a type bar leaps from the basket, the ribbon jumps, the paper shudders, an imperfect inked letter lands and settles, and the whole carriage steps one space to the left. Nothing can be erased, only struck over. Finished sheets are pulled out and drift onto a fanned pile that can be read again.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A working manual typewriter. The viewer types on their real keyboard (or an on-screen key bank on touch devices) and the machine types onto a sheet of paper with all the physical imperfection of ink and steel: uneven impressions, slightly misaligned letters, the carriage shifting one space at a time, a bell near the margin, a satisfying carriage return. Concept: a writing tool where every character is an event. Mood: nocturnal concentration, a desk lamp, 1958; but a light page: the lamp-lit paper dominates. Palette: paper #EFEADF, typewriter ink black #1C1C1C, ribbon red #A31621 (second ribbon colour), machine body in grey-green enamel #5E6B6B with darker #3F4949 and chrome highlights; desk surface a deep warm brown at the edges only, vignetted by lamp light. Composition: the upper two-thirds shows the paper rising out of the machine's platen (roller); the lower third shows the top of the typewriter: the type basket (a fan of type bars), ribbon spools and vibrator, the paper bail with scale markings, the carriage return lever at left, and below it (on wide screens) the keyboard of round glass-topped keys with nickel rims in QWERTY arrangement. The paper, not the machine, moves: as you type, the carriage (paper + platen) shifts left one character; on return it slides back right and the paper feeds up one line. Typing behaviour: characters are rendered on a canvas in a typewriter face (Courier/"Courier New"/"American Typewriter"/monospace) but degraded by hand: each strike gets random vertical misalignment (±1px), slight rotation, variable ink density (a blotchy alpha mask per glyph: heavier at the edges, occasional filled-in "e" and "a" counters, lighter when keys are struck fast), a faint ribbon-texture, and a ghost double-impression now and then. No deleting: Backspace moves the carriage back one space so you can overstrike (XXXX over a mistake), as on a real machine. Shift-lock, a margin bell 8 characters before the right margin, and a hard stop at the margin (keys jam with a dull thud and a small shake until you return). Enter performs the carriage return with a zip and a line feed. Tab jumps to stops. A ribbon selector lever switches black/red. When the page fills, or on "New sheet", the paper is pulled out upward with a ripping-zip motion and floats to a pile at the right, where finished sheets stack slightly fanned (hover a sheet in the pile to read it enlarged). Signature moment: the strike. For each keypress the corresponding key on the drawn keyboard depresses, a type bar swings up from the basket in an arc to hit the ribbon at the printing point (a fast 70ms animation with a snap-back), the ribbon vibrator jumps, the paper shudders minutely, the letter appears with an ink "settle", and the carriage steps. With sound on, each strike has a distinct synthesised clack (noise burst through resonant filters, pitch varied per key, space bar deeper), the bell is a clear sine ding with decay, the return a ratcheting zip. Sound is off until the viewer flips the designed lamp-switch-style toggle. First three seconds: the machine types the opening by itself with human rhythm: "It was a quiet night, and the page was patient." then a carriage return, then the cursor position waits with a softly pulsing printing-point guide. A tiny engraved plate on the machine reads "Halden Portable · Model 5". A small card leaning against the machine gives the designed hints in typed text: "type. enter returns the carriage. backspace does not forgive." Touch devices: a compact on-screen keyboard styled as the round keys, sized for 360px width, and the paper view above it. Accessibility: typed text is mirrored into a visually hidden live region; the keys are focusable buttons. Reduced motion: no type-bar swing or shake; letters appear with the ink texture only. Additions that belong to the brief as built: the untyped remainder of the sheet stands upright behind the platen and shortens as the typed part feeds up in front of it; the pile starts with three sheets already typed earlier that night (a service card in red and black, a letter, a draft with an XXXXXXXX overstrike); a mechanical five-digit strike counter sits beside the nameplate on wide screens; the chrome carriage return lever is itself a button; a desk-lamp shade leans into the top-left corner on wide screens. Because Tab is a typewriter key here, Tab jumps to stops while the page has focus, Shift+Tab reaches the on-screen controls, and Escape hands the keyboard back to the paper; the hint card says so. Input typed during the opening line is held and typed once the machine has finished.

Open Lava Lamp, 1971 ↗
033

Lava Lamp, 1971

A flat, saturated tangerine living-room wall carries a supergraphic of seven concentric stripes that sweeps in from the left edge and bends down around a single rocket-shaped lava lamp standing on the edge of a teak sideboard. The lamp's spun-aluminium cap and hourglass base frame a tapered glass vessel in which glossy magenta wax lifts off a settled mound, stretches, necks to a thread, pinches in two, cools at the top and sinks again, all in amber liquid with drifting sparkle, a specular streak and a small window reflected on the glass. Fat cream Cooper-style lettering with a stepped aubergine drop-shade sits low on the wall beside it, over a quiet lowercase caption that says "wait for it." until a blob lets go. The page opens cold: the stripes draw themselves, the title springs up, the bulb flickers on, its glow spreads up the wall and the first big blob leaves the mound. The mood is warm, drowsy and unapologetically 1971.

↗ Open

Original prompt

An ambient page: one tall lava lamp glowing in a 1971 living room, its wax rendered with a hand-written WebGL fragment shader as smooth metaballs that rise, neck, pinch off, cool and sink. Concept: hypnotic viscous motion, and a deliberately saturated period palette instead of a dark screen-saver. Mood: groovy, warm, drowsy. Palette: wall field orange #FF5E1F, amber liquid #FFB000, wax magenta-pink #D91E63, deep aubergine #3A0A2A for shadows, type and the lamp's base, and cream #FBE9D0. The page background is a flat saturated orange wall with a huge 70s supergraphic: a band of concentric rounded stripes in amber, magenta, cream and a thin aubergine pinstripe that runs in from the left edge and curves down around the lamp. Saturated light page. The lamp: a classic rocket-shaped silhouette: spun-aluminium conical base and cap (drawn with CSS/SVG gradients, a brushed-metal conic highlight) and a tapered glass vessel between them. The vessel's interior is the WebGL canvas, masked to the glass outline. The lamp stands on a low teak sideboard edge with a soft coloured glow cast on the wall and tabletop that pulses subtly as large blobs pass the bulb. Shader: 10–14 metaballs whose positions are simulated in JavaScript with simple thermal physics (each blob has temperature; heated at the bottom it becomes buoyant and rises, cools near the top, sinks; slight horizontal wander; soft collisions; blobs merge visually by the metaball field) and passed as uniforms. The fragment shader sums the field, uses a smooth threshold for the wax surface, computes a pseudo-normal from the field gradient for glossy lighting (a soft specular highlight, rim light, subsurface glow: wax brighter and more orange-yellow near the hot bottom, deeper magenta at the top), renders the liquid as a vertical amber gradient with faint suspended sparkle, and adds glass effects: a vertical specular streak down the left side, darker refraction at the edges (cylindrical lens: horizontally compress the scene toward the edges), and a reflection of a window on the glass. A mound of settled wax sits at the bottom, bulging before releasing a blob. Interaction: watch-only is complete in itself. Additionally: pressing and holding on the lamp heats it (blobs rise faster and stretch into long necks); dragging left/right tilts the lamp a few degrees (the liquid's "up" changes and blobs slide along the glass, then it rocks back with a damped wobble): the signature moment is a slow blob stretching, necking to a thread and pinching into two, with glassy highlights; a small rotary dimmer knob on the sideboard, drawn in period style, sets the bulb heat from "mellow" to "far out"; a row of three colour chips (enamel buttons) changes the wax/liquid combination to two alternative period schemes (teal wax in lime liquid on a mustard wall; cream wax in red liquid on a magenta wall), with the wall graphic recolouring in a staggered sweep. Typography: fat, tight 70s display lettering for the title, drawn large and low on the wall to the side of the lamp: "Lava Lamp, 1971" in a heavy rounded serif/soft slab stack (Cooper-like: "Cooper Black", Georgia bold, with tight tracking and generous curves) in cream with an aubergine drop-shade; beneath, small text in lowercase sans: "wait for it." and after a minute "there it goes." (the caption changes for a few seconds whenever a blob pinches off in mid-liquid, then returns). Tiny knob legends in tracked caps on a cream enamel control plate fixed to the sideboard front, with a pilot jewel that glows with the bulb. Keyboard: Space heats the focused lamp, arrow keys nudge its tilt and turn the knob. Entrance: the lamp is cold: wax lies at the bottom; the bulb clicks on, the glow spreads up the wall over two seconds and the first big blob lifts off. Fallback without WebGL: an SVG/CSS lamp with blobs made from blurred circles under a contrast filter (the gooey technique) animated by keyframes. Performance: shader over a small canvas only; DPR capped at 2; pause when hidden. Reduced motion: simulation at quarter speed, no tilt wobble.

Open The Museum of Obsolete Sounds ↗
034

The Museum of Obsolete Sounds

A white-cube gallery rendered as a long page: an entrance wall with the museum's name in enormous tight black grotesque under a single blue waveform that dies away into a flat line, then nine exhibit rows, each with a huge outlined catalogue number, a single-weight line drawing standing on a plinth, and a wall label written in a dry curatorial voice. The front face of every plinth is an instrument panel: a round blue button, an oscilloscope and a small spectrogram that show the sound actually being made at that moment. Nothing is recorded; a rotary dial, a modem handshake, a typewriter, a television, a camera, a floppy drive, a cassette, a slide projector and the time pips are all built from oscillators, filtered noise and envelopes, and each drawing performs one exact mechanical movement in step with its sound. The modem is the centrepiece: its plinth panel widens across the whole row and a large spectrogram writes itself out while brackets caption the conversation between the two machines. A mechanical tally counts what has been heard, and the exit wall keeps its last label in storage until all nine have played.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A virtual gallery of sounds the world has stopped making, each exhibit synthesised live in the browser with Web Audio (no samples) and visualised as it plays. Concept: a museum where the artefacts are noises; each plinth holds a drawing of the object, a museum label, and a button that performs its sound. Mood: clinical white-cube gallery, deadpan and affectionate. Palette: gallery white #FAFAF8, black #111111, International Klein-like electric blue #0047FF as the single accent (buttons, waveforms, highlights), and label grey #8C8C8C. Light page. Thin black line illustrations (SVG, single stroke weight, no fills except blue accents; the one exception is the television screen, which goes black for a second on switch-off so the white dot can be seen). Layout: a long vertical gallery; the entrance wall has the museum name set very large in a tight neo-grotesk (Helvetica/Arial bold, sentence case, negative tracking) spanning the width, with "Permanent collection · Gallery 2 · Please touch.", an index of the nine exhibits, and above the name a single blue waveform that decays into a flat line (moving the pointer across it troubles it, silently, like a plucked string). Each exhibit is a wide row on an asymmetric grid: catalogue number huge and light (outlined) at left ("01"), the line drawing of the object on a plinth in the middle, the label at right (title, date range, medium: "sine waves, filtered noise", and 40–70 words of curatorial text), and, forming the front face of the plinth beneath the drawing, an oscilloscope strip that draws the live waveform and a small scrolling spectrogram in blue while the sound plays, with a status line naming the current phase of the sound. At rest the oscilloscope shows the sound's envelope as a thin black plot. On phones the row stacks. Exhibits (9, each a carefully designed synthesis patch with envelopes, and each with a matching animation of its drawing): 01 Rotary telephone dial: wind-up ratchet and the returning pulse clicks, the count depending on which digit the viewer dials on the drawn dial (drag a hole to the finger stop, click a digit, or press a number key; the dial rotates back at constant speed, ten pulses a second, and a partial drag sends only the pulses it earned). 02 Dial-up modem handshake: a recognisable sequence of dial tone, DTMF digits, answer tone with phase reversals, warbling FSK tones, probing tones and chirps, and the wash of scrambled noise, about 10 seconds, with the label listing what each phase is doing and highlighting the current one. 03 Typewriter bell and carriage: keystrokes type a line onto the drawn paper while the carriage advances, the bell rings at the eleventh character, the carriage returns with a zip and a thud. 04 CRT television switching on: the high 15.6 kHz whine fading in (kept quiet), a degauss thump, static hiss; the drawing's screen fills with blue static and shows a shrinking white dot on switch-off. 05 Film camera: shutter click and motor wind, with a frame counter that reaches 36 and then rewinds. 06 Floppy disk drive seek: stepped buzzing head movements and a spin-up whirr, with a switch to make it "play" a rising scale as hobbyists did. 07 Cassette: the clunk of the play key, tape hiss, the wow of a tape starting (a computed tune on a variable-speed loop), fast-forward squeal. 08 Slide projector advancing: clunk-chunk and fan hum, cycling four line-drawn slides, one of them upside down. 09 The telephone time lady: not speech; instead "the pips": six sine pips at 1 kHz, five short and the last one long, synchronised so the long pip lands exactly on the next real ten-second boundary of the viewer's clock; the drawn clock shows the viewer's real time. Signature moment: the modem. While its handshake plays, the oscilloscope strip widens to the full row and opens a large spectrogram that writes itself from left to right along a ten-second time axis (on phones it becomes a horizontal scroll that follows the playhead), with annotated brackets appearing in time with each phase ("it asks", "it answers", "they agree on a speed", "they stop talking to you and start talking to each other"), and the line drawing of the beige modem blinks its LEDs in sync, continuing to flicker silently after the speaker cuts off. Interaction: sound is off until the viewer presses any exhibit's round blue play button or touches a drawing (this is the gesture that creates the AudioContext); a persistent master control in the corner shows a mechanical tally, a designed speaker icon with mute, a volume slider (default moderate) and a small level meter. Only one exhibit plays at a time; pressing again stops it. Each drawing is itself interactive (dial the phone, type on the keys, flip the TV switch, press the shutter, click the drive, press the cassette keys, click the projector for the next slide). Keyboard: buttons focusable, Space/Enter plays. A visitor counter styled as a mechanical tally ("You have heard 3 of 9") and, when all are heard, the exit wall reveals a final line: "Silence, 4′33″ of which is still under copyright elsewhere, is not in the collection." Motion: restrained: label rules draw in from left when a row enters; drawings have one precise mechanical animation each. Reduced motion: oscilloscope shows a static envelope plot; drawings do not animate. Copy: precise and wry, like a great wall label: what made the sound, when it was last common, what replaced it.

Open Totality ↗
035

Totality

A fixed sky above a stubble field outside the invented village of Ellery, where a small crowd, two tripods, a dog and a single ash tree wait for a total eclipse. Scrolling is time: the Moon bites into a pale warm Sun, the blue sky thins to steel and then to twilight indigo, the field drains to silver, shadows harden, and an inset shows the ground under the tree covered in crescent suns. In the last minute shadow bands ripple over the field, the umbra rises from the west, the crescent breaks into Baily's beads along a jagged lunar limb and collapses into a diamond ring with bloom and diffraction spikes. Then the page goes dark: a delicate, asymmetric, shimmering corona unfurls around a black Moon, pink prominences cling to the limb, Venus and stars appear, the horizon glows orange all the way round, rooks fly to roost, the dog howls, and the word TOTALITY breathes across the sky above the quiet line "Two minutes, eleven seconds." Scrolling on plays it all in reverse on the opposite limb until daylight, colour and an ordinary afternoon return and people pack up. A clock, a contact timeline, a thermometer that falls six degrees and a needle light meter keep the facts while sparse second-person annotations keep the feeling.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A scroll-driven total solar eclipse. The viewport is a fixed sky; scrolling moves time from first contact to fourth contact, and at the centre of the scroll the Moon covers the Sun completely. Everything a real eclipse does to the world happens in order: the light thins, the temperature drops, shadows sharpen, the horizon glows like sunset all the way round, stars come out, the corona blooms. Concept: the most dramatic two minutes in nature, operated by the viewer's own hand. Palette (the page travels through it): day sky blue #9CC9EA with pale sun-warm #F7E7B4; mid-eclipse the sky turns steel then deep twilight indigo; totality sky #0A0A12 with corona white #FFFFFF and a horizon band of sunset orange #FF9E5E; prominences in a hot pink-red (#FF4F6D). The page is light at both ends and dark only in the middle. Composition: a full-viewport Canvas (fixed) with the Sun at upper centre (large: diameter about 22% of the shorter viewport side); below, a landscape silhouette: a ridge of low hills, a single tree, a small crowd of spectators with tripods and upturned faces, a field. Text annotations are DOM elements that appear at the side at their moments, each tied to a scroll range. A thin vertical timeline at the right edge with tick marks labelled C1, C2, MAX, C3, C4 and clock times of an invented eclipse ("14:03:22" …) shows progress; a temperature readout and a light-level readout (in percent and as a little drawn light meter) change with the scroll. Sequence (scroll progress 0→1, all continuous, eased, reversible): 0.00–0.35 partial phase: the Moon's disc slides across the Sun from the right (in daylight it shows only as the bite it takes out of the Sun, turning into a black disc as the sky darkens); sky brightness falls slowly then quickly; the landscape colours desaturate toward silver; under the tree, the dappled light becomes hundreds of tiny crescent suns on the ground (an inset "look down" vignette); spectators raise eclipse glasses. 0.35–0.45: last minute: shadow bands ripple across the field as faint moving stripes; a dark wall (the umbra) rises from the western horizon (the viewer faces south, so west is on the right, the side the Moon arrives from); Baily's beads sparkle along the Moon's limb (bright points through lunar valleys, computed from a fixed jagged limb profile), collapsing to the diamond ring: one dazzling point with a thin ring of inner corona: a signature frame with lens-like bloom and diffraction spikes. 0.45–0.55 totality: the corona unfurls: long asymmetrical streamers and fine radial plumes drawn procedurally (noise-modulated rays, brighter and shorter at the poles, long equatorial streamers), slowly shimmering; pink prominences at the limb; the sky is deep indigo with Venus, a few bright stars and planets fading in; the horizon glows orange for 360°; the crowd is silhouetted, a dog howls (in text), birds fly to roost across the sky; the temperature readout has dropped 6 °C; a large quiet caption in the sky: "Two minutes, eleven seconds." 0.55–0.65: second diamond ring on the opposite limb, beads, light floods back from the west. 0.65–1.0: the partial phases in reverse, colour returns, people begin to pack up; final note. Signature moment: the transition into totality and the corona itself: a believable, delicate, shimmering corona with the diamond ring flash just before it. Typography: sparse, large-scale contrasts: annotation text in a refined serif (Georgia/Palatino) at modest size with small-caps time stamps; one monumental word, "TOTALITY", in very wide-tracked thin capitals (a light sans stack: "Helvetica Neue" Light/"Gill Sans" Light/system-ui 200) that fades in across the sky only during totality and whose letterspacing breathes outward; the opening screen says "Scroll slowly. This happened in four minutes." with a designed scroll cue (a tiny sun being eaten). Copy is in second person, concrete and sensory: "The light goes strange before it goes dark: thin, as if the day were running low on battery." Interaction: scroll (wheel, touch, keyboard); a small "play in real time" control auto-scrolls the whole event smoothly over about 90 seconds; hovering the Sun (or tapping it on touch screens) shows the percentage covered; the timeline ticks C1, C2, MAX, C3, C4 are buttons that glide to that contact, and the final note offers "Watch it again". Text colour switches for contrast as the sky darkens. Reduced motion: the canvas updates in discrete stages rather than continuously shimmering; no shadow bands. Performance: one canvas, procedural corona pre-rendered to an offscreen buffer and modulated.

Open Kestrel Frameworks — Bicycles Drawn to Measure ↗
036

Kestrel Frameworks — Bicycles Drawn to Measure

The page is four folded cyanotype sheets from the drawing office of a one-woman frame shop in an invented Calder-valley town. Sheet 1 opens by drafting a fully dimensioned side elevation of a lugged steel touring frame, fork, wheels and a dotted rider under a headline lettered across the whole width of the sheet, with a schedule of dimensions and a pencilled margin note from the builder. Sheet 2 hands the viewer four scale rules; moving any of them recalculates a real frame geometry and the drawing redraws itself tube by tube, numbers rolling and flashing marking-out yellow, construction arcs swinging through the tube ends, the rider leaning further forward or sitting up, the wheels shrinking to 650B for short legs, while every change is logged as a lettered revision that also updates the drawing number in each title block. Sheet 3 shows three circular detail views of the joinery with numbered balloons and the builder's reasons in her own hand. Sheet 4 sets out fourteen weeks of work as a hatched bar chart, plain prices, an apologetic waiting list, and a request form that carries the viewer's figures and ends with a yellow RECEIVED stamp slammed onto the title block. Everything is line work in four colours on blotchy blue paper with fold creases, masked borders and zone marks.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A landing page for an invented one-person bicycle frame builder, "Kestrel Frameworks" of Hebden-like invented town "Marrowdale, Yorkshire", designed entirely as a cyanotype blueprint: white line work on a deep blue field. The centrepiece is a live technical drawing of a steel bicycle frame that re-drafts itself as the viewer changes the rider's measurements. Concept: the brand is the drawing; ordering a bicycle is watching your own geometry appear. Palette: blueprint blue #1B4F9C as the full-page colour field, with a darker fold/shadow blue #0E2F66, line white #F2F6FA, and a single marking-out yellow #FFD23F for dimensions being changed and for the one call-to-action. Texture: cyanotype unevenness (subtle large-scale blotching and paper fibre via SVG turbulence at low opacity), fold creases crossing each sheet, a slightly lighter border where the "print" was masked (with zone letters and numbers in the margin), and a drawn title block. Blue colour-field page (neither dark-mode nor white). Typography: engineering lettering: uppercase, single-stroke feel using a condensed or monospace stack ("Arial Narrow", "DIN Alternate", "Roboto Mono", Menlo, Consolas, monospace) with wide tracking; dimension numerals in tabular mono; one handwritten-style italic serif for the builder's margin notes (Georgia italic) as if pencilled in white. Title block in the lower right of every sheet with boxes: TITLE, DRAWN BY (the invented builder, Wren Achterberg), SCALE 1:5, SHEET n OF 4, DATE (today's real date), DRG No. (whose suffix follows the current revision letter). Structure: Sheet 1 (hero): the side elevation of a road/touring frame and fork with wheels drawn as thin circles with spoke hints, saddle, bars and drivetrain in lighter line weight; centre lines (dash-dot), dimension lines with arrowheads and values lettered A to J (seat tube, top tube effective, head angle, seat angle, chainstay, BB drop, wheelbase, stack and reach), a small rider stick-figure in dotted line, a hatched ground line, and a schedule of dimensions beside the drawing. Headline lettered across the full width of the top: "BICYCLES DRAWN TO MEASURE" and "One builder. One torch. Twenty-two frames a year." Sheet 2: "MEASURE": the configurator. Sheet 3: "JOINERY": three detail drawings in circles (a lugged head tube joint with a kestrel-feather lug cut-out, a fillet-brazed bottom bracket, a dropout) with numbered callouts, a small key showing where each detail sits on the frame, and short notes on why each is done that way. Sheet 4: "THE ORDER OF WORK": a horizontal process timeline drawn as a Gantt-like bar chart over 14 weeks (measure, draw, mitre, braze, align, paint, build, ride) with a pencilled note per stage, then prices in a plain table (frame and fork from £2,400; waiting list currently "about a year, sorry"), and a request form whose submit stamps "RECEIVED — W.A." with the date on the title block (no network). Signature moment: the configurator on Sheet 2. Custom-drawn sliders (thin white rules with a yellow index mark and tick scales, like scale rules) for rider height (150–198 cm), inseam (68–94 cm), reach preference (upright ↔ long and low), and intended use (audax / touring / gravel: changes tyre clearance, chainstay length, angles, fork). As a slider moves, the frame is recalculated with real plausible geometry (seat tube from inseam × 0.65, top tube from trunk length and reach, angles and chainstays by use, head tube from stack and fork length, trail derived, standover clearance checked; if clearance is short the top tube is sloped, and below a 76 cm inseam the wheels change from 700C to 650B) and the drawing re-drafts itself: tube lines chase their new positions with eased interpolation, the changing dimensions flash yellow and their numbers roll, construction arcs sweep where angles and lengths change, and the dotted rider figure adjusts posture. A faint dashed ghost of the first issue (REV A) stays under the live drawing for comparison. A standover check box ticks or unticks itself with a pencilled remark. On first load the whole drawing is drafted in sequence over about 2.5–3 s: centre lines first, then tubes stroke by stroke (stroke-dashoffset), wheels compass-swept, dimension lines last (appearing in yellow and cooling to white); the headline letters are stroked in the same way. The Sheet 2 drawing drafts itself when first scrolled into view. Details: hovering, focusing or tapping any tube names it and its tubing spec in a yellow leader-line callout ("DOWN TUBE — 31.8 mm, 0.8/0.5/0.8 butted, air-hardening steel"); a crosshair with X/Y coordinates in millimetres from the bottom bracket follows the mouse over the drawing; hovering a row of the schedule highlights its dimension on the drawing; a "revisions" table that logs each change the viewer makes ("REV C — seat tube +12 mm", with the cause pencilled beneath, revision letters skipping I, O and Q); a small drawn kestrel hovering as the logo, wings flickering as kestrels do when the page is idle. Buttons are drawn as stamped boxes; focus states are yellow dashed outlines. Responsive: drawing scales to width with line weights and lettering held at a constant visual size; on phones the drawing sits above the copy on Sheet 1 and stays pinned to the top of Sheet 2 while the sliders stack under it. Reduced motion: drawing appears complete; changes apply instantly with a yellow highlight; the kestrel holds still.

Open Pendulum Wave ↗
037

Pendulum Wave

A museum-cabinet instrument on sunlit plaster: a dark walnut frame carries a row of brass bobs on fine strings, their lengths cut so that each makes one more swing per minute than its neighbour. A hinged release bar holds them to one side, lifts, and the row leaves in unison, then slides into a travelling wave, tangles, sorts itself into four, three and two interleaved groups, and at exactly sixty seconds sweeps back into one line while a brass ring spreads from the frame and the dial glows. Beneath the frame the same row is seen from above as a single undulating line with a fading wake; at the side a brass-bezelled sixty-second dial names the pattern of the moment and states the arithmetic behind it; along the bottom a brass rule lets the viewer slide to any instant. Everything is computed from the closed-form angle, so any moment can be visited exactly and the minute repeats forever without drift.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A physically exact simulation of the classic pendulum-wave demonstration: fifteen pendulums of carefully graded lengths hang in a row from a wooden frame; released together they drift out of phase into travelling waves, apparent chaos, then split into two and three interleaved groups, and after exactly sixty seconds swing back into perfect unison. Each bob chimes softly as it passes the centre. Concept: order, disorder and return, produced by nothing but arithmetic. Mood: calm, precise, a physics cabinet in a sunlit hall. Palette: plaster cream #F2ECDF background, dark walnut/iron charcoal #2A2622 for frame and type, brass #B8893A for the bobs (with highlight #E3C27A and shadow #7A5A22), and cool slate blue #6B7F8E for shadows, traces and secondary type. Light page. Physics: pendulum i (i = 0…14) completes (51 + i) oscillations in 60 seconds, so its period is 60 / (51 + i) s and its length is g·(T/2π)²: draw the lengths true to scale relative to one another. Angle θ_i(t) = θ0 · cos(2π t / T_i), exact and analytic, so the 60-second return is perfect and the page can run forever without drift; time derives from a clock that can be paused and scrubbed. Rendering (Canvas 2D, crisp at high DPI): two coordinated views of the same system. (1) Front view, large, drawn in a shallow oblique projection so the swing towards and away from the viewer reads as a sideways sweep with correct depth ordering: the frame's top beam with fifteen suspension points, a centimetre scale engraved on the right post, fine strings, brass spherical bobs with a specular highlight and soft contact-less shadows cast on the back wall (offset, blurred, slate), the bobs' lengths forming a graceful curve; motion blur trails behind fast bobs. (2) Top/side "wave" view beneath: looking down the row so the bobs' displacements read as a single undulating line of dots, with a thin spline through them and a fading history, which is where the wave patterns become obvious. A circular 60-second dial at the side shows the cycle with engraved marks at the famous moments: 0 unison, 15 s, 20 s (three groups), 30 s (two groups in antiphase), 40 s, 45 s, 60 s unison; a hand sweeps it, and the current pattern is named in small type ("unison", "travelling wave", "five groups", "four groups", "three groups", "alternating", "apparent chaos", "returning"), together with the live fraction of a swing by which each bob leads its neighbour (t/60); the engraved marks are also buttons that jump the clock to that moment. Sound (off until the viewer turns it on with a designed brass toggle): each pendulum is assigned a note of a pentatonic scale across two octaves (longest = lowest); each time a bob crosses the centre line a soft struck-bar tone plays (sine + a little inharmonic partial, 1.2 s exponential decay, slight stereo pan by position). The result: unison chords at the start, cascading arpeggios during the waves, rhythmic alternation at 30 s, a great chord on the return. Signature moment: the return at 60 seconds: all fifteen bobs sweep back into a single line, the chord lands, the dial flashes softly, and a thin brass ring pulses outward from the frame; then it begins again. The first three seconds: the bobs are held to one side by a drawn release bar (a hinged iron rod with brass knobs across the strings), which swings up and over the beam on load, and they begin in perfect unison. Interaction: play/pause; a scrubber along the bottom styled as a brass rule from 0 to 60 s that the viewer can drag to any moment (the system jumps there exactly); speed ×½, ×1, ×2, ×4; drag the release bar back down onto the strings (or press R, or the "release again" key) to gather the bobs and reset; while one bob is soloed only its chime sounds; a control for number of pendulums (9, 15, 21) which rebuilds the frame; click any bob to solo-highlight it and see its period and length in a small engraved tooltip ("No. 8 · 58 swings per minute · T 1.034 s · 26.6 cm"; pendulums are numbered from 1, longest first, and each suspension point on the beam is also a small focusable button). Keyboard: space, arrows scrub. Typography: restrained scientific-instrument style: title in small tracked serif capitals (Baskerville/Georgia) on a brass plaque shape; captions in italic serif; numerals tabular. A short paragraph in a narrow column explains the trick in three sentences. Responsive: on phones the front view fills the width (strings shorter, bobs smaller), wave view beneath, dial reduced to a slim arc. Reduced motion: runs at ×½ with no blur trails and no pulse; the scrubber remains.

Open Instant Film ↗
038

Instant Film

A sunlit blush tabletop with a cream toy-like instant camera called the dawdle, its teal neck strap looped off to the side, a small box of film, and eight dashed outlines waiting for pictures. Pressing the red shutter fires a white flash, the iris blinks, the camera kicks, and a square print is pushed out of the slot in motor steps, drops free and glides to its numbered place at a slight tilt. There it stays milky grey-green and then, over twelve seconds, shadows surface first, warm tones follow, and finally a handwritten caption appears on the border. Each of the eight frames is a tiny flat illustration in a faded-film palette: a striped parasol, a cat's back at a window, a boiled egg with steaming coffee, a caravan under pines, a bicycle, a lighthouse with a breathing beam, a cake with flickering candles, two feet at the end of a towel. The counter runs from 8 to 0, the shutter goes grey under an "empty" tag, and a sticker on the film box loads a fresh pack. There is not a line of script on the page.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A CSS-only (no JavaScript whatsoever) instant camera that takes pictures. Pressing the shutter makes the camera flash, whirr and eject a square white-bordered print, which then slowly develops from milky blank to a finished photograph, each photograph being a little landscape or still life illustrated entirely in CSS. Eight frames in the pack. Concept: the patience of instant film, built from checkboxes. Mood: sweet, nostalgic, sunlit, pastel. Palette: blush pink page #F7D6D0, camera body cream #FDF6EC, charcoal-plum #2D2A32 for lens, type and details, soft teal #7FC8C2, apricot #F4A259; photographs may also use a faded-film set derived from these (washed sky #BFE3E0, sun-bleached yellow #F9E2A6, dusty rose #E9A6A1, deep shadow #4A4350). Light, pastel; flat shapes with soft long shadows. The camera (pure CSS: divs, gradients, border-radius, box-shadow, clip-path, transforms): a boxy, friendly, original instant camera seen from the front: cream body with a pastel rainbow-less stripe motif replaced by a simple teal-and-apricot double band (avoid imitating any real brand's markings), a large lens with nested rings and a glass reflection, a viewfinder window, a flash unit with ribbed diffuser, a red-apricot shutter button on the right shoulder, an exposure dial (lighten/darken), a film counter window showing remaining shots, and the wide ejection slot at the bottom front. A woven neck strap loops off to the side. Mechanics (signature moment), built with a stack of eight invisible checkboxes, one per frame, whose labels are stacked over the shutter button so that each press advances to the next state (z-index order plus :has(:checked) selectors hide the spent label and arm the next one about two seconds later, once the motor has finished; the checkboxes belong to an empty form through the form attribute): on press: the shutter button depresses, the flash fires (a full-page white flash that decays in 500ms and a brief bright bloom on the flash unit), the lens iris blinks, the camera gives a small recoil, a handwritten "whirrr…" trembles beside the slot, then after a beat the print slides out of the slot with stepped motor-like motion (steps() easing), drops free, and glides to its place in a growing scatter of prints on the tabletop beside or below the camera, each landing at a different slight rotation on a numbered dashed outline that marks its place. Then it develops: over about 12 seconds the image area goes from pale grey-green to the finished picture using animated opacity, filter (contrast/saturate/sepia) and a milky overlay fading, with colours emerging in order (first shadows, then warm tones); the handwritten caption is written in near the end. The film counter decrements (8 → 0) using CSS counters or per-state content. After the eighth frame a tag over the greyed shutter button reads "empty" and a round "load new film" sticker (a styled reset input) pops onto the little film box that sits on the table, clearing the pack, the picked-up prints and the dial. The eight photographs (each a small CSS illustration in the faded palette, 1:1): a seaside horizon with a striped parasol; a cat on a windowsill; a breakfast table with a boiled egg and a coffee cup, steam animated; a caravan under pines; a bicycle against a wall; a lighthouse; a birthday cake with three candles whose flames flicker; a pair of feet at the end of a towel facing the sea. Each print has a handwritten-style caption on its white border in an italic script-like stack ("Brush Script MT", "Segoe Script", "Bradley Hand", cursive fallback): "Aug. — the last good day", "she ignored us for an hour", "7:40, nobody else up", "plot 14, under the pines", "borrowed. returned, mostly.", "we never did go up it", "three! already", "do not disturb". Interaction without JS: hovering/focusing a print lifts it, straightens it and enlarges it (so the developed picture can be admired; tapping or clicking its label toggles the same zoom via a checkbox, and on phones the print grows to nearly the full width); the exposure dial is a pair of radio inputs (dark / light) that turn the knob and cool or warm all pictures with a filter; hovering the lens shows a tiny reflection sweep. All inputs keyboard-reachable, labelled, with visible focus rings in charcoal. Typography: the title "Instant Film" in a rounded, heavy, friendly display stack ("Arial Rounded MT Bold", "Trebuchet MS", system-ui, bold) in charcoal-plum, large, tucked at an angle, with a small script line "a pack of eight, in no hurry" and a handwritten "press here" arrow by the shutter until the first picture is taken; the invented brand is "dawdle"; a tiny instruction engraved near the shutter: "press. wait. don't shake it." Small-caps details on the body ("FOCUS 0.6 m – ∞"). Layout: camera left / prints right on desktop; camera top / prints below on phones, everything scaling with clamp() so it fits 360px with no overflow. Reduced motion: no flash or recoil; prints appear already developed in place. Entrance: the camera drops onto the table with a soft bounce and the strap settles. Window light lies across the blush tabletop in two soft diagonal bars, and a short note beside the film box explains the film in the brand's voice.

Open ARES-HORT Console — Greenhouse B ↗
039

ARES-HORT Console — Greenhouse B

A dark room, a single moulded terminal, and an amber tube that warms from a bright line into a full raster, counts its memory, draws its own banner and then builds a greenhouse dashboard frame by frame. Everything on the glass is a character in an exact 80-column grid: bars, sparklines, a plumbing diagram with arrows creeping through the pipes, a solar array that silts up cell by cell, and six planter beds whose potatoes, wheat, lettuce, soy, radishes and one sunflower called Gerald sprout, sway, droop when thirsty and ripen while you watch. The station runs itself with a dry sense of humour until a dust storm rolls in, the screen dims, an orange alarm flashes and the machine starts switching off lamps, Gerald's first; the viewer, standing in for Dr. Imani Okoro-Lindqvist on sol 412 of a solitary posting, decides what stays lit. The log alternates terse system lines with her notes, and the wall beside the terminal quietly holds 412 tally marks.

↗ Open

Original prompt

The operations console of a fictional Mars greenhouse, displayed on an amber monochrome CRT terminal from an imagined 1980s-style spacefaring future. A dense character-cell dashboard: everything is text, box-drawing characters and block-element graphics on an 80-column grid, glowing amber. Clearly fictional telemetry simulated in the page. Concept: a dashboard as a vintage computer interface, where the plants themselves are drawn in characters and visibly grow. Mood: lonely, competent, dryly funny; sol 412 of a long posting. Palette: tube black-brown #0A0600, amber phosphor #FFB000, hot highlight orange #FF6A00 for alarms and the cursor, dim amber #5A3A00 for inactive text and grid. Dark page (required by the concept), a single hue family. CRT treatment: the screen is a rounded-corner tube inside a moulded beige-less dark bezel with a small power LED and embossed plate "HORTICULTURAL SYSTEMS TERMINAL HST-9". Text glows (text-shadow bloom in amber), scanlines (repeating gradient overlay), a slow rolling refresh band, slight barrel curvature suggested with a vignette and inner shadow, subtle flicker and phosphor persistence on changing numbers (old value ghosts briefly). Render with DOM text in a monospace stack (Menlo/Consolas/"Courier New") sized so that exactly 80 columns by 39 rows fit the tube on desktop; on phones the layout switches to a 40-column variant with the panels paged (tabs ATMOS / POWER / WATER / BEDS / LOG, also swipeable) and the six beds stacked three by two. On wide screens the dark room around the terminal carries 412 faint tally marks scratched on the wall, one per sol, the two newest brighter. Screen layout (panels drawn with box characters ┌─┐│└┘ and titled like [ ATMOSPHERE ]): top status line: "ARES-HORT v4.2 GREENHOUSE B SOL 412 LMST 14:27:09" with a ticking Mars-time clock (a sol is 24 h 39 m 35 s; minutes and seconds are derived from the real clock through Coordinated Mars Time and tick in Mars seconds, with the site's hour offset chosen so a session opens in the early afternoon), dust storm index with sparkline, and link status ("EARTH LINK: 11 MIN 42 S LIGHT DELAY"). Panels: ATMOSPHERE (O2 %, CO2 ppm, pressure kPa, humidity, each with a horizontal bar made of block characters and a sparkline of ▁▂▃▄▅▆▇█); POWER (solar array output falling as dust accumulates on a row of array cells, battery bank, load and net, a tiny day curve with the current hour marked); WATER LOOP (reservoir, condensate recovery, a pipe diagram with moving characters showing flow, bed numbers lighting while irrigated); BEDS 1–6: the signature panel: each bed shows a lamp bar, its crop growing as ASCII/Unicode character art (potato vines with tubers appearing in the soil row, dwarf wheat as rising stalks of │ with ψ heads, lettuce rosettes, soy, radish with bulbs swelling in the soil, a single experimental sunflower nicknamed "GERALD" whose head turns), a soil row whose texture shows moisture, moisture %, growth stage and cultivar name; crops advance through eight growth stages over time and droop if under-watered (valve 4 sticks, so bed 4 needs manual watering) or cold; LOG: a scrolling event log in the operator's voice mixed with system messages ("14:02 BED 3 MOISTURE LOW — irrigating 40 s", "13:40 Operator note: Gerald has turned to face the lamp again. Good lad."). Below: three lines of console output, a command line with a blinking orange block cursor, and the function-key row. Interaction: (1) Keyboard command line with real commands: HELP, STATUS, WATER <bed>, LIGHTS <0–100>, VENT, HARVEST <bed>, LOG, SCRUB (clean the solar panels with a brush sweeping the array cells and power recovering), NAME <bed> <name>, PWR (power allocation window), ACK, MUSIC (see below), plus a few unlisted words; output is typed out character by character, and HELP, STATUS, LOG and PWR open character-drawn pop-up windows over a dimmed screen. HARVEST 6 is refused: Gerald is under operator lockout. (2) Everything is also clickable/tappable: panels and beds are buttons (visible focus as inverse-video), and a row of function keys F1–F9 along the bottom (HELP / WATER / LIGHT / VENT / SCRUB / HARVEST / LOG / PWR / SND) works by touch and by the real F-keys, so the page is fully usable on a phone without a keyboard. Tapping a bed selects it; F2 and F6 act on the selection. (3) Events: a dust storm arrives every few minutes (the first about fifty seconds in): the storm index climbs, solar output drops, the console switches to power-saving (dims, sheds bed lamps one by one starting with Gerald's "ornamental" lamp just before he blooms), an alarm line flashes in orange inverse video until acknowledged; the viewer must decide in the PWR window what to keep powered (lamps, heater, pump, Earth link; life support is locked on) against a draining battery, with a brownout lock if it falls to 12 %. After the storm the array is filthy and SCRUB is needed. Signature moment: the boot sequence and the plants. On load the tube warms up (a bright horizontal line expands to a full raster), a memory test counts, the block-letter system banner draws line by line, panels pop in one at a time with their frames drawn character by character behind an orange head (all within about three seconds, skippable with any key or tap), and then the beds' plants sprout from seed to their saved stage, sway by a character and keep growing before the viewer's eyes; HARVEST collapses a plant into a yield count with a little cascade of falling characters, and replanting starts a seedling "." that becomes "," then "ı" and so on. Optional sound, off by default (toggle is the F-key "SND"): keyboard clicks and soft beeps. The MUSIC command plays a short square-wave tune ("Daisy Bell") only if sound is enabled. Reduced motion: no flicker, roll band, boot or typing animation, no sway or cascade; text appears immediately. Copy: laconic, technically plausible, with a human thread about the lone botanist, Dr. Imani Okoro-Lindqvist.

040

The Harbour Courier — Tuesday, 9 March 1926

A complete broadsheet front page for an invented port city, printed in ink black on yellowing newsprint: a blackletter nameplate with an engraved harbour vignette, weather and price ears, a ruled dateline, and seven columns of period journalism about a great fog, a new electric tramway, an escaped circus sea-lion, a police court, a serial story, shipping, sport and small advertisements whose details quietly refer to one another. On arrival a press cylinder's shadow rolls down the sheet and the ink lands block by block; the three press photographs are true halftones that can be examined dot by dot under a brass loupe. The paper carries grain, a centre fold, uneven impression and the ghost of page two showing through, and it reflows to three columns on tablets and a dense single-column "late extra" on phones.

↗ Open

Original prompt

The front page of a broadsheet newspaper from an invented port city, "Saint Elmo's Bay", dated Tuesday 9 March 1926, typeset with the density and craft of real letterpress newspapers: nameplate, ears, multi-deck headlines, column rules, justified text, cross-heads, small ads, a weather box, shipping intelligence, and procedurally generated halftone "photographs". Every story is invented, internally consistent and written in period voice. Concept: an editorial page that is all typography and ink; nothing but black on newsprint. Mood: bustling, civic, faintly comic, convincingly old. Palette: newsprint #EDE6D6 (with subtle yellowing toward the edges and a fold line across the middle), ink black #151515, and a worn grey #6B6B6B for halftones and lighter rules. The printed sheet is strictly black and white; the only colour anywhere is the brass rim of the loupe laid upon it. Texture: paper grain, slight ink spread, occasional uneven impression (very subtle per-column opacity variation), show-through of reversed type from the other side at ~3% opacity. Typography (system fonts only, used with skill): nameplate "The Harbour Courier" in a blackletter-flavoured treatment drawn as SVG lettering or, failing that, a heavily styled old-style serif with ornamental rules, an engraved harbour vignette (lighthouse, steamer, gulls) in SVG between the words, ears either side (weather: "Fresh to strong S.W. winds; showers; glass falling" and price: "ONE PENNY"), a dateline rule with "No. 14,902 · ESTABLISHED 1841 · LATE CITY EDITION". Headlines in condensed bold capitals with multiple decks decreasing in size and alternating caps/italic, separated by short centred dashes; body in a tight book serif (Georgia/"Times New Roman") at small size, fully justified with hyphenation enabled, first paragraph in slightly larger type, first words in caps; column rules as hairlines. Layout: seven columns on desktop in a true broadsheet grid with stories spanning different column counts, turning to three columns on tablets and a single column on phones (reflowed as a narrow "late extra" edition, still dense and elegant). Content: lead story across three columns: "GREAT FOG HALTS THE PORT — Forty Vessels at Anchor in the Roads — FERRY STRIKES BUOY; NO LIVES LOST — Harbour Master's Remarkable Statement"; second lead: the municipal tramway's new electric line and a ratepayers' protest; a human-interest piece about an escaped circus sea-lion seen at the fish market; "SHIPPING INTELLIGENCE" as a tight tabular list of arrivals and sailings with invented vessels, tonnages and cargoes; "THE WEATHER" box with a tiny isobar chart drawn in SVG; a serial fiction instalment ("The Lamp at Sorrel Point — Chapter XIV"); cricket and football results from invented clubs; a court report; a column of classified advertisements with period phrasing (lost umbrellas, a parrot that speaks Portuguese, piano lessons), and two two-column display advertisements with engraved-style SVG illustrations ("Dr. Fenwick's Marine Tonic", "Hallam's Waterproofs — For Men Who Go Down to the Sea"), plus small boxed house notices that square off the columns. Roughly 2,400 words of genuinely readable copy (both editions included), cross-referenced between stories, court report and small ads. Halftone photographs (signature moment): two or three "press photographs" generated on canvas: a procedural grey-scale scene (a steamer looming in fog beside a quay with cranes; a crowd of hats at the tram depot; the sea-lion on a fish slab) rendered through a real halftone screen: a 45° grid of black dots whose radius encodes tone, with slight dot gain and misalignment. On load the page is "printed": a press cylinder shadow sweeps down the sheet and ink arrives column by column over ~2 seconds. Hovering or tapping a photograph brings a brass-rimmed loupe that magnifies the halftone dots under the cursor (drawn at 4×), revealing the screen; the caption sits beneath in italic with a credit ("Courier photograph"). Interaction: an "editions" control styled as a small boxed notice ("STOP PRESS — pull the 4 a.m. Extra") that reprints the page with a press-run animation and changes the lead headline and decks ("FOG LIFTING; PORT AWAKES"), the dateline edition label, the fog story's latest paragraph, the shipping note and a late-news fudge box in the margin ("FOG LIFTING — pilot cutter reports the Roads clearing"); clicking any headline folds the paper to enlarge that story for comfortable reading and back; hovering small ads underlines them like a pencil mark and one can be "circled" in pencil on click. Keyboard accessible with focus outlines styled as pencil boxes. Reduced motion: no press sweep; edition changes cross-fade.

Jelly Alphabet

A hot-pink colour field with an oversized halftone and a glossy plate, onto which the word WOBBLE drops letter by letter as soon as the page opens: fat, translucent lemon, lime, blue-raspberry and orange jellies that squash to two-thirds of their height, bulge into each other, rebound and keep quivering. Every glyph is a hand-drawn chunky outline turned into a real soft body, so everything the viewer types, drags, pokes or flings behaves like gelatine: highlights stretch as the shape deforms, bubbles ride along inside, coloured light pools on the plate underneath. The page is a toy with almost no chrome: a tiny label, four jelly-cube flavour swatches, three round grape buttons, a candy-wrapper text field and a row of wrapped sweets that drop sample words.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A toy: big wobbly letters made of jelly. Whatever the viewer types appears as glossy, translucent soft-body glyphs that drop onto a plate-like floor, jiggle, squash against each other, and can be poked, stretched and flung. Canvas 2D with real soft-body physics. Concept: typography you want to prod. Mood: gleeful, sticky, loud. Palette: hot pink field #FF2E7E as the full background, lemon jelly #FFE14D as the main letter colour, deep grape #2B1055 for type, shadows and outlines, white #FFFFFF for highlights; additional jelly flavours that letters cycle through: lime #7CFF6B, blue raspberry #3DA5FF, orange #FF8A1F, each semi-translucent so overlapping letters mix. Saturated colour-field page, flat background with a subtle huge halftone dot pattern in a slightly darker pink (dots swell toward the upper right). The floor is drawn as a wide, shallow glossy plate in lighter pinks with a white rim highlight and a grape shadow; on tall narrow screens there are two plates stacked (a shelf and a floor) so a word breaks into two rows of big letters, and letters hop between plates when the word reflows. Glyph construction: a custom outline font defined in the page for A–Z, 0–9 and a few punctuation marks (! ? . - +): chunky rounded shapes (fat inflated letterforms with no thin strokes; counters in A, B, D, O, P, Q, R, 0, 4, 6, 8, 9 as holes), each defined as closed polygon outlines with rounded corners, sampled into roughly 30–60 boundary points. Each letter becomes a soft body: boundary point masses connected by structural and bend springs to neighbours, plus cross-bracing springs across the stroke and shape-matching toward the rest pose (best-fit rotation blended with an area-preserving linear fit, so a squashed letter bulges sideways) so it holds its form but wobbles like gelatine; verlet integration at 120 Hz with viscous internal damping; collisions with the floor, walls and other letters (point-vs-polygon push-out) with sticky friction. Deeply tangled letters ooze through each other rather than knotting. Untouched letters gently slide and hop to their place in the word and stay upright; once the viewer drags a letter it is free to lie wherever it lands. Rendering: smooth the boundary with Catmull-Rom curves; fill with a vertical gradient of the flavour colour at ~85% opacity; an inner darker rim (stroke in a deeper tone, clipped inside) and a thin grape skin line, a broad soft specular highlight band along the upper-left that is rebuilt from the live outline so it deforms with the shape, a band of pooled light on the lower-right, three small bright glints, a few tiny bubbles suspended inside, and a soft coloured caustic glow on the plate (the pink beneath a jelly glows lighter, as light through gelatine does) plus a grape contact shadow. When a letter squashes, the highlight stretches: this sells the material. Interaction: type on the keyboard: each key drops its letter from the top at the next position with a little spin; letters land, splat, rebound and settle into a quivering word, shrinking smoothly as the word gets longer. A text field styled as a white candy-wrapper strip (zigzag ends, pink stripes) opens the system keyboard on touch devices, plus a row of big "sample word" candies (squish, yum!, gloop, boing, jelly, blop?, 2sweet, wobble). Drag any letter with pointer/finger: it stretches from the grab point like taffy and springs back (pull too far and it slips off the finger); fling it and it sails and splats against the walls, flattening and recovering. Poke (click) sends a jiggle wave through it; letters also flinch when the cursor brushes them, and poking the empty table nudges nearby letters. A "wobble the table" control (or the Space bar) gives everything a jolt. Backspace removes the last letter with a pop into droplets that fall and fade; Escape/Delete pops everything. Capacity capped at 14 letters; the oldest is slurped away off the top of the screen when exceeded. Signature moment: the landing: a word of fat letters dropping in sequence, each squashing to about two-thirds height, bulging sideways into its neighbours, and jiggling in a decaying wave while highlights slide over them. On load the page performs it unprompted: the word "WOBBLE" drops in letter by letter within the first two seconds and keeps quivering; then a designed hint appears in small type: "type something. then poke it." Typography (DOM): minimal; a tiny label top-left in bold grape lowercase rounded sans ("Arial Rounded MT Bold"/"Trebuchet MS"/system-ui): "jelly alphabet" with a flavour picker beside it drawn as four jelly-cube swatches (pressing one turns every letter that flavour, pressing it again returns to the mixed bag in which lemon dominates), and at top-right a gravity toggle drawn as an arrow (normal / upside-down / zero-g, in which letters float and bump slowly), the wobble-the-table button and a sound toggle (off by default): soft wet "blop" sounds on impacts, pitch by letter size. The hint line doubles as a small status whisper for these controls. Reduced motion: letters appear in place with a single gentle settle and reduced jiggle amplitude. Resize keeps letters within bounds.

Open The Dance of Venus ↗
042

The Dance of Venus

A single sheet from an imagined eighteenth-century astronomical atlas, lit as if by a candle: laid paper with chain lines, grain and foxing, a border of tiny asterisks, and a title cartouche set in mixed capitals, small caps and italic. At its centre sits an embossed plate-mark holding a graduated degree circle, an engraved radiant Sun, and two small planets circling at their true relative speeds. Every few days a hair-fine line is ruled between them and left behind; within three seconds the first petal appears, and over eight simulated years the tangle resolves into the five-petalled rose of the 13 : 8 near-resonance. When the cycle closes a red flourish draws itself under the caption, asterisks mark the five conjunction points, and the caption states plainly how far the figure misses closure. Left alone, the next eight years are then ruled slowly in madder red over the first, a hair askew. The viewer can change pace, weave density and view, scrub through time, and swap in five other planetary pairs, each with its ratio and loop count worked out from the same twelve numbers. A short two-column essay insists, calmly, that it is all only division.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A data visualization of orbital resonance: draw a straight line between Earth and Venus every few days as they circle the Sun, and over eight years the lines weave a five-petalled rose. The page draws it live, line by line, in the manner of an eighteenth-century astronomical engraving, and lets the viewer try other pairs of planets. Real, certain data only: sidereal orbital periods in days (Mercury 87.97, Venus 224.70, Earth 365.26, Mars 686.98, Jupiter 4,332.6, Saturn 10,759) and mean orbital radii in AU (0.387, 0.723, 1.000, 1.524, 5.203, 9.537); orbits drawn as circles, which is stated on the page as a simplification. Mood: scholarly wonder, candlelit study. Palette: laid paper #F3EAD7, sepia-black engraving ink #26221C, madder red #9C3D2A for Venus and highlights, Prussian blue #2F5D73 for Earth and secondary lines. Light page with paper texture, a faint plate-mark (the embossed rectangle of an engraving plate) around the figure, and slight ink irregularity. Layout: the main plate is a large circular diagram (Canvas for the accumulating lines, SVG for the instruments around it): the Sun at centre as an engraved face-less radiant disc, the two orbits as fine circles with degree ticks, the two planets as small discs travelling at their true relative angular speeds, the connecting line between them drawn each time step and left behind at low opacity so the envelope accumulates into the rose. Around the plate: a title cartouche at top left in engraved lettering ("The DANCE of VENUS, being the Figure traced by a Line joining the EARTH and VENUS over Eight Years"), a scale of years as a ruler along the bottom with a marker advancing, and "Fig. 1" annotations. Beside or below: a row of small "plates" for other pairs (Earth–Mars, Mercury–Venus, Jupiter–Saturn, Earth–Jupiter, Venus–Mars), each a thumbnail of its finished figure; choosing one swaps it into the main plate. Signature moment: the drawing itself: lines laid down one after another, hundreds of them, the five-fold figure emerging out of what first looks like a tangle; when the eight-year cycle closes (13 Venus orbits ≈ 8 Earth years) the figure completes, the page marks it with a small engraved flourish and the caption notes the near-resonance "13 : 8, the pattern missing closure by about a day in eight years, so each rose is turned a hair from the last" (the figure is computed from the stated periods: 13 × 224.70 against 8 × 365.26 days), and small red asterisks spring up at the places where Venus overtakes the Earth, one to each petal. On load the drawing starts immediately at a brisk pace so the first three seconds already show the first petal forming. Interaction: play/pause; a "begin anew" button; speed dial (engraved knob with seven detents) from "ten years per second" (chosen automatically for the long outer-planet cycles) and "one year per second" (the default) down through a season, a month, a week and a day per second to "as the heavens go" (true real time, with the readout counting hours, minutes and seconds); a slider for line interval (every 1–20 days: denser or sparser weave); a toggle between three views of the same motion: "lines" (the chords), "path" (Venus's geocentric path: its position relative to a fixed Earth, which traces the same five loops with retrograde curls), and "midpoints" (the locus of the chord midpoints); hover/touch the plate to scrub time back along the ruler and see the planets at that date with a readout ("Year 3, day 112 · Venus 47° ahead"); the ruler itself is a draggable, keyboard-operable index that sets the date in earnest. In the path and midpoints views the interval slider sets the spacing of pricked points along the curve. For other pairs, the page computes the ratio of periods and states the nearest simple fraction (the first continued-fraction convergent within 1.25 per cent) and the symmetry to expect ("Earth : Mars ≈ 15 : 8 … seven loops"), and on completion how many days the figure misses closure by. A sixth small plate returns to Earth–Venus, and a small table lists the twelve numbers used. Typography: engraved-book style: title in mixed roman capitals, small caps and italic at varying sizes within one sentence as on old title pages (Baskerville/"Hoefler Text"/Georgia), long-s-free but with ligature-like flourishes drawn as SVG swashes; numerals old-style; captions in italic; all labels curved along orbits with SVG textPath. A short explanatory text in two narrow justified columns, drop cap included, about 180 words, precise and unmystical: this is arithmetic, and the page says so, while acknowledging its beauty. Details: zodiac-free; small compass rose and a decorative border of stars drawn as tiny asterisks; when idle after completion, the next cycle slowly redraws in red over the old one, turned by its true small offset; a faint candle-flicker vignette lies over the page. Line rendering uses very low alpha with multiply-like accumulation so dense areas darken like cross-hatching. Responsive: plate scales to the viewport width; thumbnails wrap. Reduced motion: the completed figure is shown at once, with the scrubber still available.
