Open Lichen on Slate ↗
043

Lichen on Slate

A weathered slab of slate lies on dark ground, lit from the upper left, with a hairline crack, stepped cleavage and two rust veins. Three lichen colonies are already growing on it when the page opens: a mustard crust branching into coral lobes, a sage shield lichen unrolling broad leafy lobes, and a chalk-white crust budding at its edge. They grow slowly but visibly, with pale young margins, raised relief and soft shadows; their centres age, darken, crack and break out in tiny dark-rimmed fruiting discs. When colonies meet they stop and hold a thin black frontier, and over a century the slab turns into a map of territories. A pinned field card in a typewriter face and italic hand records the year and each species' share of the stone; a specimen tray offers four species to sow; a brass hand lens follows the pointer and magnifies the crust two and a half times. The mood is quiet, damp and northern.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A generative piece in which lichens colonise a slab of stone in real time, driven by a Gray-Scott reaction–diffusion simulation running in WebGL 2 (ping-pong float framebuffers, hand-written shaders), then shaded so the chemical field reads as living crust: lobed, ringed, slightly raised, with pale growing margins. Concept: slow life; mathematics that looks exactly like something you have seen on a churchyard wall. Mood: patient, damp, northern; quietly beautiful. Palette: slate grey #3A3F44 with darker cleavage #24282C and a few rusty mineral veins; lichen colours: mustard-ochre #C9B458 (a Xanthoria-like crust), sage grey-green #9DB5A0 (a leafy foliose type), chalk white #E7E3D4 (crustose patches with black dots), and a rare burnt orange #D9793B. Dark-stone page: mid-dark grey rather than black, matte, no glow. The slab is an object lying on darker ground: weathered outline, two chipped corners and a conchoidal notch, with a soft shadow to the lower right. Simulation: two-chemical Gray-Scott on a float texture (RGBA32F, or RGBA16F where full float is not renderable or filterable) at about half screen resolution, capped at 640 on the long side, several iterations per frame. Different feed/kill parameter pairs give different growth habits: branching coral lobes (mustard), broad leafy labyrinthine lobes (grey shield), a crust that buds at its edge (chalk), and a solid crust rippled with concentric rings and pits (ember). Species are implemented by a species-id channel: each seeded colony carries its own parameters, diffusion scale, rate and colour; bare stone is claimed by the strongest neighbouring colony, nothing diffuses across into occupied territory, so colonies grow until they meet and then hold a dark contact line as real lichens do. A fourth channel stores the time each texel was colonised, which gives age for shading. A slowly drifting moisture map (noise, plus the crack) modulates the local reaction rate so growth favours damp areas. Growth is visible but unhurried: at the default speed a colony takes roughly 30–40 seconds to reach palm size, after a brief time-lapse in the first seconds. Shading (display fragment shader): stone first: procedural slate with layered noise, fine parallel cleavage lines, stepped cleavage terraces, a hairline crack running in from the top edge and two rust veins, lit from the upper left with a normal derived from the noise height (baked once per resize into a texture, recomputed procedurally inside the lens so it stays sharp). Lichen on top: thallus colour by species with variation by concentration; pseudo-relief from the gradient of the chemical field (lobes look raised, with a soft shadow on the lower right and a damp halo on the stone); a paler, brighter margin where growth is young; older centres darken, fill in, crack into areoles and develop tiny fruiting bodies (apothecia: small dark-rimmed discs in orange, brown or black, placed by hashed noise where the colony is old enough); concentric growth rings faintly visible from the colonisation time. Where two territories touch, a crisp dark line is drawn. When "rain" is on the stone darkens, colours deepen, a wet sheen appears and small drop rings come and go. Interaction: click/tap the stone to seed a colony of the currently selected species (spores take only on bare stone); drag to smear a line of spores. A small specimen tray at the bottom shows four species as round drawn swatches with invented-but-plausible field names in italics ("mustard wall-crust", "grey shield", "chalk map", "ember rim"); select one to seed. Controls styled as a naturalist's field card: "Rain" (a ticked box: raises moisture, speeds growth, darkens stone, adds sheen), "Years per minute" (a ruler slider, 10–300, default 60), "Scrape clean" (a slate-knife wipe that clears the stone with a scraping sweep from left to right and resets the year), "Fast-forward a century" (runs a century of iterations quickly while the year counter spins and a progress line fills). Hovering shows a hand-lens circle that magnifies the surface 2.5× with a brass rim and handle; on touch the lens floats above the finger; with the keyboard the arrow keys move the lens and Enter sows. Signature moment: the first ten seconds and the collisions: on load three colonies are already seeded and visibly expanding with bright margins, lobes splitting; when two species meet, a crisp dark boundary forms and the pattern becomes a map of territories. Typography: field-notebook restraint: a small ruled card in the corner on off-white (#E7E3D4) with ink #24282C, pinned with a brass drawing-pin: title in a typewriter face (Courier/"American Typewriter") that types itself in, handwritten-style italic notes (Georgia italic): "North wall, slate. Growth: about a millimetre a year. Watching speeds it up." plus a large italic year counter ("Year 37") and coverage percentage per species, and for bare slate, as tiny horizontal bars. The card folds away (and starts folded on phones). Fallback without WebGL 2 or without float render targets: a Canvas 2D reaction–diffusion at low resolution scaled up with the same palette, species rules and controls (no lens). Performance: adaptive iteration count to hold frame rate; canvas pixel count capped; coverage read back asynchronously; pause when hidden; handle resize by re-creating targets and re-seeding. Reduced motion: shows a stone already grown to Year 60 that advances only when the viewer presses "Advance a decade".

Open Ariadne's Thread ↗
044

Ariadne's Thread

The page is the decorated surface of an Attic black-figure drinking cup: a terracotta ground with clay grain, wheel marks, hairline cracks and a chipped rim, black glaze bands carrying the title in spaced, slightly uneven cream capitals, a Greek-key meander above and below, a running-wave band, and two palmettes flanking a round tondo. Inside the tondo is a freshly generated ring labyrinth. A small black silhouette of Theseus steps in at the gate paying out an oxide-red thread that lies along the corridor floors with a little slack; Asterion sleeps in the central chamber under his own name. Reaching him wakes him, the meanders begin to scroll, and the way out is the thread itself, rewound step by step while the Minotaur pathfinds behind, stopping to sniff at junctions. Escape sends the whole thread springing back along the remembered route, pulling taut, lifting off the pot and winding into a ball on the cream roundel beside the title while the vessel makes one slow turn and a cream slip panel delivers the inscription.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A labyrinth game drawn in the style of ancient Greek black-figure pottery: black glaze figures and walls on terracotta, with incised detail lines, meander borders and bands of pattern. The player walks a small figure through a generated maze to its centre and back out, unspooling a red-brown thread behind them; something else is in the maze. Concept: the myth as a mechanic: the thread is the map, the memory and the way home. Palette: terracotta #C8622D as the main ground (the fired clay), black glaze #17110D, buff cream slip #EAD9B8 for highlights and text panels, dark oxide red #7A2E12 for the thread and accents. Surface texture: subtle clay grain, faint horizontal wheel marks, a few hairline cracks and a chipped edge; the black has a slight glossy sheen. Colour-field page, no dark mode look. Composition: the maze is presented as the decorated field of a large vessel: either a circular composition like the interior tondo of a drinking cup (a round maze with the centre as the goal: preferred on square-ish screens) or a frieze panel; framed by a Greek-key meander band above and below, with a band of lotus/palmette or running-wave ornament, all generated as SVG patterns. Walls are thick black lines; the corridor floor is bare terracotta. The player figure is a small black silhouette in profile with incised cream lines (a youth with a short tunic and a ball of thread), animated with a simple two-frame walk and turning to face the direction of travel; the Minotaur is a larger horned silhouette. Game: mazes generated by a depth-first or Wilson's algorithm on a grid (or on concentric rings for the circular version), sized to the screen (small on phones), with a few loops added so there are choices. Phase one: reach the centre, where the Minotaur sleeps; arriving wakes it (a drum-like beat begins if sound is on, the border meander starts to scroll). Phase two: get out. The Minotaur pursues by pathfinding at slightly less than the player's speed, pausing at junctions to sniff; the thread the player laid on the way in shows the way out; walking back along it rewinds it. If the player strays from the thread into unexplored corridors, they must rely on memory. Being caught ends the attempt ("The thread goes slack.", the thread visibly sagging) with the option to try the same maze again or a new one. Three sizes of labyrinth (small / great / Daedalus's own). Fog of memory: corridors not yet visited are drawn with fainter, thinner walls; visited ones stay strong; the outer wall is always full glaze. Movement is screen-directional: each key picks the open passage that best matches its direction on the round plan. Signature moment: the thread: a smooth red line that unspools behind the figure with slight slack and curvature (a rope simulation or a smoothed polyline with a little sag at corners), lying along the corridor floors, wound and unwound precisely as the player retraces; a faint scored line stays in the clay wherever the thread has lain; on escape, the whole thread springs back along the remembered way in, then tightens, straightens, lifts off the maze and winds itself into a ball of thread on a cream roundel beside the title while the whole pot does a slow celebratory turn (the pattern bands scrolling) and an inscription appears. Controls: arrow keys/WASD move cell by cell with smooth eased motion (hold to keep moving, with corner-buffering so turns feel responsive); swipe on touch, plus tapping a corridor ahead moves the figure there along the straight; a designed on-screen four-way control appears on touch devices as a small meander-ornamented disc. Pause with P. Visible focus styling on all buttons. Typography: inscriptions in the manner of vase lettering: spaced, slightly irregular capitals in a classical face (a stack like "Lithos Pro", "Trajan Pro", Optima, Georgia with uppercase and 0.2em tracking), written in cream on black bands or in black along the border; names beside figures as on real vases ("THESEUS", "ASTERION"). Title "ARIADNE'S THREAD" in large tracked capitals on the upper band; small instructions phrased in-world: "Walk in. Wake him. Follow your own thread home." Status band at the bottom: thread paid out in "cubits", time, maze name. Entrance: the pot's bands paint themselves in from left to right like glaze being brushed on (clip-path wipe), the maze walls draw in from the centre outward, then the figure steps in at the gate. The upper band also carries two small vase-painter's inscriptions ("ARIADNE KALE", "DAIDALOS EPOIESEN") on wide screens; palmettes flank the tondo. Optional sound toggle (off by default): soft frame-drum pulse that speeds as the Minotaur nears, a lyre-like pluck on reaching the centre. Reduced motion: no pot rotation or scrolling bands; movement remains but without sag animation.

Open Halden Grotesk — A Type Specimen ↗
045

Halden Grotesk — A Type Specimen

A long-scroll specimen for Halden Grotesk, an invented geometric grotesk from the invented foundry Nordre Type, in which no font file exists: all 35 glyphs are stored in the page as explicit on-curve and off-curve points and drawn from that data. The page is clinical and Scandinavian: specimen-paper off-white, black, and a single cobalt used for construction lines, points and one giant letter, organised on a twelve-column grid with hairline rules and mono section numbers. The cover crops an enormous "Halden" at the viewport edges and assembles it from points, outline and a rising flood of ink. Anatomy pins one glyph out on a metrics grid with every coordinate labelled and every point draggable; because that drawing is the master, damaging the "a" damages every "a" on the page until it is reset. Construction shows the compass work behind O, G and D and an eight-times vertical exaggeration of the overshoot; In use sets a waterfall, a weight row, a poster and ferry-terminal signage from the drawn letters only; Spacing lets the viewer kern a pair by hand until it snaps to the foundry's value; the Colophon carries the designer's note, a licensing joke and the full character grid with its gaps.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A type specimen for a typeface that does not exist as a font file: "Halden Grotesk", from an invented foundry, "Nordre Type, Halden". Every display glyph on the page is drawn as hand-authored SVG path data (a geometric-grotesk design with a few distinctive features), so the specimen can show what font files hide: the bezier skeleton, on-curve and off-curve points, handles, metrics lines and construction circles. Concept: a specimen that x-rays its own letters. Mood: clinical, exacting, Scandinavian; a foundry that numbers its screws. Palette: specimen paper off-white #F4F2EC, cobalt #1238D6 as the single colour (construction lines, points, highlights, one giant letter), black #0B0B0B. Light page, large flat areas, razor-thin rules. The glyph set: design and draw as SVG paths a consistent set: at minimum the capitals H A L D E N G R O T S K, the lowercase a e g n o r s t k d l, and numerals 0–9 would be ideal but at least 1 2 5 8; all sharing stroke weight, x-height, cap height, overshoots and terminals cut at consistent angles. Distinctive features to make it a design, not a default: a single-storey "a" with a flat-cut terminal, a "G" with a spur-less straight jaw, an "R" with a straight diagonal leg, a "t" with a slanted top cut, perfectly circular "O". As built: 12 capitals, 11 lowercase, numerals 0–9, comma and full stop (35 glyphs); curved terminals are cut vertically throughout. Each glyph is stored as explicit points (on-curve and control points) so that the same data renders both the filled glyph and its skeleton overlay. Other text on the page uses a neutral system grotesk (Helvetica/Arial) and a monospace for data. Structure (long scroll, strong grid, each section numbered in mono): 01 Cover: the word "Halden" set enormous, cropped by the viewport edges, black on paper, with a cobalt "Grotesk" small beside it and foundry line "Nordre Type · Specimen No. 4 · 2 weights drawn, 7 promised". 02 "Anatomy": one giant glyph (default "a", selectable from a strip of all glyphs) on a metrics grid with labelled baseline, x-height, cap height, ascender, descender, side bearings, and the outline shown with points (squares for on-curve, circles for off-curve) and handles in cobalt, coordinates in tiny mono beside each point. 03 "Construction": the O, G and D with their construction circles and tangent lines, overshoot exaggerated in a callout ("the O is 12 units taller than the H. It has to be, to look the same."). 04 "In use": a waterfall of sample settings built from the drawn glyphs: words that can be composed from the available letters at several sizes ("HALDEN", "GROTESK", "LONGER NORTHERN SHADE", "stone, salt, strand", "TOLL ROAD ENDS"), a short text line and figures, a nine-weight row (Regular and Bold drawn, seven shown as empty "promised" boxes), a mock poster ("LETTERS DRAGGED ASHORE", cobalt field with a giant black g) and a mock signage panel (ferry terminal wayfinding). 05 "Spacing": a kerning demonstration with two glyphs whose gap the viewer can drag, numeric value updating, and a correct value that snaps with a tick; four pairs to choose from (T o, L T, A T, k e). 06 Colophon: the invented designer Solveig Aamodt's short note on why the "a" has one storey, licensing joke ("Desktop licence: one handshake"), a giant cobalt "a", and the character set grid (undrawn letters shown hatched). Signature moment: glyphs draw themselves from their bones. When the Anatomy section enters (and whenever a new glyph is selected), the points appear first in sequence with coordinate labels ticking, then handles extend, then the outline strokes on along the path direction (stroke-dashoffset), then the fill floods in with a crisp wipe; a toggle switches the whole page between "ink" (filled), "outline" and "x-ray" (skeleton + points over ghost fill), and in x-ray mode every display glyph on the page shows its points. Dragging any point in the Anatomy view actually deforms the glyph live (the path updates), with a "reset drawing" control: the viewer can ruin the "a" and put it back. The Anatomy drawing is the master: every instance of that letter elsewhere on the page (cover included) deforms with it. A selected point can also be nudged with the arrow keys. On load, the cover word assembles the same way in a staggered 1.5 seconds. Interaction details: hovering a glyph in the character grid enlarges it and shows its advance width and point count; keyboard: typing a letter that exists selects it in Anatomy; unavailable letters shake a tiny "not drawn yet" tag in mono. Focus rings in cobalt. Motion: precise, linear-ish, no bounce: cubic-bezier(.4,0,.1,1), 200–700ms; grid lines extend from the margins. Reduced motion: glyphs appear complete; x-ray toggle still works. Responsive: the giant glyph scales to width; point labels thin out on phones.

Open Evening Snow at the Bridge ↗
046

Evening Snow at the Bridge

A single woodblock print hangs on a pale wall and quietly lives. An arched timber bridge runs in a long diagonal from a snowbank and its reeds to a far shore of thatched and tiled houses, a stone lantern and two cinnabar gate posts, under one snow-capped mountain that dissolves into the sky. Everything is cut as a printer would cut it: flat colour blocks with woodgrain showing through, hand-wiped bokashi at the top of the sky and along the water's edge, dark indigo keyblock lines whose width breathes, colour sitting a hair out of register, and snow that is simply unprinted paper. On arrival the sheet is pulled block by block (sky, water, greys, red, key lines), the cartouche settles and the two artist seals are stamped last. Then snow falls in three depths, wave lines drift down the river, a figure in a straw cape under a red umbrella crosses the bridge with a dog or a lantern-bearer behind, chimney smoke curls, crows pass now and then, a roof sheds its load. The hour follows the viewer's clock; pressing the round seal re-inks the sky from the top down, and at dusk the windows and lanterns come on one after another, their light laid on the river as short warm strokes. On phones the scene recomposes as a tall oban sheet; on wide screens it is a diptych.

↗ Open

Original prompt

An ambient scene composed as an original Japanese woodblock print (ukiyo-e / shin-hanga manner: flat colour areas, fine keyblock outlines, bokashi gradation in the sky, visible woodgrain in large flats) that is quietly alive: snow falls, a river moves, a figure with an umbrella crosses a wooden bridge, lanterns come on at dusk. A new composition, not a reproduction of any existing print. Concept: a print that breathes. Mood: hushed, tender, deep winter stillness. Palette: snow-sky grey-blue #DCE3E6, indigo (Prussian-blue ink) #2E4057 for water, night sky top and outlines, cinnabar red #A63A2B for the small accents (a torii-less shrine gate post, the umbrella, the seals), snow white-paper #F6F3EA, and muted slate #6F8696 for distant layers. Light page: the snowy paper tone dominates; the sky darkens to indigo only at the very top by a bokashi gradient. Composition (Canvas 2D or SVG; vertical "ōban" portrait proportion on phones, wider landscape diptych on desktop, shown as two sheets hung edge to edge with a hairline seam): foreground: a snow-laden pine branch entering from the upper left and a bank with reeds poking through snow; middle: an arched wooden bridge on timber piles crossing a river in a long diagonal, its railing topped with snow; three or four thatched and tiled houses on the far bank with deep snow on their roofs, a small shrine lantern, bare willows; background: a soft range of hills and a single mountain as flat grey silhouettes fading into the snow sky. The river is a flat indigo shape with pale wave lines drawn in the woodblock manner and snow-covered boats moored at posts. A print margin surrounds the image, with a vertical title cartouche in the upper right (a red-bordered rectangle with the English title set vertically in small capitals, one letter per line, in two short columns: "EVENING SNOW" / "AT THE BRIDGE") and two artist seals in cinnabar (invented, geometric). Print craft: keyblock lines in dark indigo with slightly varying width (not uniform vector strokes: jitter the offsets or draw as thin filled shapes); flat colour fills with subtle woodgrain (long wavy parallel streaks at low opacity) and paper fibre texture; bokashi: smooth hand-wiped gradients at the top of the sky and along the water's edge; a tiny misregistration between colour and line in places; snow rendered as unprinted paper. Life: snow falls in three depth layers (large slow soft flakes in front, small faster ones behind), drawn as round off-white dots with no blur, drifting with gentle wind; snow accumulates imperceptibly on the bridge rail and slides off a roof once in a while with a soft puff; a figure in a straw cape under a red umbrella crosses the bridge slowly from right to left (a simple, elegant walk cycle of a flat silhouette with bobbing umbrella), occasionally followed by a second traveller with a lantern or a dog; the river's wave lines shift slowly; smoke rises from one chimney as a thin curling line; a pair of crows crosses the sky rarely. Interaction: the pointer/finger is the wind: moving left or right leans the snowfall and makes the willow branches and the walker's cape respond; a click/tap on the pine branch dislodges its snow load, which falls in a clump and bursts; clicking a house lights its window. Time of day follows the viewer's real clock in four moods (morning pale, afternoon grey, dusk with lit lanterns and warmer windows, night with deep indigo sky and bright snow), and a small round control drawn like a seal cycles them manually: the bokashi sky re-inks with a slow wipe and lanterns glow (warm #F2C078 used only for lantern and window light): the signature moment is dusk falling over the print: the sky gradation deepening from the top, windows and lanterns lighting one by one, their glow reflected as short strokes in the river, while the snow keeps falling. Typography: minimal: the vertical cartouche title "EVENING SNOW AT THE BRIDGE"; in the lower margin, tiny tracked small caps: series title "Twelve Views of a Quiet Province · No. 7" and a discreet hint "the wind follows you". Optional sound toggle (off by default) drawn as a small seal: very soft wind and the creak of snow underfoot when the walker passes. Reduced motion: sparse slow snowfall, the walker stands still mid-bridge, no wipes. Entrance: the print is "pulled": colour blocks appear in the order a printer would lay them (sky, water, greys, red, then the keyblock lines) over about 2.5 seconds.

Open Atelier Vesper — Calibre V.03 ↗
047

Atelier Vesper — Calibre V.03

A jeweller's-black landing page whose hero is a working watch. The Heure Bleue 38 lies caseback-up beside a large Didone title: under the sapphire, a ruthenium-dark calibre with rose-gold wheels is actually running, every wheel meshing at its true ratio and the whole train stepping six times a second to the viewer's own local time. In the first three seconds the crown turns three clicks, the ratchet advances, the blued mainspring tightens in the barrel, and the balance kicks into life. Turning the watch over reveals a restrained ivory dial with applied gold indices, blued leaf hands, small seconds at six and a moon-shaped power reserve; everything on both faces is annotated in French and English on hover or tap. Below, four sparse sections ruled in thin gold give the specifications, the two-person workshop, three finishing close-ups that respond to light, and a waiting list whose confirmation is engraved letter by letter onto a caseback ring.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A landing page for an invented independent watchmaker, "Atelier Vesper" of the invented Jura village "Les Brenassons", presenting one watch, the "Heure Bleue 38", whose mechanical movement runs live on the page: an SVG calibre with mainspring barrel, gear train, escapement and balance wheel, all meshing at correct ratios and keeping the viewer's actual local time. Concept: transparency as luxury; the page is a working watch. Mood: nocturnal, exacting, quietly opulent. Palette: black-anthracite #0C0D0F background, rose-champagne gold #C7A66B (gear plating, hairlines, display type), ivory #E9E4DA for text and dial, oxblood ruby #7B1E1E for jewels and one accent, blued steel #2A3138 (with a brighter heat-blue highlight #3F5F8A for screws and hands). Dark page (the concept calls for a jeweller's black), matte, with no neon or gradient text; metal rendered with conic and linear gradients plus fine engraved stripes (Côtes de Genève as repeating diagonal bands) and circular graining (perlage as overlapping small circles) on the plates. Signature object: the movement, drawn large (it should fill most of the first viewport on desktop, and the width on phones). Components, each a real SVG gear with computed involute-like teeth (tooth counts chosen so ratios are right): the barrel (slow), centre wheel (one turn per hour, carrying the minute hand), third wheel, fourth wheel (one turn per minute, carrying the small seconds), escape wheel with its distinctive club teeth advancing in steps, the pallet fork rocking between banking pins with two ruby pallets, and the balance wheel with a spiral hairspring that breathes (the spiral's turns compress and expand) oscillating at 3 Hz (21,600 beats per hour), so the seconds hand advances in six tiny steps per second. Bridges with engraved text ("ATELIER VESPER · V.03 · 21 RUBIS · AJUSTÉ 5 POSITIONS"), jewels set in gold chatons, blued screws with slots at varied angles. All rotations derive from one time source (Date.now()), so the hour and minute hands on the dial view show the real time and everything is phase-consistent. Two faces: the page opens on the caseback side; a toggle button drawn as a crown ("Retourner la montre / turn the watch over") flips between the caseback side and the dial side with a 3D rotateY of the whole watch case (lugs, strap and crown included): the dial is restrained (ivory with applied gold indices, leaf hands in blued steel, a small seconds sub-dial at 6, a discreet moon-shaped power-reserve arc, "Vesper" in a fine serif); the back shows the calibre under sapphire with a moving reflection. On load: the balance is still; the crown turns three clicks (winding), the mainspring barrel tightens, the balance kicks into life and the train starts: the first three seconds. Interaction: hovering/tapping any component highlights it in brighter gold, dims the rest, and opens a hairline-ruled annotation with its name in French and English, its tooth count and its rate ("Roue de centre · centre wheel · 80 teeth · one turn each hour"); a "ralenti" control slows the escapement 20× so the viewer can watch the pallet fork lock and release each tooth; dragging the crown winds the power reserve (the arc fills) and, pulled out one notch (click, or Enter), sets the time by dragging: hands and the entire train spin accordingly, then ease back to real time on release. A numbered index of the twelve organs (01–12) under the annotation selects the same highlights by keyboard or tap and turns the watch to the right face; a small status line shows the displayed time, the reserve in hours and the rate. An optional sound toggle (off by default; drawn as a tiny tuning fork) adds a precise tick at 6 beats per second synthesised with short filtered clicks, alternating tick/tock timbre. Sections beneath (sparse, generous spacing, thin gold rules): "La Montre": specifications as a two-column table (38 mm, 9.8 mm, hand-wound, 60-hour reserve, steel or rose gold, 12 pieces a year); "L'Atelier": a short text about the two-person workshop (Colette Vesper-Aubry and her nephew), specific and unboastful ("We make one watch at a time because we own one bench."), with a short inventory of the workshop; "La Montre" also carries a hairline side-elevation drawing with the 38 mm and 9.8 mm dimensions; "Finissage": three close-up SVG vignettes (anglage on a bridge edge catching light on hover, a black-polished screw head, perlage); "Liste d'attente": a single-field request with a custom input and a confirmation engraved onto a caseback ring (no network). Typography: display in a refined Didone/transitional serif (Didot/"Bodoni MT"/Baskerville/Georgia), light and large, with French small-cap labels at 10–11px tracked 0.3em; numerals in tabular lining figures; movement engravings in a narrow engraved-capitals style. Motion: slow, precise, 800–1200ms with cubic-bezier(.2,.7,.1,1); nothing bounces. Reduced motion: the movement shows the correct time but only the hands update (once per second); gears and balance are still.

Open Cross-Stitch Studio ↗
048

Cross-Stitch Studio

A sunlit sewing table seen from above: a beech embroidery hoop with a brass screw holds a square of cream aida cloth, and on it a small sampler stitches itself in over the first three seconds while a steel needle darts from stitch to stitch. Everything the viewer places is rendered as real thread: two crossed strands of twisted floss with sheen, shadow and hand-made irregularity, pulled from hole to hole with a little tightening bounce. To the left sit a stitched fabric title label, a wooden sewing-box tray of tools, ten bobbin cards of floss and a library of stamped motifs with a "stitch these words" field; to the right a paper pattern card keeps the project name, floss usage in metres, finished size and time. The hoop can be turned over in 3D to reveal the honest, messy back of the work, computed from the true sewing order, or swapped for a printable symbol chart with a key.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A pattern editor for cross-stitch embroidery in which the pattern is not a pixel grid but rendered thread: every cell the viewer fills becomes two crossed stitches of twisted floss on woven aida cloth, with highlights, shadow and slight irregularity, inside a wooden embroidery hoop. Concept: a genuinely usable tool whose output looks like the finished object. Mood: homely, sunny, meticulous. Palette: aida cloth cream #F4EEDD (with weave holes and a slightly darker thread shadow #D9CFB8), default floss set: turkey red #C0392B, delft blue #2C5F8D, moss green #3F7D4E, marigold #E3A72F, charcoal #2B2B2B, plus a second row of softer flosses (rose #D98C9A, sky #8DB7D6, sage #A3B899, cream-white #FBF7EC, walnut #6B4A2F). Light page on a pale linen-textured table (#E9E2D0) with a wooden hoop (beech tones #C9A26B / #A47B45) and brass screw. Canvas rendering: the fabric is drawn as a real even-weave: a grid of small square holes with woven thread bundles between them. Each stitch is drawn as two diagonal strands (the lower "/" first, the upper "\" on top) with rounded ends entering the holes, each strand shaded with a lighter centre line and darker edges to look twisted, a faint drop shadow on the cloth, and ±1px jitter and slight thickness variation per stitch so the work looks hand-made. Half stitches (chosen with a stitch-type toggle in the tray: full cross, rising half, falling half) and backstitch outlines (thin single lines along cell edges, for lettering and outlines) are supported. The hoop ring overlaps the cloth with a shadow, and the cloth outside the hoop is slightly slack and darker. Pattern grid: 48×48 cells at every screen size (phones start zoomed out and pinch in), with a bolder guide line every 10 cells as in printed charts (toggleable). Tools (drawn as objects in a sewing-box tray, with tooltips and keyboard shortcuts): needle (stitch one cell; drag to stitch a run), seam ripper (remove), flood-fill ("fill area"), backstitch line tool (click two points on grid intersections), colour picker, mirror mode (vertical, horizontal, both: for symmetrical folk motifs), move/pan, zoom in and zoom out (plus mouse wheel), undo/redo (Ctrl/Cmd+Z, shift for redo). A floss palette shown as wound skeins/bobbin cards with numbers ("No. 321 turkey red"). A starter library of motifs designed for the page as cell data that can be stamped onto the cloth: a folk heart, an eight-pointed star, a strawberry, a small bird, a house, a tree, a border repeat, and a complete 5×7 pixel alphabet for stitching text typed by the viewer ("stitch these words" input). On first load, a charming sampler is already on the cloth (a border, the alphabet's first letters, two birds facing a tree, the current year flanked by two small hearts and underlined in backstitch), stitched in by an animated needle over the first three seconds: each stitch appears in sewing order with the two strands laid down one after another. Signature moments: (1) every placed stitch animates as thread being pulled through: the strand draws from hole to hole with a tiny tightening bounce; (2) "Turn it over": the hoop flips in 3D to show the back of the work: the same pattern mirrored but rendered as the messy reverse: short vertical/horizontal travelling stitches, long thread carries between distant areas, knots and tails: computed from the actual stitch order; (3) "Chart view" toggles to a printable-style symbol chart (each colour as a symbol in a grid, with a key and thread lengths estimated in metres). Side panel in tidy type: project name (editable), stitch count by colour with estimated floss length, finished size at 14-count in cm and inches, time estimate at "a comfortable 300 stitches an hour". Buttons: "Save as picture" produces a PNG of the hoop via canvas.toBlob and a download link (no network); "Clear cloth" (with an in-page confirmation styled as a paper note, not a browser dialog); patterns persist for the session in memory only. Typography: title in a cross-stitch lettering drawn from the same pixel alphabet as actual stitches on the page header ("CROSS-STITCH STUDIO"), body UI in a warm humanist sans ("Gill Sans"/"Trebuchet MS"/system-ui) small and neat, numbers tabular. Touch: one finger stitches, two fingers pan/zoom; tool tray collapses to a bottom strip at 360px with large targets. Keyboard: arrows move a cell cursor, Space stitches, 1–9 choose floss. Reduced motion: stitches appear without pull animation; no flip (cross-fade to the back).

Open A Cabinet of Computed Shells ↗
049

A Cabinet of Computed Shells

A sunlit natural-history gallery wall in shell-pink plaster. A large porcelain-white shell floats above a linen-covered drum that stands on a pale cabinet of eight brass-labelled drawers; beside it hang a paper museum label and a brass-and-ivory instrument of five sliders and two rows of small specimen trays. On arrival a chambered nautilus grows out of its own apex, whorl after whorl, flame stripes and all. Pulling a drawer makes that shell become another before the visitor's eyes: the flat coil tightens and telescopes into a turret, swells into a cone shell tiled with computed tents, or flares open into a ribbed cockle valve, while the label re-sets itself, the slider handles slide to their new places and a crosshair travels across the map of all possible shells below. Sawing the nautilus open reveals a spiral of pearly chambers. The tone is quiet scholarship: classical serif titles, tracked small-cap legends, plain accurate copy about why a shell that grows only at its edge must be a logarithmic spiral, and how David Raup found that life uses only part of the space the equation allows.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A museum exhibit on the mathematics of seashells: one growth equation, four numbers, and nearly every coiled shell in the sea. The centrepiece is a real-time 3D shell rendered on Canvas 2D (software-projected mesh with shading; no WebGL needed) from the classic coiling model: a generating curve (an ellipse) that revolves around an axis while expanding at a constant rate (W, whorl expansion), moving away from the axis (D), and sliding along it (T, translation), with the aperture shape (S). Changing the parameters morphs a nautilus into a snail, a turret, a cone, a clam. Concept: the exhibit lets visitors turn the handles of evolution's design space. Mood: gentle scholarship; a natural-history gallery with sun on pale walls (a soft, slowly drifting patch of window light with mullion shadows lies across the page). Palette: shell-pink plaster #F3E9E2, coral-rose #D9A38F, umber #7C5C4D, dark sepia #2D2424 for type, and sea-glass green #9FB8B3 as the secondary accent. Shell surface colours derive from these plus cream #FBF5EC and a deep chestnut #6A3B2B for stripes; the brass of the instrument is a muted gold derived from the umber. Light page, soft shadows, paper-label textures (procedural grain). The shell renderer: build a mesh by sweeping the generating ellipse along the logarithmic spiral for 2–9 whorls (60 segments around the aperture × about 260 steps along the spiral on desktop, 48 × 190 on phones; steps are distributed per half-whorl and weighted toward the large final whorls, and for very fast expansion only the visible part of the spiral is swept). The apex tapers to a closed protoconch. Wall that lies buried inside the previous whorl is removed analytically. Project with a simple perspective camera; sort quads back-to-front with a bucket sort (painter's algorithm) and fill each with shading from a light at upper left: soft wrapped diffuse with warm shadows plus a tight specular so the surface looks porcellaneous; draw the interior of the aperture darker and pinker, deepening with distance from the lip, with a nacreous sheen option (sea-glass and pink iridescence). A thin cream lip gives the aperture its thickness. Surface ornament computed in the parameter space of the surface: growth lines (fine rings parallel to the aperture), spiral cords, axial ribs or shoulder knobs (real displacement of the mesh), and pigment patterns: axial flame stripes, spiral bands, dots, or a tent pattern from a 1D cellular automaton laid down along the growing edge (an excitable row of cells: each lit cell lights its neighbours and then rests, with rare spontaneous firing, so fronts travel diagonally and annihilate where they meet, leaving white tents on a golden ground, as on cone shells), which is noted on the label. Pigment is supersampled per quad, and for the tent pattern each quad is split in two so the automaton runs at twice the mesh resolution. A soft elliptical shadow on a linen-covered drum plinth, which stands on the cabinet below. Layout: the specimen on its plinth at centre-left, large; directly beneath it the cabinet of eight preset drawers; at right, a museum label and the "instrument": four brass-and-ivory sliders (W expansion on a warped logarithmic scale from 1.05 to 100,000; D distance from axis; T translation along axis; S aperture shape) plus whorl count, and ornament/pattern selectors drawn as small specimen trays with drawn swatches. Above, the gallery title in a quiet classical serif, large: "A Cabinet of Computed Shells" with gallery number 49. Below the main display: a "morphospace" map beside the wall text: a 2D chart of W against T dotted with tiny rendered shells at their coordinates (recomputed for the current D, S and whorl count), with a crosshair showing where the current shell sits, a dotted trail for the last journey, numerals marking the eight drawers, and regions labelled (planispiral ammonoids, high-spired gastropods, bivalves, "not found in nature: too weak, or cannot close"; a fainter unlabelled patch marks the low-spired snails); clicking anywhere in the chart sends the shell there. Signature moment: the morph. Presets as engraved drawer labels (chambered nautilus, garden snail, turret shell, cone, top shell, cockle-like bivalve half, ram's horn/open spiral, and "impossible": a loose corkscrew that almost nothing builds) glide all parameters, ornament, pigment and the viewing angle over about 2 seconds with eased interpolation, so one shell literally grows into another on the plinth, whorls tightening and the spire telescoping out, while the label text re-sets and the slider handles and chart crosshair travel with it. The cone preset also leans its generating ellipse outward at the shoulder (a hidden fifth quantity carried by the presets) so that it reads as a true cone. On load the shell grows from the protoconch outward along its spiral (the sweep extends from the apex to the aperture) in the first 2.5 seconds: a strong entrance. Interaction: drag on the specimen to rotate it (free trackball rotation with inertia; touch supported, vertical swipes still scroll the page), double-click/tap to reset the view; when left alone the shell turns very slowly from side to side; sliders update the mesh live; a "section" toggle saws the shell in half to reveal the interior: spired shells are cut along the axis (only alternate half-turns of the sweep are drawn, with cream cut faces showing wall thickness), flat coils are cut through their plane of symmetry so the spiral of chambers lies open, and for the nautilus and ram's horn presets the septa appear as dished walls with visible cut edges; the saw animates and the view turns to face the cut. A "nacre" toggle switches the pearly interior; a "grow" control replays growth; the lamp follows the pointer subtly. Keyboard: sliders focusable and adjustable with arrows; presets, trays and toggles are buttons. Label copy: museum voice, accurate and plain: shells grow only at the edge, by adding material at the aperture while keeping the same shape, which forces a logarithmic spiral; the palaeontologist David Raup mapped this space of forms with early computers in the 1960s and noticed that real animals occupy only part of it. Each preset has a two-sentence note; forms reached by hand are labelled "Unnamed form, Forma computata" with a note for the province of the map they fall in. The label also reports province, whether the whorls touch, and the surface treatment. Typography: title and specimen names in Baskerville/Palatino/Georgia with italics for Latin-style names; label data in small caps and tabular figures; slider legends in tiny tracked caps. Reduced motion: no growth animation, no idle turning; presets switch with a cross-fade; rotation only on drag. Performance: rebuild mesh only on parameter change; fewer quads on small screens; DPR capped at 2; drawing pauses when the tab is hidden or the specimen is off screen, and halves its rate when only the idle turn is moving.

Open The Aldwyn, Source to Sea ↗
050

The Aldwyn, Source to Sea

A sideways journey down an invented English river, drawn as one continuous flat-colour panorama in the manner of a children's atlas for grown-ups. The page opens in rain on a moor, with the title lettered large in rough-edged Palatino italic and a paper boat dropping into a spring no wider than a pencil line. Scrolling carries the reader 138 kilometres downstream: over a gritstone fall, under a packhorse bridge, past a turning mill wheel, through buttercup meadows, a terraced brewery town, a working lock, pylons and poplars, mudflats, docks, and finally past a lighthouse into an evening sea. The river is a single unbroken blue path that widens from a thread to the whole lower third of the stage, and the little boat rides it the entire way, behaving differently at every obstacle. Each of the eleven stations carries a white-on-green kilometre sign and a short, dry caption; a slim strip along the bottom shows the whole river in miniature with an altitude profile and a live readout of distance, height and flow.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A horizontally scrolling illustrated journey down an invented river, "the Aldwyn", from a spring on a moor to the open sea: 138 kilometres as one continuous panoramic drawing that the reader travels along sideways. Vertical scroll input (wheel, trackpad, touch swipe up/down as well as sideways, keyboard) is translated into horizontal travel, with no native horizontal page overflow on any device. Concept: a river as a sentence read left to right; one unbroken ribbon of water. Mood: fresh, observant, affectionate; an illustrated children's-atlas for adults. Palette: pale meadow paper #EAF2E3, river blue #2F6690, leaf green #3A7D44, buttercup yellow #F4B942, deep bottle-green ink #254441 for outlines and type. Flat, limited-colour illustration in SVG with a consistent line weight, texture from simple hatch patterns, no gradients except in the water. Light page. Structure: a fixed viewport-height stage containing a very wide SVG world (about 9–11 viewport widths on a desktop screen, proportionally more on a narrow phone, since the drawing is scaled to the stage height) translated horizontally according to scroll progress (a tall scroll container with a sticky stage). Three parallax layers: distant hills and sky (slow), the river and its banks (base speed), foreground reeds, fence posts and birds (fast). The river itself is one continuous path that begins as a thread and widens steadily to fill the lower third at the estuary; it is always on screen. Stations along the way (each with a small typeset caption block and a kilometre marker in the form of a white-on-green river sign; captions specific and lightly witty, 25–45 words): km 0, the spring at Hob Moss: a trickle under cotton grass, a curlew; km 6, the first waterfall, Grey Mare's Step; km 17, a packhorse bridge and a dipper bobbing on a stone; km 31, the old mill at Farrow, with a turning water wheel; km 48, water meadows, cows standing in the shallows, a heron; km 63, the town of Aldwynbridge: a weir, terraced houses, a brewery chimney, a rowing eight passing, anglers; km 80, a lock and a narrowboat rising in it; km 97, the reservoir intake and a pylons-and-poplars stretch; km 115, the tidal limit: mudflats, moored boats leaning, waders; km 131, the docks: cranes, a rusty dredger, a ferry; km 138, the bar and the open sea: a lighthouse, a line of surf, and the river's blue dissolving into a wide horizon. Life and motion: water flows: animated dashes/ripples moving downstream along the river path at a speed that changes with the gradient (fast and white at the fall, slow and broad at the estuary); the mill wheel turns; the narrowboat "Patience" rises in the lock as the reader scrolls through that stretch (scroll-linked water level and gates; the camera slows there), leaves upstream, and the chamber then empties again with the paper boat inside; birds fly when approached; rain crosses the moor at the start, sunlight at the meadows, an evening tint by the sea (the sky layer shifts hue along the journey). Signature moment: a small paper boat (folded, white with a yellow flag) launched at the spring that rides the current the whole way: it stays near the viewport centre, bobbing and rotating with the flow, tumbling over the waterfall, spinning in the mill race, waiting in the lock, dodging the rowing eight, and finally heading out past the lighthouse; if the reader stops scrolling it drifts on a little and circles in an eddy. Navigation aids: a fixed slim strip at the bottom: the entire river in miniature as a line with station dots and the boat's position, clickable to jump (smooth animated travel); an altitude profile above it falling from 520 m to 0; a readout: "km 48 · 71 m above the sea · flow 9 m³/s" (invented but consistent, increasing downstream), with the name of the current reach beside it on wide screens; and a small designed sound toggle (off by default) for a synthesised water noise that swells at the fall, the weir and the surf. The opening screen: title lettered large in a hand-drawn-feeling serif italic (Palatino/Georgia italic) "The Aldwyn" with "source to sea, 138 km" and a designed cue: an arrow made of flowing dashes and the words "scroll: the river does the rest". End screen: the boat on the open sea, a low sun with a glitter path, and a line: "It took the water eleven days. You were quicker." with a "Back to the spring" button. Typography: captions in a book serif with small-caps place names; kilometre signs in a bold condensed sans; a few hand-lettered labels along the bank set on curves (SVG textPath) for tributaries ("Farrow Beck"). Touch: vertical and horizontal swipes both travel; a mouse can also drag the landscape; keyboard: arrow keys and Page Up/Down move along. Phone: stage height fits the viewport; captions sit in the upper third. Reduced motion: no parallax or water animation; the view snaps from station to station and the boat simply appears at each one.

Open Pinscreen ↗
051

Pinscreen

A black acrylic desk toy fills the viewport: a glossy rounded frame slung between two chrome posts, holding roughly ten thousand hexagonally packed steel pin-heads (about five thousand on a phone). On arrival a hand presses in from behind and withdraws, and its impression slowly melts back into the lattice. Every pin is a tiny domed, lathe-turned disc lit by a single raking lamp, so slopes facing the light flash white, slopes facing away drop to dark steel and raised forms throw shadows across their lower neighbours. The pointer itself becomes a hand pushing through the field; dragging leaves a subsiding trail. A tray of machined black keys presses in a hand, a mask-like face, a sphere, a travelling ripple, a typed word and the current time, and a flip key tips the frame so the whole field cascades out and back in a glittering wave. The only colour anywhere is one thin red line on the brushed-steel logo plate, which states the true pin count.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A digital version of the pin-art desk toy: a dense field of steel pins that can slide in and out, taking the impression of whatever presses against them. The viewer pushes shapes into the pins from behind with the pointer, and the surface responds as a gleaming metallic relief. Concept: thousands of identical objects forming a soft image; the wow is the material: brushed steel pin-heads catching a raking light. Mood: sleek, tactile, 1980s executive-desk. Palette: black acrylic frame and background #0B0C0E, steel highlights #C9CED6, mid steel #6C7480, specular white #FFFFFF; a single thin red line (#E5383B) on the frame's logo plate as the only colour. Dark page, monochrome metal. Rendering: a hexagonally packed grid of round pins seen nearly front-on with a slight perspective tilt: about 9,000–14,000 pins on desktop (pin pitch ~9–10px), 4,000–6,000 on phones. Each pin has a height h in [0,1]. Draw each pin-head as a small disc whose position is offset by parallax according to height (raised pins shift up-left and grow ~15%), whose brightness comes from a lighting model using the surface normal estimated from neighbouring heights (raking light from the upper left, strong specular falloff so slopes facing the light flash white and those facing away fall to dark steel), with a drop shadow cast onto lower neighbours on the lower right. Flat regions (all pins down or all up) show the mesmerising regular lattice with a soft sheen gradient. Implement efficiently: precomputed sprite discs in ~16 brightness levels drawn with drawImage, or a WebGL point-sprite/instanced renderer with a fragment shader for round heads and a Canvas 2D fallback; typed arrays for heights and targets; update only what changed where possible. Physics: pins follow their target height with a fast spring and a little overshoot (so the surface ripples slightly when a shape arrives) and fall back slowly under "gravity" with friction when the pressing object leaves: a slow, satisfying relaxation taking a few seconds, slightly randomised per pin, so impressions linger and melt. Interaction: moving the pointer/finger presses a rounded hand-sized form into the pins from behind (a soft dome with finger-like lobes); pressing harder (mouse down / touch force / hold) pushes further; dragging leaves a trail that slowly subsides. A tray of presets pushes objects in with an animated press: a hand (five-finger relief), a face (a simple mask-like relief generated from ellipsoids: brow, nose, lips, chin), a sphere, a ripple (animated concentric wave that keeps travelling), the word of the viewer's choosing (type up to 8 characters: rendered to an offscreen canvas, blurred and used as a height map; on portrait screens a long word breaks into two lines), and "the time" (the current clock time embossed as digits, updating every minute with the old digits sinking and the new rising; stacked hours over minutes on portrait screens). A "flip" control tilts the whole frame on its side pivots (like turning the toy over): every pin slides fully out in a wave travelling from top to bottom, then, as the frame swings back, slides home again in a second wave, leaving the field reset: the signature moment: a cascading metallic shimmer travelling across the whole field with a soft rattling sound if sound is enabled. On load: a hand presses in from behind and withdraws, leaving its slowly fading impression. Frame and details: the pin field sits inside a glossy black acrylic frame with rounded corners, a faint reflection and two chrome side posts; a small plate reads "PINSCREEN · 12,480 PINS" (actual count). Dragging the light: a small draggable sun icon/lamp rides around the frame's bezel and changes the light direction, making the relief shading swing (the bezel gloss and the field's sheen follow it; with the lamp focused the arrow keys move it). Keyboard: arrow keys move a virtual presser; Space presses; 1–6 select presets; F flips; S toggles sound. Typography: minimal: tracked small caps in steel grey, a geometric sans (Futura/"Century Gothic"/"Avenir"/system-ui), 10–11px; the preset tray labels are tiny icons drawn in SVG with text labels. A designed hint under the frame: "press into the pins" which fades after first use. Optional sound toggle (off by default): a soft metallic shimmer (band-passed noise bursts) scaled by how many pins are moving. Reduced motion: pins move without overshoot; flip resets instantly with a fade; no travelling ripple preset. Responsive: the frame fits the viewport with margin at all sizes; pin count adapts to keep 60fps (measure frame time and reduce density if needed).

Open BÉTON BRUT — Six Buildings Nobody Asked For ↗
052

BÉTON BRUT — Six Buildings Nobody Asked For

A photocopied architecture zine, issue six of BÉTON BRUT, laid out as nine full-viewport spreads on a board-marked concrete-grey ground. Colossal condensed capitals crop at the page edges and run behind six isometric concrete models of an invented new town, Northmere; typewritten essays by the zine-maker Dot Ferreira sit on taped-down pale slips with orange marginal numbering. As each spread arrives its building is poured storey by storey out of the base plate, flashing orange formwork as each lift sets, then the giant name slams in behind it, the page shakes and a rubber stamp lands. Every model can be turned, pulled apart into a labelled section and re-lit with a sun slider that throws a long shadow across the page. A charcoal manifesto spread and a back cover with a creased fold-out site plan and a cut-out subscription coupon close the issue. The tone is monumental, argumentative, affectionate and a bit funny.

↗ Open

Original prompt

An architecture zine about brutalism: six invented concrete buildings in an invented post-war new town, "Northmere", each presented as an isometric model built from CSS 3D transforms (stacked boxes, cantilevers, ramps, service towers, repeating window modules), set against enormous, heavy grotesque type. Concept: typography with the mass of concrete; pages that feel poured rather than laid out. Mood: monumental, argumentative, affectionate, a bit funny. Palette: raw concrete grey #BDB9B1 as the page ground, shuttering-shadow charcoal black #1A1A1A, safety orange #FF5A00 as the single violent accent, and pale cement #E9E6DF for light faces. Grey colour-field page. Texture: board-marked concrete (fine horizontal plank lines with knots via repeating gradients and SVG noise), tie-holes as small dots in a grid, water stains running down from parapets, a little moss; photocopier grit over the type. Typography: the main voice. Colossal condensed grotesque capitals (Impact/"Haettenschweiler"/"Arial Narrow"/"Helvetica Neue Condensed Black" stack), set so large they crop at the viewport edges, stacked with almost no leading, sometimes rotated 90°, sometimes running behind the buildings so the isometric masses collide with and overlap the letters; body text in small, tight monospace or typewriter (Courier) in narrow columns, with orange marginal numbering; captions in tiny caps with arrows. Overprint effects: orange words multiplied over black. Issue line: "BÉTON BRUT · No. 6 · THE NORTHMERE ISSUE · 50p". Structure: a vertical scroll of "spreads", each a full viewport: Cover (the title three lines tall: BÉTON / BRUT / No.6, the last line in overprinted orange; an orange circle "sun" behind a tower model, a 50p price roundel, the line "Six buildings nobody asked for and everyone now defends"); then one spread per building; then a manifesto spread and a back cover with a fold-out style site plan (an isometric town plan with the six buildings marked) and subscription coupon with dotted cut line (a button snips it loose). The plan's numbered markers and legend jump to the buildings. Buildings (each with name, date, architect of an invented municipal office, a 90–130 word essay with opinion and detail, and three "facts" such as tonnes of concrete and number of steps): 1) Northmere Civic Centre (1967): an inverted ziggurat on pilotis; 2) The Trinity Road car park: a helical ramp tower, "the best room in town has no walls"; 3) Hallam Point: a 22-storey slab with a separate lift tower joined by bridges every third floor; 4) St Jude's Church: a folded concrete tent with a slot of coloured light; 5) The Water Board pumping station: windowless drum and fins; 6) Queensway bus station: a long canopy on mushroom columns, scheduled for demolition, "write to the council". Isometric models (CSS 3D, transform-style: preserve-3d, built from divs as extruded boxes with three visible faces shaded light/mid/dark concrete with the board-mark texture, plus orange-highlighted details): each model sits on a base plate with a dashed north arrow and a scale figure (a tiny person). Signature moment: as each spread scrolls into view the building is "poured": its volumes rise storey by storey from the base plate with a heavy, slightly delayed ease (cubic-bezier(.7,0,.2,1), each lift 250–400ms, staggered), formwork lines flashing orange for an instant as each level sets, and the giant building name slams in behind it with a slight page shake. Hover/drag on a model rotates it around its vertical axis (pointer x mapped to rotateY within the isometric rig, with inertia; touch drag supported), and holding on it "explodes" the floors apart vertically to show the section, with floor labels in orange. A vertical sun slider (06h to 18h) at the outer edge of each model stage changes the light direction (face shades swap smoothly) and casts a long skewed shadow across the base plate and the page. Under each model sit two small buttons, "Cut section" (a keyboard-friendly toggle for the explode) and "Pour again", plus a tiny-caps hint; arrow keys turn a focused model and Space lifts its floors. Details: orange "LISTED" / "AT RISK" / "DEMOLISHED 1998" rubber stamps slapped at angles (and "NOT NEUTRAL" on the manifesto, which is set on a charcoal field); the demolished pumping station has its giant name half in outline; a running footer with page numbers in huge numerals; crop marks at corners; an editor's note in the voice of the invented zine-maker Dot Ferreira; the manifesto set as numbered slogans that are specific, not generic ("4. A ramp is a staircase with manners."). Navigation: a fixed orange index of six numbered squares on the right edge, jumps smooth-scrolled. If the condensed display face is missing, the fallback grotesque is measured and squeezed to the same width so the type keeps its mass. Phone: type still huge (clamp with vw), models scale to width, columns collapse to one. Reduced motion: buildings appear built; no shake; rotation only on drag.

Open Sandpile ↗
053

Sandpile

A living four-colour screenprint. A cream sheet carries a giant stacked "sand / pile" title with misregistered orange and blue shadows, and the dot of its "i" is itself a seven-by-seven sandpile that never stops toppling. Beside it a large square table with a black outline and a hard shadow sits empty for half a second, then a tap opens over the centre and a fractal medallion of ink, orange and blue blooms outward behind a ragged yellow front before settling into a slow drip. Everything else on the sheet is a readout of that one rule: a looping four-panel pictogram, a legend whose bars and counts follow the table, a tally in tabular numerals, and a log-log plot whose orange points drop into place along a blue fitted line as random rain is measured. The viewer pours, pins taps, drops handfuls of ten thousand grains, computes the group identity, sweeps the table with a diagonal wipe and saves the result as a poster.

↗ Open

Original prompt

An interactive simulation of the Abelian sandpile model: drop grains of sand onto a grid; any cell holding four or more grains topples, sending one grain to each of its four neighbours, which may topple in turn. From this single rule comes an intricate, symmetric fractal tapestry and avalanches of every size. Concept: self-organised criticality as a hands-on poster; the viewer pours sand and watches order assemble itself. Mood: bright, graphic, like a four-colour screenprint that happens to be alive. Palette: paper #F5F0E6 for cells with 0 grains, deep ink #111111 for 1, vivid orange #F25C05 for 2, strong blue #0E79B2 for 3, and bright yellow #F2C80F for cells that are unstable (4 or more) and toppling: so avalanches flash yellow as they propagate. Light page; flat colour; crisp pixels (image-rendering: pixelated) with a subtle paper grain overlay so it feels printed. Simulation: grid stored in typed arrays (Int32 grain counts so a heap of many thousands fits in one cell, Uint8 flags), sized so cells are about 3 CSS px (an odd side length up to 301, about 249 on a 1440x900 desktop, about 109 on a phone); open boundary (grains fall off the edge into a sink border). Toppling processed with an active list, several waves per frame under a time budget so large avalanches play out visibly as spreading yellow fronts rather than instantly; a five-step speed control goes from "one topple wave per frame" (slow, mesmerising) through 4, 16 and 64 waves to "flat out" (as fast as possible). Rendering through ImageData scaled up with nearest-neighbour. Modes: (1) "Pour": click/tap and hold to pour a steady stream of grains at the pointer; the pile under it grows and collapses outward in pulses, building the classic fractal figure with its nested triangles and stripes. Multiple pour points can be dropped and left running (click to pin a "tap" that keeps dripping; click it again to remove): two taps make interfering figures with a seam between them. (2) "Identity": a button that computes and displays the sandpile group's identity element for the current grid size (start with every cell at 6, stabilise; subtract from 6 everywhere, stabilise again), animating the long stabilisation in two captioned stages: a famously beautiful picture. (3) "Rain": single grains fall at random positions on a critical pile (if the table is too empty it is first loaded, with a diagonal wipe, to the critical density of 2.125 grains per cell); each grain's avalanche is highlighted with a flat yellow overprint and measured; tapping the table drops a grain of your own. Layout: poster-like and asymmetric: the square field large at the centre; at the left a tall narrow column with the title in giant stacked lowercase letters in a heavy geometric sans (Futura/"Century Gothic"/"Avenir Next"/system-ui heavy) with hard orange and blue offset shadows like a misregistered print: "sand / pile" with the dot of the "i" drawn as a tiny living sandpile that keeps toppling; below it, the rule stated as a four-step pictogram (a cell with three dots holds, four is too many, it topples, one dot goes to each neighbour) animated in a loop; a live legend of the colours with counts and bars; and an avalanche-size histogram on log-log axes that accumulates in Rain mode, its points settling onto a straight line (the power law, with a fitted line and its slope), with a one-sentence explanation: "Small slides are common, vast ones rare, and nothing in the rule says where one becomes the other." A narrow rail at the right holds the controls and the tally. Phone: title above, field full width, controls beneath, then rule, legend and histogram. Signature moment: the first big collapse. On load the field is empty for half a second, then a centred tap starts pouring fast-forwarded grains so that within about three seconds a crisp fractal medallion has bloomed outward in yellow-fronted waves; it then slows to a gentle drip so the viewer sees the structure refine. Dropping a large handful (double-click: 10,000 grains at once) causes a huge, slow, symmetric avalanche: the showpiece. Interaction details: hover shows a 1-cell cursor with the cell's grain count; a "handful" size selector (1 / 100 / 10,000 grains) sets what a double-click or the "drop it" button (at the centre) releases; "sweep the table" clears with a diagonal wipe; a fourfold mirror toggle mirrors every drop and pinned tap; a "save as poster" button exports the current field as PNG (canvas only). Controls are drawn as flat geometric chips in the palette with black outlines and hard offset shadows that depress on press; keyboard focus rings in blue; Space toggles a tap at the centre; with the field focused, arrow keys move the cursor, Enter pins or removes a tap and Shift+Enter drops a handful. Counters in tabular numerals: grains added, grains lost off the edge, topplings, largest slide. A status line under the field explains the current mode and reports the last avalanche. Reduced motion: avalanches resolve instantly without yellow flashing; intro shows the finished medallion. Performance: time-budgeted toppling loop (about 8 ms per frame), pause when hidden.

054

Seven Sisters — A Nest of Dolls

A sheet of cream paper with a folk-painted shelf across its lower third, a slowly turning floral rosette behind it, and one lacquered nesting doll standing in the middle. Tap her at the waist and her top half springs up, tips and is set down leaning on the shelf, a dark wooden hollow appears, and the next sister rises out of it, hops over the rim, lands with a squash and a wobble, and gets her name ribbon; the row slides to stay centred as the family grows. Six times this repeats, each sister smaller by a fifth and painted with her own scarf, face and apron motif (sunflower, strawberries, rooster, samovar, rowan, tulip), until Little Zoya, who is solid and asleep. Tapping Zoya wakes her: her eyes open, she blushes and jumps, the painted bird leaves her apron, grows as it climbs and circles above the sisters, and seventeen flowers bloom one by one along the bottom border. All of it runs without a single line of script.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A CSS-only (no JavaScript) set of seven nesting dolls. The viewer clicks the largest doll: it splits at the waist, its top half lifts and tips aside, and the next doll hops out and stands beside it; and so on down to the seventh, which is tiny and solid and holds a surprise. Each doll is illustrated purely in CSS (divs, border-radius, gradients, box-shadows, pseudo-elements, clip-path), each with its own painted pattern and expression. Concept: the delight of opening, reproduced with checkboxes. Mood: joyful, folk, hand-painted, warm. Palette: cream paper #F8EFD9 background, lacquer red #C8102E, sunflower gold #F2A900, forest green #0B6E4F, soot black #1D1D1B for outlines, hair and eyes; plus small touches of cream-white for faces and a rosy cheek pink (#F4A6A0). Light page; glossy lacquer highlights on the dolls (a soft white streak down the left side), small contact shadows beneath. The dolls: classic nesting-doll silhouette (a small head dome merging into a wide rounded body: built from two overlapping rounded shapes or one element with an elaborate border-radius), with a round face panel (cream), simple features (two dot eyes with lashes, tiny curved mouth, pink cheeks), a headscarf knotted under the chin, an apron panel on the belly carrying each doll's own motif made from CSS shapes, and a decorative band at the waist that hides the seam. The seven sisters, largest to smallest, each with a name lettered on a small ribbon beneath and a distinct colourway and motif: 1 Vasilisa (red scarf, apron with a big sunflower built from rotated petals); 2 Marfa (green scarf, strawberries and leaves); 3 Darya (gold scarf, a rooster); 4 Olena (black scarf with gold dots, a samovar); 5 Yeva (red with white polka dots, a bunch of rowan berries); 6 Nina (green, a single tulip); 7 Little Zoya (plain red, eyes closed, asleep, does not open). Sizes decrease by a constant ratio (about 0.8). Mechanics: seven (six opening) hidden checkboxes with labels covering each doll. When doll n is checked: its top half (a separate element clipped at the waist) rises with a springy cubic-bezier(.34,1.56,.64,1), rotates and settles tilted beside its bottom half like a set-down lid, revealing a dark hollow interior with a wooden rim; then doll n+1, initially scaled down and hidden inside, pops up with a hop (two-stage keyframes: rise, overshoot, land with a squash), slides to its place in the row to the right (or below on narrow screens), and gives a little side-to-side wobble; its label becomes clickable. The dolls are nested in the markup exactly as they are in life (each sister lives between her elder's hollow and front wall), every length is in the doll's own em so the 0.8 ratio is one font-size rule, and the lid is set down on the shelf to the left, leaning behind its own bottom half. As sisters come out the whole row glides to stay centred. Unchecking (clicking an opened doll's top) reverses: the smaller doll hops back in and the lid closes: only allowed visually when inner dolls are closed (use sibling selectors so closing an outer doll also hides all inner ones gracefully). The seventh, Little Zoya: clicking her does not open her; instead she wakes: her eyes open, she blushes, and a small painted bird that was her apron motif flies up out of the pattern and circles above the row (keyframed path), while tiny painted flowers bloom along the bottom border of the page one after another: the signature finale. Composition: the dolls stand on a shelf line drawn as a decorated folk border (a repeating pattern of diamonds, dots and leaves made with gradients) across the lower third; behind them a large faint circular floral rosette built from conic/radial gradients rotates extremely slowly. Title at top in a decorative but legible display treatment: "Seven Sisters" in a bold serif (Georgia/"Palatino" bold) with small ornamental dots between letters, red on cream, and a subtitle in small tracked caps: "a nest of dolls · open gently". A counter line uses CSS counters to report how many dolls are out: "3 of 7 sisters are out". Beside it a short red hint changes with every state ("tap Vasilisa at the waist", "Olena has put the kettle on", "hush, Little Zoya is asleep. Tap her", "Zoya is awake, and so is the garden"). Name ribbons hang beneath the shelf and unfurl after each landing. Idle life: closed dolls blink occasionally (staggered keyframes) and sway by a degree; hovering a doll makes her lean toward the cursor side (using left/right hover zones) and brightens the highlight. All labels are keyboard-focusable (inputs visually hidden but focusable, with a clear focus ring drawn around the doll in black-and-gold dashes), Space toggles. Layout: a single row on desktop, with the row wrapping onto a second decorated shelf on phones and portrait tablets (two sisters above, five below; Darya hops down to the lower shelf) (dolls scale via clamp/vw so the largest is no wider than ~40vw at 360px); no horizontal overflow at any stage of the animations. Reduced motion: dolls appear/disappear with short opacity transitions and no hopping, blinking or rotation.

Open Kettlewick Observatory — Seismograph No. 2 ↗
055

Kettlewick Observatory — Seismograph No. 2

A working observatory instrument presented as a page of stationery: an engraved masthead, a slate-and-brass elevation drawing of the seismograph (clockwork gearbox with a stepping escapement, a paper-wrapped drum sliding along its lead screw, long boom, knife-edge pivot, oil damper, sprung mass) and beneath it the unrolled recording sheet, where a simulated pen draws a continuous trace line by line. The page opens with the pen dropping onto the drum in the decaying surface waves of a distant earthquake, whose P, S and L flags, distance, magnitude nomogram, telegram and ledger entry appear as the first seconds pass. Everything the visitor does to the page (knocking, shaking, striking the pier with the mallet) is recorded by the same damped pen, annotated in red ink on the sheet and entered in the ledger with a dry remark. Analog readouts, a rack of filed sheets and a framed notice complete the bench.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A live drum seismograph for a fictional observatory: a clockwork cylinder wrapped in paper turns slowly beneath a pen on a long boom, which draws a continuous wiggling trace; the trace advances one line down the sheet each revolution, building the classic helical record of an observatory "day". The ground is quiet apart from microseisms, the occasional fictional distant earthquake, and the viewer: tapping or shaking the page is recorded by the instrument. Concept: a dashboard made of one instrument and its paper, where the viewer's own actions become data. Mood: attentive, old-fashioned, humane science. Palette: recording paper #F1EBDD, pen ink black #1F1F1F, observatory red #B3261E for time marks, annotations and alarm, instrument slate blue-grey #4C6A7A for metalwork; brass touches allowed in the mechanism only (#B08D3C). Light page. Composition: upper portion: the instrument in elevation, drawn in SVG/canvas: the drum (a cylinder shaded with a vertical gradient, paper wrapped around it, visible seam, end caps and a clockwork gearbox with a slowly turning escapement), the pen arm with its pivot, counterweight and a fine stylus touching the drum, damping vane in an oil pot, and the mass on its spring frame. Lower, larger portion: the unrolled sheet: the "seismogram" as a flat chart of parallel trace lines (each line representing one minute in accelerated observatory time at the standard drum speed, six times faster than real time; 26 lines visible), with red minute ticks made by the time-marker (a small lift in the trace every minute), hour labels at the left margin written in a neat hand, and the newest line being drawn live at the bottom with the pen position shown as a small red index. When the sheet fills, it scrolls up and the oldest lines leave. Signal model: baseline microseism noise (band-limited noise with slow amplitude swells, like ocean storms); a fictional teleseism every few minutes (generated with a seeded schedule from the real clock so it is the same for a given hour): a sharp small P-wave onset, a gap, a larger S-wave, then long rolling surface waves that grow and decay over many seconds, with a coda; local "events" from the viewer. The pen is simulated as a driven damped oscillator (so sharp inputs ring down realistically) and can clip against its stops with a visible flat-topped trace on very large shakes. Interaction (signature moment): tap, click or thump anywhere on the page (or press the Space bar; on phones, tapping the screen) and the instrument registers it: the whole page gives a tiny shudder, the pen arm kicks, and the trace records a spike that rings down; rapid tapping builds resonance; dragging the mouse fast "shakes the building". A "strike the pier" mallet control gives a calibrated pulse. When a larger event occurs the dashboard reacts: phase arrival flags are pinned to the trace as it is drawn (red "P", "S", "L" letters with hairlines), a distance estimate is computed from the S-minus-P time ("S − P = 41 s → about 380 km"), a magnitude estimate from amplitude appears on a small nomogram where a line connects distance and amplitude scales, and a telegram-style slip is typed out: "KETTLEWICK 0417 GMT · MODERATE SHOCK · DIST 380 KM · BEARING UNKNOWN (ONE STATION)". Each event is added to a ledger table (time, S−P, max amplitude in mm, remark), with the viewer's taps logged as "local disturbance (visitor)" with dry remarks. Side panel readouts in analog style: drum speed, paper remaining as a dwindling roll, pen ink level, a small 24-hour dial of observatory time, a "station noise" needle gauge that rises with the sea state. Controls: change drum speed (15 / 30 / 60 mm per minute: trace stretches); "change the paper" (the sheet lifts off, is initialled and dated in script, and files into a rack of previous days at the side; a fresh sheet wraps the drum); magnification ×1 / ×5 / ×25. Typography: observatory stationery: title in a modest engraved-style serif with small caps ("KETTLEWICK OBSERVATORY · SEISMOGRAPH No. 2 · VERTICAL COMPONENT"), handwritten-style italic annotations in ink (Georgia/Palatino italic, slightly rotated), typewriter face for telegram slips (Courier), tabular figures in the ledger. A short framed notice explains how to read P and S in three sentences. Entrance: the pen lowers onto the drum and immediately draws the tail end of an arriving earthquake so the first three seconds show a dramatic wavetrain decaying. Optional sound toggle (off by default): faint clockwork ticking and pen scratch whose loudness follows amplitude. Reduced motion: no page shudder; trace updates in one-second steps.
