Open The Combination ↗
056

The Combination

A lamplit, velvet-dark room with one object in it: a bottle-green enamelled Holloway & Stern safe with gilt pinstriping, three brass hinges, a five-spoke handle and a large combination dial. After a filament flicker the lamp comes on and the dial gives one idle spin to zero. The viewer cracks a random three-number combination by intimacy with the mechanism rather than by reading a display: every graduation clicks, but the gate of the wheel being worked sounds lower, softer and doubled, the dial drags for an instant, the red index shivers and the needle of a small stethoscope gauge leaps. A notepad quietly pencils in each number when the viewer rests on it. Throwing the handle on a wrong setting gets a clunk and a shudder; on the right one the three bolts thud back one by one, the handle spins, and the heavy door swings open in true perspective, showing its thickness and boltwork, onto felt shelves holding one of five small stories: a pearl, a bundle of letters, a ship in a bottle, a sleeping cat, or a card that says "Too late. — M."

↗ Open

Original prompt

A safe-cracking game played by ear and by feel. A heavy 1920s safe door with a combination dial fills the screen; the viewer turns the dial, listening and watching for the faint tell of each wheel, to find a three-number combination, then throws the handle and swings the door open to see what the vault holds. Concept: intimacy with a mechanism; a game whose main feedback is subtle sound and tiny visual cues rather than a HUD. Mood: tense, velvet-dark, luxurious, cinematic. Palette: near-black green #101412 background, bottle-green enamel door #1F3A2E, brass #C9A24B (dial, handle, hinges, lettering; highlight #E8CF8A, shadow #7A5F22), ivory #E7E0CF for numerals and text, deep red #8C2F23 for the indicator mark and felt. Dark page (the concept calls for it) with a single pool of lamplight on the door, falling off to darkness at the corners. The safe (SVG): a door with bevelled edges, pinstriping and a gilt-lettered maker's name in an arc ("HOLLOWAY & STERN · FIRE & THIEF RESISTING · LONDON"), three heavy hinges, a five-spoke brass handle, and the dial: a knurled brass ring with 100 graduations (numbered every 10 in ivory) rotating under a fixed red index mark at twelve o'clock, with a domed centre knob showing a moving specular highlight as it turns. Mechanism model (simulated faithfully enough to feel real): three wheels with gates; the combination is random each game (e.g. 34 – 71 – 12) with the classic sequence: turn left (counter-clockwise) at least three full turns then stop on the first number; turn right past the first number once and stop on the second; turn left directly to the third; then try the handle, which throws the bolts. The wheels are modelled as a real pack: the dial carries the third wheel, which picks up the second after a full turn, which picks up the first after another, and a wheel's gate can only be felt once the wheels behind it are already set. The viewer discovers each number by cues: as the dial passes a wheel's gate, the regular click of graduations is slightly different: softer, lower-pitched and with a tiny double "tick", and the dial has a barely perceptible visual notch: the red index mark trembles and the dial "sticks" for about 30ms (45ms on Apprentice; a slight resistance in the drag). Each time a wheel is picked up there is a single duller knock. A stethoscope gauge at the side (a small brass-rimmed meter whose sprung needle flicks with each click, swings far towards a red "GATE" zone with a double bounce at a true gate, and kicks the other way, towards "LIFT", when a wheel is picked up) gives a visual equivalent, so the game is fully playable with sound off. Difficulty levels: "Apprentice" (strong cues, gate width ±2), "Journeyman" (weaker cues, ±1), "Master" (faint cues, the exact graduation only, and one false gate that sounds low but gives no second tick). Changing the grade sets a new combination. Interaction: drag the dial in a circular motion with mouse or finger (angle from the dial centre, with momentum disabled for precision; the dial must feel weighted: slight smoothing), or use the left/right arrow keys (one graduation per press, Shift for five), or scroll over it. A notepad in the corner, on cream paper in pencil-style italic, records what the viewer has found ("First: 34?"), automatically pencilling a number when the viewer pauses on a detected gate, with the ability to rub it out. A "Try the handle" control (the handle itself): if the combination is wrong, the handle clunks a few degrees and springs back, the door shudders; if right: the signature moment: the bolts retract with three heavy thunks in sequence (visible bolt rods sliding at the door edge), the handle spins, and the door swings open on its hinges in 3D perspective (rotateY with a slow heavy ease, swinging past ninety degrees on large screens to reveal the door's thickness, the bolt ends in its edge and the boltwork, wheel-pack cover and a typed service label on its back; on phones it stops edge-on at the left), light spilling out; inside, on red felt shelves, is one of several randomly chosen contents rendered in SVG with a line of copy: a single pearl on a cushion; a stack of love letters tied with ribbon ("To be burned unread. — H."); a model ship in a bottle; a sleeping cat that opens one eye; nothing but a note reading "Too late. — M." Top-shelf set dressing (a deed box, ledgers) sits above. A timer and number of handle attempts are shown as a discreet engraved plate, and the result is pencilled on the notepad ("Opened in 0:43, 2 tries."). A brass "Lock it again" tag inside the vault swings the door shut, throws the bolts and resets with a new combination and new contents. Sound (Web Audio; off until the viewer turns on the designed brass toggle marked "Listen"): crisp per-graduation clicks (short filtered noise ticks), gate ticks with lower pitch and a second soft click, a low room tone, handle clunks, bolt thunks with sub-bass thud, door creak (filtered noise sweep). Stereo panning follows dial direction slightly. Typography: gilt signwriter capitals for the title "THE COMBINATION" in an arc above the door (SVG textPath; a Didone/wide serif stack with gold fill and thin dark outline, no gradients beyond a flat two-tone), instructions as a typed card slipped under a brass clip: "Left three turns. Listen for the one that sounds tired." Entrance: darkness; a lamp switches on with a filament flicker and the brass catches light; the dial gives one idle spin and settles at 0. Reduced motion: door opens with a cross-fade; no tremble (the gauge and sound still cue). Responsive: door scales to the viewport; on phones the view is a close-up of the door, cropped at the sides so the dial occupies most of the width for comfortable dragging, with the gauge and Listen switch directly beneath and the notepad, plate, grades and card below; when the combination is right the view pulls back to show the whole safe before the bolts go.

Open A Year of Daylight ↗
057

A Year of Daylight

A pale sunlight-yellow museum wall chart in Swiss information-design dress: a tight navy grotesk title, a serif-italic lede, tracked-caps labels and thin navy rules. The centrepiece is the daylight hourglass, a navy block of starry night with a sun-yellow band of day running through it, edged by orange, plum and blue twilight. On load the latitude sweeps from the equator to the Arctic Circle and settles on London, so the flat ribbon visibly swells into a lens. Beside it a small desk globe with a graduated meridian arc carries the latitude bead, the selected parallel and a terminator that tilts with the date, coloured with the same twilight bands as the chart. Above the chart the day length for the chosen date is set very large with its daily gain or loss, sunrise and sunset. Below, a heat map shows every latitude and day at once with labelled contours, a ring calendar bends the year into a sun-shaped figure, and a table of superlatives recalculates live. Everything is computed in the page from latitude and date.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A data visualization of how long the sun is up, everywhere, every day of the year: computed in the page from astronomy (solar declination from the day of year via the sun's mean longitude and anomaly, then sunrise hour angle from latitude: cos ω = −tan φ · tan δ, with polar day and polar night where the formula has no solution). No external data. Concept: one elegant chart that makes the seasons physically obvious: the hourglass of light widening and pinching as you move from the equator to the poles. Mood: luminous, clear, optimistic; a science-museum wall chart with a strong graphic identity. Palette: pale sunlight yellow #FFF4C2 (page ground and day), strong sun yellow #F7B801, orange #F18701 for twilight bands and highlights, deep twilight blue #1B2A5B, midnight navy #0B1026 for night. Light page: the ground is the pale yellow; night appears only inside the charts. Main chart ("the daylight hourglass"): for the chosen latitude, a large chart with the 365 days of the year along the horizontal axis (month initials, solstices and equinoxes marked with thin rules and small labels) and the 24 hours of the day on the vertical axis (midnight at top and bottom, noon in the middle, in local solar time). The area between sunrise and sunset is filled sun-yellow; civil, nautical and astronomical twilight are drawn as three successively darker bands around it, orange, a plum mix #8A4660, then twilight blue (sun 6°, 12°, 18° below the horizon, each computed with the same formula using the altitude term); night is navy with a sprinkling of tiny stars. At the equator the band is a flat ribbon; at 52° it is a plump lens; at 69° it balloons into midnight sun in summer and closes entirely in winter. The selected date (today on load) is marked with a vertical hairline carrying sunrise and sunset times and a dot at the current time; when another date is scrubbed, today stays marked with a dotted line and its dot. Labels "MIDNIGHT SUN" and "POLAR NIGHT" appear inside the chart where they apply, and the chart's headline number is today's day length at that latitude in hours and minutes, set very large, with the daily change ("gaining 2 min 41 s a day"). Signature moment: dragging latitude. A vertical latitude control at the left, drawn as a meridian arc of a small globe with a sliding marker from 90°N to 90°S (drag with pointer/finger, arrow keys, or pick a city chip), reshapes the chart continuously and smoothly at 60fps as the marker moves: the lens swelling, pinching, flipping upside-down as you cross the equator into the southern hemisphere; the globe beside it shows the latitude ring and the day/night terminator for the selected date, tilting through the year. On load, the latitude sweeps by itself from the equator to the Arctic Circle and back to a default mid-latitude (London, 51.5°N) within the first three seconds so the viewer immediately sees the shape transform. Secondary views: (1) "All latitudes at once": a heat map with day of year across and latitude down, colour = hours of daylight from navy (0) through blue, orange to yellow (24), with contour lines at 6, 9, 12, 15, 18 hours and labels, the 12-hour contours pinching together at the equinoxes (the −0.83° correction keeps them a few days apart); hovering reads off any cell ("68°N, 21 June: 24 h 00 m"). Clicking sets latitude and date; arrow keys move a probe and Enter selects. (2) A ring calendar for the chosen latitude: the year as a circle, each day a thin radial wedge whose yellow length is the day length: a sun-shaped figure that is round at the equator and a lopsided flower in Tromsø-like latitudes, with the twilight bands and stars beyond it; dragging around the ring sets the date. (3) A small table of superlatives computed live: longest day, shortest day, fastest change, dates of the first and last midnight sun and the start and end of polar night where they exist. (4) A short panel giving the general formula and the simplifications. City chips (latitudes that are well-known and safe): Quito 0°, Singapore 1°N, Cairo 30°N, Tokyo 36°N, New York 41°N, London 51.5°N, Reykjavík 64°N, Tromsø 69.7°N, Longyearbyen 78°N, Sydney 34°S, Ushuaia 55°S; chips are simple outlined labels in navy that fill yellow when active. A date scrubber along the bottom of the main chart moves the hairline; a "today" control returns to the real date. A note in small type states the simplifications (no atmospheric refraction adjustment beyond the standard −0.83° for sunrise/sunset, local solar time, no equation of time unless implemented). Typography: confident Swiss-style information design: a bold grotesk (Helvetica/Arial bold) for the headline number and title "A Year of Daylight" set large and tight in navy; small tabular numerals; axis labels in tracked caps at 10–11px; a three-sentence explanatory lede in a serif italic. Canvas for the charts with crisp DPR handling; smooth interpolation when switching cities (latitude eases to its target over 800ms, cubic-bezier(.3,0,.1,1)). Responsive: on phones the chart is full-width with the latitude control as a horizontal slider above it, secondary views stacked. Reduced motion: no intro sweep; latitude changes apply without easing.

Open The Drawloom — Cloth from a Diagram ↗
058

The Drawloom — Cloth from a Diagram

A quiet linen-paper workshop page laid out as a classical weaving draft: threading on top, tie-up in the corner, treadling down the right, and a large cloth below that is computed entirely from those three grids. On arrival a wooden shuttle is already flying across an indigo warp, laying linen picks that the reed beats into goose-eye diamonds while the pressed treadle and lifted shafts glow gold in the diagram, making the cause-and-effect of the notation visible. Every thread is rendered as rounded, twisted yarn with highlights, dive-under shadows and looped selvedges, and a brass linen-tester lens enlarges the interlacement and warns in red of floats too long to wear. The left column holds the masthead, loom transport, generators, yarn choices and a plain-language reading of the weave; the right column is a pattern book of eight historical structures, each a tiny computed swatch.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A generative weaving studio built on the weaver's traditional notation, the draft: a threading (which shaft each warp thread passes through), a tie-up (which shafts each treadle lifts) and a treadling (which treadle is pressed for each weft pick). From these three small grids the cloth (the drawdown) is completely determined. The viewer edits the grids or generates new drafts, and watches the cloth weave itself pick by pick in rendered yarn. Concept: a centuries-old visual programming language, made playable; pattern as computation. Mood: quiet workshop, linen and wool, patient mathematics. Palette: unbleached linen #EFE7D6 ground, indigo #1E3A5F, madder red #A8452C, weld gold #D9A441, iron-black #2A2A2A for grid lines and type. Light page with a woven-paper feel. Warp and weft colours are chosen from these five yarns (default indigo warp, linen weft). Layout (the classical draft layout, which is itself beautiful): cloth drawdown as the large central field; threading grid above it (8 shafts tall × 72 warp ends wide on a desktop, 96 on very wide screens, 48 on tablets, 28 on phones; the number of picks fills the available height); treadling grid to its right (8 treadles wide × picks tall); tie-up in the small square at the upper right where they meet (8×8). Thin iron-black grid lines; filled cells as solid ink squares; every 4th/8th line slightly heavier. Above the threading and beside the treadling are single-row colour bars for warp and weft colour sequences (click or drag to paint stripes with the chosen brush colour). To the left, a narrow column with title and controls; to the right, a narrow "pattern book" column of preset swatch cards (below the draft on medium screens); on phones the draft is scaled to the width and the controls and pattern book move below. Cloth rendering (Canvas): not flat squares: each interlacement drawn as yarn: where the warp is up, a vertical rounded segment of warp colour passes over the weft with a soft highlight along its centre and shadows at both ends where it dives under; where weft is up, a horizontal segment likewise; floats run continuously across cells; slight thickness variation per thread and yarn twist streaks; at the selvedges the weft visibly turns around the edge threads. Below the fell the bare warp ends hang unwoven and fade out as a fringe. A magnifier circle follows the pointer over the cloth showing the interlacement enlarged 4× in a brass linen-tester frame, with a small tag naming the end and pick under the glass; floats longer than 7 threads are flagged in red in the glass and the tag turns red ("float of 9 — will snag"). While the glass is over the cloth, the matching threading column and treadling row are outlined in madder. Signature moment: weaving. The cloth is not just displayed: it is woven: a shuttle (a small boat-shaped wooden shape trailing a weft thread) flies across the fell from side to side, one pick at a time; with each pick the corresponding treadling row lights up in weld gold, the shafts lifted by that treadle (per the tie-up) flash in the threading grid with their cells turning madder, the new weft row appears, and the reed beats it down with a small bounce of the cloth. Speed is adjustable from contemplative (2 picks per second) to a blur; any edit to the draft re-weaves from the changed row at a quick catch-up pace (or, if weaving is paused, repaints at once). On load, a handsome default draft (goose-eye, a 2/2 point twill in indigo and linen) starts weaving at a brisk pace so the first three seconds already show diamonds emerging row by row. Interaction: click/tap or drag in the threading, tie-up and treadling grids to toggle cells (threading enforces one shaft per warp end; treadling one treadle per pick, with a "several treadles to a pick" option); the cloth updates immediately. Preset drafts as labelled swatch cards with tiny previews: plain weave (tabby, checked four and four), 2/2 twill, herringbone, goose-eye, rosepath, a huck-lace-like texture, overshot "honeysuckle"-style figure, shadow weave with alternating colours. Generators: "mirror the threading" (point repeat), "tromp as writ" (treadle as threaded: copy threading to treadling), "random twill family", "advance" (advancing twill), each animating the grid cells as they change in a ripple. Colour: two yarn swatches each for warp and weft (tap a swatch to step through the five yarns) and striping sequences (solid, every other, 4×4 for checks/tartan-like setts, random stripe), plus a five-colour brush for painting the colour bars directly. A "colour / structure" toggle shows the drawdown as yarn colours versus black-and-white interlacement. Transport: pause/weave, "from the top". Export: "save swatch" as PNG via canvas, with a typeset caption. Information panel in small type: a plain-language sentence naming the weave if recognised ("this is a 1/3 broken twill on 4 shafts"), number of shafts and treadles used, repeat size, longest float (in red when over 7), a pick counter, and a short glossary sentence of five terms (warp, weft, shaft, treadle, float) whose definitions are revealed on hover or focus. Typography: title "The Drawloom" in a restrained old-style serif (Garamond-like stack: "Adobe Garamond Pro", "EB Garamond", Georgia) with a subtitle in small caps: "cloth from a diagram"; UI labels in small tracked sans caps; numbers tabular; shaft and treadle numbers along the grid edges in tiny figures, with end and pick counts every eighth line. Optional sound toggle (off by default): the soft wooden clack of shafts and the thump of the beater in time with the weaving, synthesised from filtered noise bursts. Keyboard: grids and colour bars are focusable with a cell cursor (arrows + Space). Reduced motion: cloth renders complete without the shuttle animation.

Open Pluvia & Sons — Umbrella Makers ↗
059

Pluvia & Sons — Umbrella Makers

A grey, overcast landing page for a fifth-generation umbrella maker on which it is actually raining: several hundred simulated drops fall in three depth layers across the whole viewport, splash on the top edges of the paper panels and the pavement, and blur and drain the colour from everything they fall on. The viewer holds the only bright thing on the page, a canary-yellow umbrella drawn in quarter view, which replaces the cursor. Rain bursts on its canopy, beads slide down the panels to the rib tips and fall in a steady drip, and beneath it a soft circle of the page is dry: sharp, full-contrast, and in colour. Reading the page means sheltering it. The copy is dry in the other sense: a headline that says it is raining in Garrow and usually is, an exploded drawing of the Garrow No. 4 that assembles itself when scrolled into view, a house catalogue of eleven grades of rain, a ruled price list for repairs, the shop's hours ("closed on dry days, i.e. the 3rd of August"), a note from Odalys Pluvia, and a footer wordmark standing at the edge of a puddle that reflects it upside-down and ripples when drops land.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A landing page for an invented umbrella maker, "Pluvia & Sons, est. 1871", in the rainy invented city of "Garrow". It is raining on the page: real simulated rain falls across the whole viewport, over the text and pictures; and the viewer's cursor is an open umbrella. Rain hits the umbrella's canopy, runs off its edge in beads, and the area beneath stays dry; text sheltered by it becomes crisp and readable in full contrast while the rest of the page is seen through streaks. Concept: the product demonstrated by the interface itself. Mood: wet-pavement melancholy with dry wit and one bright colour. Palette: rain grey #8E9AA3 as the page ground (overcast sky colour-field), wet slate #2C3338 for text and illustration, canary yellow #FFD400 as the single accent (the umbrella, key words, buttons), and dry white #F2F2EE for paper panels and highlights. Grey colour-field page, neither dark nor white. Rain system (full-viewport canvas above the content, pointer-events none): 400–900 drops depending on viewport, drawn as short slanted streaks with slight wind sway and depth layers (thin fast far drops, thicker slow near ones); drops that reach the "ground" of each content block's top edge or the bottom of the viewport make tiny splash crowns (two or three pixels arcing up) and ripple rings; drops that hit the umbrella canopy (an arc collision test against the canopy's upper curve) burst into small droplets that slide down along the canopy curve to the rib tips and drip from the eight points in a steady beaded fall, so a visible dry cone exists beneath. The intensity cycles slowly between drizzle and downpour, with a rare gust that slants everything. The umbrella cursor: a custom drawn yellow umbrella (top-quarter view: scalloped canopy with eight panels and ribs, a ferrule, a slim shaft and a curved malacca handle) that follows the pointer with a slight springy lag and tilts into its direction of travel; on touch devices it follows the finger and, when not touching, rests where last placed (and drifts slowly on its own tour of the page until first touch). Clicking (or, on touch, tapping the umbrella itself; a tap elsewhere moves it there) closes and reopens it with a snappy two-stage animation (ribs folding): while it is closed, everything gets rained on. Under the umbrella, a circular "dry" region (CSS mask or clip tracked to the pointer) reveals the dry version of the page: text at full contrast, illustrations in yellow and white; outside it, the wet version: text slightly blurred/greyed with running-water distortion (a subtle SVG displacement filter or layered streak overlay), so the viewer naturally reads the page by sheltering it. The page remains fully legible and usable without the effect (contrast outside the dry circle still passes readability; a "Come inside" toggle, fixed at the top right, stops the rain entirely and puts the umbrella away for those who prefer; a small live readout in the hero names the grade of rain currently falling). Content: 1) Hero: giant headline in a tight condensed grotesk, slate on grey: "IT IS RAINING IN GARROW." second line in yellow: "It usually is." Sub-line: "Pluvia & Sons have kept the city dry, one head at a time, since 1871." A row of pedestrians with umbrellas (SVG silhouettes) walks across the horizon line, one of them yellow. 2) "The Garrow No. 4": the flagship umbrella shown as an exploded technical illustration (canopy, eight ribs of solid carbon steel, stretchers, runner, shaft of a single piece of ash, handle) with numbered callouts and specifics: 61 cm ribs, 420 g, canopy woven at 190 threads per inch, "tested in a car wash on Finch Street". 3) "A Short Catalogue of Rain": the house's eleven grades of rain as a typographic list, each with a one-line verdict on whether an umbrella helps and a tiny canvas vignette of that rain (a still frame at rest, looping when hovered, focused or tapped): mizzle, smirr, a steady soak, stair-rods, sideways, "the kind that gets in at the cuffs". 4) Repairs: "We mend what we make, and several things we did not" with prices in a plain ruled table and a lifetime re-covering promise. 5) The shop: address at 14 Tolhouse Arcade, opening hours ("closed on dry days, i.e. the 3rd of August"), a note from the fifth-generation maker Odalys Pluvia. Footer: puddle at the bottom of the page in which the footer text is reflected upside-down and rippled when drops land. Signature moment: bringing the umbrella over the hero headline for the first time: the rain audibly (if sound is on) and visibly drums on the canopy, beads race off the points, and the words beneath sharpen and dry. Typography: headline in Impact/"Arial Narrow"/"Helvetica Neue Condensed" bold caps; body in a sturdy serif (Georgia) at comfortable size; labels in tracked small caps; prices in tabular figures. Optional sound toggle (off by default; drawn as a small cloud that rains): steady rain from filtered noise plus a distinct patter that rises when drops hit the umbrella. Reduced motion: light static drizzle texture, no splashes, no cursor lag; the dry-circle reveal remains. Performance: single canvas, typed arrays, DPR capped at 2, pause when hidden.

Open TIDE.TXT ↗
060

TIDE.TXT

A sheet of green-bar fan-fold listing paper steps up out of a dark platen, a print head sweeps a JCL job banner and a huge "#" banner title across it, and then the rest of the sheet prints a seascape that will not hold still: a horizon, cloud wisps, a lighthouse on its rock, a little sailing boat, a bell buoy and a beach, every one of them made of typewriter characters and recomputed each frame from a perspective wave field in two ribbon colours, black for faces, crests and foam and sea-green for deep water. Moving the pointer is the weather: down for a glassy calm with mirror reflections, up for a gale with towering slashes, slanting rain, inverted-sheet lightning and a typed thunderclap, sideways for the wind. Whatever the viewer types falls from the sky into the water and rides the swell as a legible sentence, each letter on its own column of the wave, until it washes up as a tide line of their words. A footer in the voice of a shipping forecast for "Greenbar Bight" reprints itself in place as conditions change.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A typographic experiment: a living sea rendered entirely in typewriter characters on continuous-feed green-bar printer paper. Swell, breaking crests, foam, spray, rain, a horizon, a small boat, gulls and a lighthouse are all made of glyphs chosen by density and shape, recomputed every frame from a wave simulation, and printed in monospace. Concept: the oldest computer art form given real fluid motion and typographic care. Mood: wry, nostalgic, mesmerising; a mainframe daydreaming about the coast. Palette: paper white #F3F6EC with alternating pale green bars #D6E8D0 (every three text rows, as on fan-fold listing paper), ink black #1B1B1B for characters (and for the printer platen seen behind the paper and through the sprocket holes), and sea-green ink #1F6F5C as a second ribbon colour used for the deep water glyphs and headings. Light page. Paper details: tractor-feed sprocket holes down both margins (one per three lines), perforation lines at the sheet edges, a faint pre-printed line number column on both sides and the tiny legend "FORM 1403-A" set vertically in the margin; ribbon unevenness (ink knocked back in soft vertical bands and specks) and a barely misaligned row now and then. Rendering: one fixed character grid sized to the viewport and filling the whole sheet (about 150-200 columns x 56-66 rows on desktop; 54-60 columns on phones with larger glyphs), rendered to a canvas in a monospace stack (Menlo, Consolas, "Courier New"). Every glyph is cached once on an offscreen atlas in each ink (black, overstruck bold black, green, bold green, faint black, faint green, and paper-coloured for inversion) and blitted per cell. For each scene cell compute a value and choose a glyph: sky: blank, with sparse "." and "'" for distant rain and "~" wisps for clouds built from slow noise; horizon line of "_" and "-"; sea: a height field from five summed, sharpened, wind-skewed travelling waves in perspective; glyph by slope and depth: troughs "_" "." ",", rising faces "/" and backs "\", crests "^" "~" "=", deep water in green ":" ";" "=" "#" by darkness, breaking crests and foam "@" "%" "&" "*" "o" "°" decaying to "." as foam dissipates; spray flung upward as "'" "`" "," particles. Nearer waves are taller, longer and use bolder glyphs (perspective by row, with tall near water displacing what is behind it), giving real depth. Shore at the bottom rows: waves run up a beach of ":" and "." with a foam fringe and recede leaving a wet line of "_" that dries. Objects in glyphs: a small boat (a "\____|____/" hull, a "|" mast and a stack of ")" sails) that rides the swell, pitching by choosing different sprites according to local slope and mirroring with the wind; a lighthouse on a rock at the right built from bracket, bar and "#" characters with green "=" bands, and at night a beam of "-" and "=" characters sweeping across the sky and a starburst when it points at the viewer; three gulls ("v" and "~v~") gliding by day; a bell buoy "(o)" that prints DING when it rocks; occasionally a whale spout "( )" far off; a sun placed by the real hour, or a moon at night. Interaction: pointer/finger position controls the weather: left-right is wind direction and strength (waves lean and steepen, spray flies, rain slants, the boat turns), up-down is sea state from flat calm at the bottom (a glassy sheet of "_" and "-" with a perfect reflection of the lighthouse and the boat) to full gale at the top (towering faces of "/" and "\" and foam, rain filling the sky as streaks, lightning flashing the whole sheet to inverted colours for a frame, a typed "!" thunderclap). Clicking or tapping drops a stone: it falls, and rings of "(" ")" "-" "_" spread on the water (a stone thrown at a gull gets "AWK!"). Typing any characters on the keyboard launches them as flotsam: the typed characters fall into the sea and bob on the waves as themselves in a small clearing of water, drifting ashore to form a tide line of the viewer's words on the beach (the newest three lines are kept; older ones dry out and crumble to dots): the signature moment: your sentence riding the swell, legible, rising and falling letter by letter. Enter ends a line, Backspace takes a letter back. Before the viewer types, the machine casts "WISH YOU WERE HERE" itself, and "HELLO, WORLD" is already lying on the beach. Time of day follows the real clock (the paper stays light; at night the sky fills with "*" and "." stars and the lighthouse beam operates), with a printed on-paper button and the F2 key (labelled PF2, mainframe style, so that every letter key stays free for casting) to toggle the watch. Typographic frame: the page is presented as a printout: a header block printed at the top in caps as a job banner: "//TIDE JOB (SEA),'COAST WATCH',CLASS=A", date and time of the real clock ticking in place, then a ruled line of "=" and the title in huge block letters made of "#" characters in green ink (a banner font defined in the page: "TIDE.TXT", scaled to fill the sheet width), with a small printed glyph legend beside it where there is room. Beneath the sea, a printed status footer updates in place like a line printer, with a visible print head: "WIND SW 6 · SEA STATE 5 · SWELL 2.4 M · VIS GOOD · BOAT: HOLDING", a shipping-forecast-style line for the invented sea area Greenbar Bight that changes with the weather, and a flotsam count. The designed hint is a printed line: "MOVE TO CHANGE THE WEATHER. TYPE TO CAST WORDS ADRIFT." On touch devices and narrow sheets a small on-paper text field (CAST>) lets the viewer cast words. Entrance: the paper feeds up from the bottom with a stepping motion and the header prints left to right line by line (a print-head sweep), then the sea prints row by row and starts moving. Optional sound toggle (off by default; printed button or F4, labelled PF4): surf from filtered noise that follows the sea state, a dot-matrix buzz whenever the print head is working, a buoy bell, a stone plop and thunder. Reduced motion: no feed or print animation, the sea updates twice a second with small amplitude; no lightning. Performance: glyph atlas cached on an offscreen canvas and blitted per cell for speed; grid capped; device pixel ratio capped at 2; paused when hidden.

Open The Moon Tonight ↗
061

The Moon Tonight

A quiet nocturnal instrument: one cratered Moon hanging in a sparse field of fixed stars on night black, shown at the phase it has at this very minute. Beneath it the phase name is set in very large, light serif type, followed by one italic line of copy written for that phase and a single ruled row of tabular data (illumination, age, date and time). A small column lists the next four principal phases, with the coming full moon in harvest gold; along the bottom edge a tape of day-by-day moon glyphs slides under a fixed index, today ringed in gold. Dragging the tape sweeps the terminator across real relief: crater rims catch the light, floors fill with shadow, the old Moon in earthshine appears around new, and every readout ticks along. The page opens in darkness, the lit limb arrives as a hairline, the light sweeps to the true phase and the title letters settle into place.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A single-purpose tool: it shows the Moon as it is right now: the phase computed from the viewer's actual date and time: as a large, beautifully shaded cratered sphere, with the illuminated fraction, the age of the Moon in days, the name of the phase, and the dates of the next new, first-quarter, full and last-quarter moons. Concept: a quiet instrument you would keep open in a tab; one object in a field of emptiness. Astronomy (computed in the page; no external data): phase from the mean synodic month (29.530588853 days) counted from a known new moon epoch (2000 January 6, 18:14 UTC, Julian date 2451550.1), which is accurate to within several hours: state on the page that phase times are approximate ("to within half a day"); illuminated fraction k = (1 − cos(phase angle)) / 2; age in days; phase names by eighths (new, waxing crescent, first quarter, waxing gibbous, full, waning gibbous, last quarter, waning crescent). Optionally apply the main periodic correction terms if the implementer is sure of them; otherwise keep the mean model and the stated tolerance. Mood: still, nocturnal, reverent, precise. Palette: night black #0A0A0D background (required by the subject), moonlight ivory #E8E4D8, shadowed regolith grey #8F8B80, and one warm accent, harvest gold #F2C879, used only for the "today" marker and the next-full-moon date. Dark page with nothing decorative: no gradients, no glow halos beyond a very faint, physically motivated bloom around the lit limb; a sparse field of tiny fixed stars that are occulted by the Moon's dark side. The Moon (signature object): a sphere rendered per-pixel on Canvas 2D (or a WebGL fragment shader with a Canvas fallback) at a diameter of about 60% of the shorter viewport side (up to 70% on phones, where there is room): for each pixel inside the disc compute the sphere normal, apply the sun direction for the current phase angle, and shade with a lunar-like (flatter than Lambert) falloff so the full moon looks evenly bright to the limb while the terminator is sharp; surface from procedural data: a height field of many craters of varying radius generated from a fixed seed (bowl, raised rim, central peak for the largest, bright ray systems for a few young ones), plus large dark maria placed as soft-edged irregular blotches in a plausible arrangement (an evocation, not an accurate map: do not label real features), with normal perturbation and a row-wise horizon sweep for true cast shadows, so craters near the terminator cast long shadows and stand out in relief exactly as through a telescope, while near the limb they foreshorten. Earthshine: the dark side is faintly visible (very dim blue-grey) around new moon. The orientation shows a waxing moon lit from the right (northern-hemisphere view), with a small N/S toggle that flips it for southern observers. Layout: the Moon centred slightly above middle; beneath it, in very large thin type, the phase name ("Waning Gibbous"); below, a single line of small tabular data separated by thin rules: illumination "83.4%" · age "18.2 days" · the real date. At the bottom edge: a month strip built as a sliding tape under a fixed centre index: small moons, one per calendar day (about thirty across a desktop, fewer on a phone), each correctly shaded as a simple two-arc glyph with its day number, with today ringed in gold and the four principal phases dated beneath; the strip can be dragged (with a little momentum), wheeled or arrow-keyed (one day; Shift for six hours) to scrub through time: the signature moment: as the viewer scrubs, the big Moon's terminator sweeps smoothly across the cratered surface, shadows lengthening and shortening in real relief, and the readouts tick; releasing leaves it at that date with a "return to tonight" control (gold dot). A "play a month" control runs one full lunation in about 12 seconds. Details: hovering/touching a point on the lit surface shows the local sun elevation there ("the Sun is 6° above this horizon: long shadows"); a line of quiet copy that changes with the phase, written with care (new: "Nothing to see. It is up there all day, lost beside the Sun."; full: "Rises at sunset, sets at sunrise, keeps the whole night."; thin crescent: "Look west after sunset. The dark part is lit by us."); next-phase list in a small column: "Full · 24 Nov" etc. computed from the model; "moonlight mode" key (M, or its button) hides all text for a pure view and floats the Moon to the centre; any tap or key returns. Further keys: Space plays a month, T returns to tonight, H flips the hemisphere. The browser tab title and icon carry the live phase. Typography: one family used with extreme weight contrast: phase name in a light, elegant serif at huge size (Didot/"Bodoni MT"/Baskerville/Georgia, weight normal, tight tracking) in ivory; data in 11–12px tracked small caps sans and tabular numerals in grey; nothing else. Entrance: black; the Moon's lit limb appears as a hairline and the light sweeps across to the correct phase in about two seconds while the title letters settle in. Reduced motion: the Moon appears at the correct phase immediately; scrubbing updates without easing. Performance: craters baked once into a height/normal buffer; per-frame shading only when the phase changes; DPR capped at 2.

Open Wheat, Before Rain ↗
062

Wheat, Before Rain

A full-bleed painted landscape in gold and slate: two-thirds of the screen is a field of ripe wheat receding to a hazy horizon of hills, hedgerow trees, a spire and a lone oak, under stacked flat-shaded storm clouds with a cream sun-break and light shafts. After one still beat a gust enters from the left and the whole field comes alive: every stalk is its own spring, leaning and overshooting as bands of wind sweep through in perspective, turning the crop silver-pale where it bends and dark just ahead, so the wind itself becomes visible as waves rolling away. In the foreground, individual ears with paired grains and trembling awns nod above olive grass, a few poppies and cornflowers; swallows scatter from a telegraph wire when a gust reaches them and a kite circles. Over four minutes a dark bank slides in, the light narrows, the caption counts down, heavy drops streak and spot the wheat, thunder mutters, and the weather eases back into bright wind to begin again. The experience is meant to feel like standing at a field margin, expectant and tender, with the option of putting a hand out into the wheat.

↗ Open

Original prompt

An ambient scene: a wide field of ripe wheat under a heavy sky, a few minutes before a summer storm. Wind moves through the field in visible waves; each of thousands of stalks bends individually. Full-bleed Canvas 2D. Concept: the sight of wind made visible by a crop; golden light against slate cloud. Mood: expectant, golden, vast, tender. Palette: ripe wheat gold #E9C46A, shadowed straw ochre #B8862B, hedgerow/field-margin olive #5B6C3A, storm-cloud slate blue #6E7F91 (sky, and the cornflowers), and pale straw-cream #F7EFD6 for highlights, the sun-break and type; darker tones are mixes of these. Light, warm page: the field fills the lower two-thirds and glows; the sky is slate but luminous near the horizon, not dark. Scene construction: the field is drawn as many rows of stalks from the horizon (tiny, dense, pale, hazy) to the foreground (large, detailed, darker at the base), ordered back to front; about 8,000 stalks on desktop, about 3,400 on phones, with the nearest 120–270 drawn in detail: a curved stem (quadratic curve), a dry leaf, and an ear with paired grains and fine awns (bristles) splaying out, which sway with extra delay. Middle-distance stalks carry simpler spindle ears with awn hints; far stalks are single strokes. Each stalk bends according to a wind field sampled at its ground position: the bend is a curve whose tip displaces downwind, with per-stalk stiffness and phase variation and a spring so stalks overshoot and recover. The wind field: large travelling gust fronts (bands of higher wind speed that sweep across the field in perspective from one side, at varying angles), layered with smaller turbulence noise; where a gust front passes, stalks lean over and their colour shifts lighter (the silvery underside/sheen of bent wheat), with a darker shoulder just ahead of the front: this produces the famous dark-and-light waves rolling to the horizon. The same field tints a soft ground layer under the stalks. Cloud shadows also drift across the field independently, dimming the gold in soft patches, and one patch of sunlight breaks through and travels with the gap in the clouds. Faint paired tramlines converge on the vanishing point. Sky and details: layered storm clouds built from soft shapes with flat-ish shading (a graphic, slightly painterly treatment rather than photoreal: stacked rounded cloud forms in three slate tones with a bright rim where the sun is behind, and light shafts falling from the sun-break), slowly advancing; a distant rain curtain as fine slanted lines under one cloud on the horizon; far hills, a line of far trees with a small church spire, and one lone oak; a telegraph line with a few swallows that take off when the gust hits and later return to the wire; poppies (small red dots, #C8402B, used very sparingly) and cornflowers among olive grass at the near margin; chaff and a few seed heads blown across the foreground in strong gusts; a red kite wheeling. A fine paper grain lies over everything. Over about four minutes the storm approaches: a dark cloud bank moves in, the light narrows, gusts strengthen, the first heavy drops fall as sparse long streaks and darken spots, a low thunder with a soft sheet-lightning flicker (thunder audible if sound is on), then the cycle eases back to bright wind: an endless five-minute loop with weather. Interaction: watch-only is complete. Moving the pointer/finger across the field sends a gust in that direction and with that speed from that position (a brushing hand through the wheat: the stalks beneath part and spring back); a quick swipe makes a strong squall line that travels to the horizon: the signature moment: a single dark-light wave racing away across thousands of stalks into the distance. Click/tap startles a skylark out of the wheat: it rises vertically, singing (a rising trill if sound is on, drawn as small fading arcs either way), hovers, and parachutes back down. Keyboard: G sends a squall, L raises a lark. The pointer is a small cream ring that sheds short wind streaks. Typography: almost none: a small caption block in the top-left of the sky in an old-style serif italic (Garamond-like stack/Georgia italic), cream, 13–14px: "Wheat, before rain." and beneath in tiny tracked caps a slowly updating line of observations, beginning "STILL AIR" and then "WIND SOUTH-WEST, FRESHENING · 4 MINUTES TO RAIN", counting down with the cycle through "ANY MOMENT NOW", "FIRST HEAVY DROPS", "RAIN PASSING EAST" and "LIGHT RETURNING". That line is also a quiet button ("hurry it on" appears on hover/focus) that runs the weather forward to the edge of the rain, or on past it. A one-line italic hint about the gestures fades in after the entrance and fades away. A minimal designed sound toggle (off by default) drawn as a wheat ear that lifts, fills gold and opens its awns when on: wind through wheat (band-passed noise shaped by gust strength, with a dry rustle layer), a whoosh for squalls, rain patter, distant thunder, skylark trill. Entrance: the field at rest in still air for one beat, then the first big gust enters from the left and rolls through to the horizon within about three seconds. Performance: stalks stored in typed arrays; far stalks drawn as batched single strokes per colour bucket within depth bands; near stalks individually; wind field sampled from a low-res grid that is denser toward the horizon; clouds, oak and horizon pre-rendered as sprites; DPR capped at 2; adaptive stalk count; pause when hidden. Reduced motion: a gentle uniform sway at low amplitude, no racing gust fronts, flying chaff, rain streaks or lightning.

Open The Astrolabe ↗
063

The Astrolabe

A history-of-science vitrine with the glass taken away. A large engraved brass astrolabe lies on a lamplit parchment table beside a column of museum text headed by the cinnabar exhibit number IV.12 and the invitation "Please touch." The instrument is lowered onto the table, its shackle swings, and the rete makes one slow turn before stopping at the present hour with the rule laid through the Sun's bead, so that at rest it is already telling the time. Every circle on it is real stereographic geometry for the chosen latitude, and a label-card panel reads the instrument back in plain language as the visitor turns it: the Sun's place in the zodiac, its height, the hour under the rule, the degree rising and the degree culminating. Three numbered tasks teach sunrise, day length and culmination step by step, ticking themselves off as the visitor succeeds. On request the whole instrument separates into a calm axonometric stack of pin, horse, rule, rete, plate and mater, each named and explained, and then settles back together.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A museum exhibit of a planispheric astrolabe, the analogue computer of the medieval sky, drawn in SVG as a richly engraved brass instrument whose parts the visitor can turn and take apart. The geometry is real stereographic projection computed in the page (tropics and equator as concentric circles; almucantars: circles of equal altitude: and azimuth arcs for a chosen latitude on the plate; the ecliptic as an off-centre circle on the rete). The star pointers on the rete are presented as an illustrative, stylised set (about a dozen flame-shaped pointers labelled with decorative invented names in the exhibit's own cartouche style, and the label says plainly that the star positions on this demonstration model are schematic), so no inaccurate star data is claimed. Concept: let people handle an instrument they would never be allowed to touch. Mood: scholarly, warm, golden-age-of-instruments; a vitrine in a history-of-science museum. Palette: vellum/parchment gallery ground #EADFC6, brass in two tones: shadow brass #8A6A2F and bright brass #C8A951 (with a pale highlight #E9D79A), ink blue-black #1F2A30 for type and engraving shadows, cinnabar red #7B2D26 for the rule's index line, numerals of note and the exhibit number. Light page with soft drop shadows as if the instrument lies on a parchment-covered table under a lamp. The instrument (layered SVG, each layer independently transformable, with engraved line work: hairlines, double rings, degree scales with ticks at 1°, 5°, 10°, numbers every 10° or 15° rotated along the circle): (1) the mater: the body with a raised limb engraved with a 360° scale and the 24 hours (two sets of I–XII), and the throne at the top with a decorative openwork design and a suspension ring (shackle) that swings slightly; (2) the plate (tympan) for latitude: stereographic almucantars every 5° or 10° from horizon to zenith, azimuth arcs, the horizon line, twilight line, and unequal-hour lines below the horizon; three plates available for latitudes 30°, 40° and 52°: switching them lifts the rete, swaps the plate with a slide, and sets everything back; (3) the rete: the pierced star map: a network of curved brass straps with flame/leaf-shaped star pointers, the ecliptic ring divided into twelve 30° signs marked by engraved zodiac names in small capitals and degree ticks; ornate but legible; (4) the rule: a straight double-ended arm with a declination scale, pivoting at the centre; (5) the pin and the "horse" wedge that holds it all together. Interaction: drag the rete to rotate it (the daily turning of the heavens): it turns with weight and a little inertia, and a faint ratchet tick; drag the rule separately. A live panel reads the instrument as the visitor works it: the visitor chooses a date (the page finds the Sun's place on the ecliptic from the date with the standard approximate longitude formula) and the instrument shows the Sun's point on the ecliptic with a small red bead; rotating the rete until that point reaches the eastern horizon line shows the time of sunrise read off the limb by the rule: the panel explains in plain steps ("1. Find today on the ecliptic: 17° Scorpio. 2. Turn the rete until that point touches the horizon in the east. 3. Lay the rule through it. 4. Read the limb: about 7:10."). A "Set to now" control turns the rete to the current real local time for the chosen plate, so the instrument shows which part of the ecliptic is rising at this moment. "Take it apart" (signature moment): the instrument explodes in a slow, precise axonometric separation: pin and horse withdraw, the rule lifts, the rete rises and tilts, the plate slides out, each part casting its own shadow and gaining a label with a leader line and a one-sentence explanation, then reassembles on request in reverse order with soft brass-on-brass settles. Exhibit apparatus: a left column of museum text: object label ("Planispheric astrolabe (demonstration model). Brass, engraved. After instruments made between the 10th and 16th centuries across the Islamic world and Europe."), a three-paragraph wall text, plain and accurate: what it did (tell time by Sun or stars, find the direction and times for prayer, survey heights, cast horoscopes, teach astronomy), why the latitude plate must match the observer, why the map looks strange (the sky projected from the south pole onto the equator's plane). "Try it" tasks as three numbered cards: find sunrise; find how long today is; find what is culminating now. Exhibit number in red "IV.12". Typography: wall text in a dignified book serif (Baskerville/Palatino/Georgia), title in spaced capitals; engraved lettering on the instrument in small roman capitals following the curves (SVG textPath) with varying sizes; numerals engraved in an old-style manner. Entrance: the instrument is lowered onto the table (scale and shadow settle), the shackle swings, and the rete makes one slow full turn before coming to rest at "now": the first three seconds. Hover states: parts glow slightly brighter brass with a thin red outline when grabbable; cursor changes to an open/closed hand drawn in SVG. Keyboard: Tab to rete or rule, arrows rotate by 1° (Shift 15°). Touch: one-finger drag rotates the nearest part. Reduced motion: no intro turn, no inertia, explode view cross-fades. Responsive: on phones the instrument takes the full width with the reading panel beneath; text labels on the instrument thin out below 400px. As built: almucantars are engraved every 5° (heavier every 10°), azimuth arcs every 15°, limb degree numerals every 10°; there are twelve star pointers; the rule is counter-changed so that its red index edge runs through the centre; the date is chosen with day and month steppers; the reading panel offers the three tasks as tabs with a "Show me" control that works the instrument through the chosen task, and the rete and rule settle gently onto the horizon, the Sun's point or the meridian when released close to them; the ratchet tick and the brass settles are heard only once the visitor switches sound on; the suspension ring can be flicked; on phones the exploded view labels carry names only and the one-sentence explanations move into the panel beneath.

Open Par Avion — The Journey of One Letter ↗
064

Par Avion — The Journey of One Letter

A single airmail envelope sits at the centre of a kraft-paper stage and never leaves it. As the viewer scrolls, nine flat, duotone postal scenes conveyor past behind it — a rain-streaked pillar box, a cancelling machine, the pigeonholes of a night mail train, an aerodrome, a desert stopover, a flying boat, a colonial G.P.O. counter, a mail boat and a cliff-road bicycle, a schoolhouse — while a top ribbon traces the route on an invented map and a ticker runs the local date and time. The envelope is the travelogue: each stage physically strikes, sticks, stains or creases it, with handstamps that drop in, thump and lift away leaving patchy, slightly doubled ink that dries from wet to matte. Captions on airmail-edged cards speak in the dry voice of a public-information film. At the end the envelope turns over, a paper-knife opens it, and the letter it has been carrying the whole way unfolds: a brother writing to his sister about rain, a ship's cat, an honest kettle and their mother's knee. Scrolling back up un-stamps every mark and sends the letter home.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A scroll-driven story following a single airmail letter from a pillar box in an invented northern harbour town to a village on the other side of the world, in 1961. The envelope stays fixed at the centre of the viewport for the whole page; scrolling moves it through the postal system, and the envelope itself accumulates the evidence: postmarks, cancellation waves, transit stamps, a customs label, a redirect sticker, a crease, a rain blot, a salt-water stain, a thumbprint, a clerk's pencil. Concept: an object that is its own travelogue. Mood: tender, bureaucratic, wistful, quietly funny. Palette: kraft/manila sorting-office ground #D9C4A0, airmail blue #2A3D66, airmail red #C8102E, envelope paper cream #F7F1E3, graphite ink #3B3B3B (plus tints of these, and one violet ink for the desert transit mark). Light page. Texture: fibrous envelope paper, slightly uneven ink, the red-and-blue lozenge airmail border around the envelope edge, a fine paper grain over the scenes. The envelope (large SVG, centre stage, about 70% of viewport width on desktop and 90% on phones, gently rotating a degree or two as it "moves", and tilting very slightly in 3D toward the mouse): hand-addressed in a script-style italic stack ("Snell Roundhand", "Segoe Script", "Bradley Hand", "Brush Script MT", cursive) in blue-black ink: "Miss Ottilie Varga, The Schoolhouse, Kestrel Bay, via Port Halcyon, Southern Ocean Territory" (all invented); a "PAR AVION / BY AIR MAIL" etiquette label; two invented postage stamps drawn in SVG with perforated edges (one showing a lighthouse in blue engraving style, value 1/3; one a tern in flight in red, value 6d; issuing country invented: "NORDHAVN"); return address on the flap side ("T. Varga, 3 Net Loft Row, Skerrigate, Nordhavn"), where a violet Port Halcyon G.P.O. backstamp and a pencilled "1/9 pd." are also waiting to be found. Stages (each a scroll segment with a scene illustrated in flat duotone-ish SVG behind/around the envelope, as vignettes at the left and right edges on wide screens and as a window above the envelope on phones; scenes slide to the next with a horizontal conveyor motion; a caption block in the lower third; and marks added to the envelope): 1) The pillar box in Skerrigate, 5:45 p.m. collection: rain; the letter falls from above into frame and picks up a rain blot on the address. 2) The sorting office: a cancelling machine, a clock at 6.15: the first postmark: a circular date stamp "SKERRIGATE · 14 MAR 61 · 6.15 PM" with seven wavy lines across the stamps. 3) The night mail train: a travelling post office, pigeonholes, telegraph poles passing the window; the letter is flicked and gains a sorter's thumbprint. 4) The aerodrome (Halvard Field): sacks, a propeller airliner, a control tower; a blue oval "AIR TRANSIT" stamp. 5) A stopover on a desert airfield: a violet hexagonal transit mark "RAS EL-TAYYIB · TRANSIT" and a customs label slapped on. 6) The flying boat down the coast: a salt-water stain and a crease across one corner. 7) Port Halcyon G.P.O.: the address is found insufficient: a red pointing-hand stamp "NOT KNOWN AT THIS ADDRESS — TRY KESTREL BAY", then a clerk's pencilled note and underlining. 8) The weekly mail boat, then the postman's bicycle along the cliff road: a "REDIRECTED" sticker and a boxed "SHIP MAIL · M.V. PETREL" stamp. 9) Delivered: a final receiving postmark "KESTREL BAY · 2 APR 61", and the letter is opened. Signature moment: every postmark is physically stamped. At each stamping trigger, a handstamp (seen from above: a die plate in the shape of the mark, with a black knob) descends into view, strikes the envelope with a thump (a fast scale-down with a tiny envelope recoil and paper bounce), and lifts away, leaving the mark with uneven ink (SVG turbulence mask for patchy coverage, slightly doubled edge on one side, random small rotation), which then "dries" from wet-dark to matte over a second. Labels are slapped on, blots spread, the crease draws itself, the pencil note writes itself. Scrolling back up brings the handstamp back to lift each mark off in reverse (the letter un-travels). By the end the envelope is a dense, beautiful collage of thirteen marks, creases and labels; a counter in the corner tallies "19 days · 17,400 km · 9 pairs of hands". Finale: the envelope turns over (3D flip), a paper-knife slits the top fold, the flap opens to show a blue cross-hatched lining, and the letter slides out and unfolds in three panels: three paragraphs of handwritten-style text that the viewer has been carrying all along: a warm, specific, slightly comic letter from a brother to his sister about the harbour, a new kettle, their mother's knee and a ship's cat, ending with a line that lands gently. Then a small line: "She replied on the 4th. It took 23 days." and a "Post it again" button that un-travels the whole journey back to the start. Furniture: a route ribbon fixed at the top: a dotted great-circle-like line across a simplified invented map strip with nine place dots lighting up (each dot is a button that jumps to its stage), and a tiny envelope/train/aeroplane/boat/bicycle glyph that changes by stage; a date and local-time ticker that runs through each stage; a running tally; captions in a typewriter face (Courier/"American Typewriter") on small cream cards with a blue-red airmail edge, 30–50 words each, in the voice of a Post Office public-information film ("Seventeen people will handle your letter. None of them will read it. One of them will bend it."). Typography: titles in a 1960s public-service sans (Gill Sans/"Gill Sans MT"/"Trebuchet MS", bold caps tracked), script for handwriting, typewriter for captions. Opening screen: the title "PAR AVION" in airmail blue between rows of red and blue lozenges, each letter struck into place, subtitle "the journey of one letter", and a cue: a small stamp-shaped scroll indicator. A hidden detail: pressing and holding the envelope holds it up to the light and shows the folded letter inside. Optional sound toggle (off by default): handstamp thumps, rain, train rhythm, propeller drone, sea, gulls: all synthesised. Reduced motion: marks fade in without the handstamp animation; scenes cross-fade; the envelope cross-fades to its back instead of flipping. Responsive: scenes simplify on phones; the envelope remains the hero.

Open Ripple Tank ↗
065

Ripple Tank

A bright, clinical lab-manual page built around a living ripple tank. A framed tray of pale aqua water sits on a white bench with corner screws, legs and an apparatus shelf beneath it; numbered experiment tabs run across the top like headings in a practical handbook, and a side column carries the experiment note, one live formula, the frequency control and the probe plot. The page opens on the double slit: a plane wave marches from a red bar, strikes a lab-blue wall, and two fans of wavelets cross into alternating beams of glittering crests and calm nodal lanes, while red hand-drawn arrows name the central maximum and a nodal line and the plot beside the tank grows a row of fringes under thin red prediction ticks. Everything in the tank can be dragged, rotated, resized or thrown out, so each textbook diagram can be bent into another one.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A virtual ripple tank: the classroom apparatus in which waves in a shallow tray of water are lit from above so their crests and troughs project as bright and dark bands. Here it is a real-time 2D wave-equation simulation in WebGL (ping-pong float textures, hand-written shaders) with draggable wave sources and barriers, rendered as shimmering light on a white lab bench. The viewer builds classic experiments: single-slit diffraction, double-slit interference, reflection from a curved mirror, refraction over a shallow region. Concept: textbook diagrams turned into living water. Mood: clinical, bright, lucid; a well-lit physics lab. Palette: white #FFFFFF bench and page, clear cyan #00B4D8 for wave light, deep lab blue #023E8A for troughs, type and apparatus outlines, and signal red #E63946 for sources, measurement cursors and annotations. Light page: the tank itself is pale: crests render as near-white caustic brightness, troughs as blue shade, on a faint aqua base (#EAF8FC). No dark mode. Simulation: discretised 2D wave equation with damping on a grid of 512×320 (384×240 on phones): two-step update u_next = 2u − u_prev + c²·∇²u, with a per-cell wave-speed map c(x,y) (shallow regions slower, for refraction), a barrier mask (u forced to zero, or reflective boundary), and absorbing edges (a damping sponge near the borders so waves do not reflect off the tank walls: the "beach"). Several simulation steps per frame. Sources: point dippers oscillating sinusoidally (frequency adjustable), and a plane-wave bar along one edge. Rendering shader: treat the height field as a water surface lit from above: brightness from the Laplacian/curvature (crests focus light into bright lines, troughs defocus: the real ripple-tank shadowgraph effect), tinted cyan-to-blue, plus a subtle refraction of a faint bench grid beneath the water (offset the grid lookup by the height gradient) so the grid lines wobble with the waves; barriers drawn as solid lab-blue blocks with a soft shadow and a highlight edge; shallow-region inserts as a translucent glass plate shape with a rim; sources as small red dippers with a ring. Layout: the tank is a wide rectangle with a thin drawn frame, corner screws and legs, taking most of the viewport; a slim apparatus rail beside/below it holds draggable components drawn as tidy line icons with labels: "point dipper", "plane-wave bar", "wall", "wall with one slit", "wall with two slits", "curved mirror", "glass plate (shallow)", "prism plate". Experiments as numbered preset tabs across the top, set like headings in a lab manual: "1 Circular waves · 2 Two sources · 3 Single slit · 4 Double slit · 5 Parabolic mirror · 6 Refraction · 7 Free bench". Each preset has a two-sentence note in a side card explaining what to look for, with one measured quantity. Interaction: drag components from the rail into the tank; drag to move them, rotate with a handle, delete by dragging out; slit width and separation adjustable by dragging slit edges; frequency slider (changes wavelength visibly); a "strobe" toggle that freezes the apparent wave motion by sampling in phase (as real ripple tanks do with a stroboscope) so the standing pattern can be studied; click/tap on open water drops a single pulse (a pebble). Measurement tools: a red ruler that reads wavelength when laid across crests, and an intensity probe line: drag it across the pattern and a small plot beside the tank draws the time-averaged amplitude along that line: in the double-slit preset this shows the interference fringes as a row of peaks, with the predicted maxima (where the path difference from the two slits is a whole number of wavelengths; spacing λL/d printed beneath) marked by thin red ticks that line up with the measured peaks. The numbers for the ruler and the probe are taken from a small 320×200 companion simulation run on the CPU in step with the displayed one, so no pixels are ever read back from the graphics card. Selecting a component also opens a small inspector with sliders for its dimensions (slit separation and width, focal length, lengths), and components are keyboard-operable (arrow keys move, [ and ] rotate, Delete removes). Signature moment: the double slit. On load the page opens directly on preset 4: a plane wave marches from the left, meets a wall with two slits, and two fans of circular wavelets emerge and cross, forming the unmistakable alternating beams of bright rippling and calm nodal lines; within three seconds the pattern is established and glittering. Dragging the slits closer or further visibly spreads or tightens the beams in real time. Typography: lab-manual clarity: a geometric/grotesk sans (Helvetica/Arial/"Avenir") in deep blue; title "Ripple Tank" set small-caps tracked at top left with "Apparatus 7 · waves in two dimensions"; numbers and formulae in a monospace or with proper italic variables (λ, d, θ); red hand-drawn-style annotation arrows (SVG) that point out "nodal line" and "central maximum" in the double-slit preset and fade after ten seconds. Buttons are flat outlined rectangles that invert on press; focus rings in red. Fallback without WebGL or float textures: a Canvas 2D CPU simulation at reduced grid size (256×160) with ImageData rendering, same interactions. Performance: adaptive steps per frame; grid capped; pause when hidden; handle resize by re-allocating. Reduced motion: strobe mode on by default, showing a still pattern that updates slowly.

Open The Comet Diner, 3 A.M. ↗
066

The Comet Diner, 3 A.M.

A tiny diorama of a roadside railcar diner at three in the morning, drawn entirely with HTML boxes and CSS 3D transforms and floating on a disc of asphalt that fades into the dark. Nothing is lit when the page opens except the windows and one streetlamp; then the billboard strikes, tube by tube, the M failing twice, and pink and cyan light spills over the roof, the lot and the puddles, which reflect the words themselves upside down. Inside, a cook flips, a waitress walks the counter and stops to pour, one customer stirs and another turns a page. Around it: a parked sedan, an idling pickup, a phone box, moths at the lamp, a freight train on the horizon and, now and then, a car going somewhere else. A paperback-noir title and three lines of prose frame it, with a small bakelite panel of switches and jukebox keys.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A CSS-only (no JavaScript) isometric illustration of a small roadside diner at night, built from HTML elements with CSS 3D transforms, gradients, shadows and keyframes, under a big neon billboard that flickers on tube by tube. The viewer can switch the sign and the kitchen on and off, pick a song on the jukebox, and peer in by hovering, all with checkboxes, radios and :hover. Concept: a tiny glowing world in the dark that feels inhabited. Mood: lonely, cosy, cinematic; coffee at 3 a.m. Palette: night indigo-black #0B0F1A ground and sky, neon pink-red #FF3864, neon cyan #2DE2E6, warm window cream #F6F1D1 (interior light, counters, paper cups), deep violet #261447 for building shadow faces and asphalt reflections. Dark page: the concept needs night, and the neon is literal here: actual neon tubes on a sign. Scene (one isometric rig: a container with rotateX(~55deg) rotateZ(45deg) and preserve-3d; every object an extruded box or plane): a diner shaped like a long streamlined railcar: stainless-steel body (built from repeating-linear-gradient horizontal ribbing in cool greys tinted by the neon), rounded end suggested by stepped boxes, a band of large windows glowing cream with silhouettes inside: a counter with five stools, a cook at the grill (a simple figure whose arm moves, flipping), a waitress with a coffee pot who walks the counter and pauses (keyframed), two customers: one stirring coffee (tiny looping arm), one reading a newspaper that occasionally turns a page; pendant lamps; a pie under a glass dome; a jukebox with glowing arches at one end. Outside: a small parking lot with painted bays, one parked car with a long hood and tail lamps, a pickup with its headlights on throwing two cones of light, a telephone box, a newspaper vending box, a fire hydrant, a road with dashed centre line, a streetlamp with a pool of light where moths (tiny dots) circle, puddles on the asphalt that reflect the neon as blurred, wavering pink and cyan smears (flipped, blurred pseudo-elements with animated skew). The sign (signature moment): a billboard on two tall posts standing behind and above the diner: "COMET" in pink neon script/capitals built from CSS-shaped tube segments or text with layered text-shadow glow, "DINER" in cyan block capitals beneath, a shooting-star/comet outline with a tail of three streaks that animate in sequence (chasing lights), and "OPEN 24 HRS" in small cream bulbs. On load the sign is dark; then it strikes: letters flicker on one after another with authentic irregular neon stutter (keyframes with uneven opacity steps per letter, staggered delays), the "M" buzzing and failing twice before catching, the comet tail chasing; as each tube lights, its coloured glow washes onto the roof, the pole, the lot and the puddles (large blurred radial gradients whose opacity is tied to the same animation). One letter keeps an occasional nervous flicker forever. Interaction without JS: a small panel of physical-looking controls at the bottom, each a styled checkbox/radio with label: "SIGN" knife switch (off: sign dark, the scene lit only by windows and streetlamp; on: replays the strike sequence), "GRILL" (off: cook stands still, on: flipping and the grill glows, a patty jumps, and a column of steam curls from the flue stack behind the roof as animated blurred puffs), "RAIN" (overlays slanted rain streaks made from repeating gradients and makes puddle reflections livelier and the asphalt glossier), and three jukebox radio buttons labelled with invented songs ("B4 · Blue Formica", "C2 · Last Bus to Odessa", "A7 · Slow Comet"): selecting one changes the jukebox glow colour and the bounce tempo of musical notes (CSS-drawn quavers) drifting out of the diner door and the pulse of the window light; hovering/focusing the diner swings the whole roof up on a hinge along its back edge (rotateX with a slow ease), showing its lit ceiling and opening the interior to view from above; hovering the pickup makes its indicator blink; hovering the phone box makes it ring (the receiver wiggles and small arcs radiate). Framing typography: beneath the scene, a title in the manner of a paperback noir cover: "The Comet Diner, 3 A.M." in a condensed italic serif (Georgia/"Times New Roman" italic bold, tight) in cream with a pink underline rule, a three-line vignette of prose in small type, a "now playing" line that follows the jukebox, and a one-line hint naming the three hover targets; on wide screens the title sits in the empty lower-left corner beside the scene and the control panel in the lower-right: specific and atmospheric ("Route 9, between a town that closed and a town that hasn't opened yet. The pie is yesterday's. Nobody minds."). Tiny menu board text readable inside when the roof is lifted ("COFFEE .15 · PIE .30 · ADVICE FREE"). The window blinds carry the blurred shadows of the waitress and cook, and the puddle reflections are literally the words COMET and DINER, flipped. A passing car crosses the road every 40 seconds, its headlights sweeping the scene (a moving gradient), and far lightning-free stars twinkle sparsely; a distant freight train of tiny boxes crawls along the horizon line. Responsive: the isometric rig scales with min(vw, vh) units so the scene fits a 360px-wide phone without horizontal overflow (the faded ground corners are clipped); controls stack in a two-column, three-row grid with 44px targets. All inputs focusable with visible cream dashed focus rings. Reduced motion: sign simply on with steady glow, no flicker, no walking figures, no rain animation.
