Open A Short History of Fog in Port Verrow ↗
067

A Short History of Fog in Port Verrow

A magazine long-read from the invented quarterly "The Lee Shore" whose weather is its subject. The page opens almost blank: an amber harbour lamp glows at the end of a silhouetted mole, the letters of the huge wide-tracked title condense out of the mist one by one, and then a slow, volumetric fog settles into its drift between the reader and the essay. Text is always dark slate ink underneath; the fog is thick in the top and bottom of the window and thin in a reading band across the middle, so paragraphs come into legibility as they are scrolled to the centre and thin away again. Each of the five sections has its own weather: a light sea-fret for the vocabulary, a dense smoor for the foghorn, a near-total blanket for 1952, and a clear, sharp morning for the close. A fixed "Visibility" readout names the current conditions, the foghorn sends an amber ring through the fog every ninety seconds of story-time, and a "Lift the fog" control clears everything for anyone who simply wants to read.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A long-read magazine essay about fog in an invented harbour town, which the reader must read through fog. Slow volumetric-looking fog (WebGL fragment shader with layered fbm noise; CSS fallback) drifts over the whole page between the reader and the text; the pointer clears it locally, like a sleeve wiping a window or a lamp carried through mist; paragraphs condense into legibility as they reach the middle of the viewport and thin away again at the edges. Concept: an editorial page whose weather is its subject. Mood: muffled, maritime, literary, dry-humoured. Palette: fog white-grey #E6E8E6 page ground, thicker fog #BFC5C4, wet slate-green ink #26302F for text, and sodium amber #D98E04 as the one warm accent (the harbour lamp, drop caps, rules, the foghorn icon, links). Light page, low contrast in the fog but full contrast where cleared: body text itself is always dark ink at AA contrast underneath; the fog layer above never exceeds an opacity that would make text unreadable in the reading band, and a "Lift the fog" control removes it entirely. Fog system: a fixed full-viewport WebGL canvas above the content (pointer-events none): two or three layers of domain-warped fbm noise drifting at different speeds and directions, alpha shaped so the fog is densest at the top and bottom thirds of the viewport and thin in a horizontal "reading band" around the middle; a clearing mask: an array of recent pointer points passed as uniforms, marking where the pointer/finger has passed, which punches soft holes that slowly close again over ~4 seconds as the fog rolls back in: so the reader drags a clear patch around; a warm amber glow follows the pointer as if carrying a lantern, tinting the nearby fog. Scroll velocity stirs the fog (faster scrolling shears the noise). The fog density varies through the essay: section by section it is described in the text and enacted on the page (a "sea-fret" light haze; a three-day "blanket" during which the page is noticeably thicker and a foghorn sounds; a clear morning at the end when the fog withdraws entirely and the page stands in sharp light for the last section). The essay (about 900–1,200 words of real, good prose, bylined to the invented writer Maren Tollefsen for the invented quarterly "The Lee Shore"): numbered sections with cross-heads: I. "The Town That Is Sometimes There" (geography: the cold current, the warm land breeze, 140 fog days a year); II. "Seven Words" (the local vocabulary for fog: haar-like invented dialect words with definitions: "fret", "smoor", "wool weather", "the grey lady", "a dumb day", "a blanket", "a lifter"); III. "The Horn" (the foghorn installed 1883, its two-tone note every ninety seconds, the year it was silenced and the petition to bring it back because nobody could sleep without it); IV. "The Blanket of 1952" (three days, the ferry that tied up to the wrong town, the wedding conducted by voice alone); V. "What Fog Is For" (a reflective close: the town is the only place the writer knows where people give directions by sound). Layout and typography: classic magazine long-read: a generous single text column (max 34em) set in a fine book serif (Baskerville/"Iowan Old Style"/Palatino/Georgia) at 19–21px with real paragraph indents, hanging punctuation where possible, amber drop caps three lines deep, small-caps cross-heads with section numerals, pull quotes set very large in italic that sit half-submerged in fog ("We do not say the fog comes in. We say the town goes out."), marginal notes in small sans at the side on desktop (inline on phones). The title is set huge in a light-weight serif with wide tracking, its letters appearing out of the fog on load: the first three seconds: the page begins almost blank white-grey; the amber harbour lamp glows first, then the title's letters condense one by one as if seen through thinning mist, then the fog settles into its drift. Illustrations (SVG, monochrome slate with amber): a harbour chart fragment with soundings; the foghorn in elevation with sound arcs; a row of rooftops and masts in silhouette that sits behind the fog as a fixed parallax layer at the top of several sections, appearing and vanishing as density changes; a tide-and-fog calendar for an invented October as a small data graphic (days as cells shaded by hours of fog). Signature moment: section III's foghorn: when it scrolls into view, a long amber sound-wave ring expands from the foghorn illustration across the page every ninety seconds of story-time (every ~9 real seconds), physically pushing a ripple through the fog shader; if sound is enabled (designed toggle, off by default) a soft two-tone horn plays (two detuned low oscillators with slow attack and long release) with filtered-noise sea wash. Other interaction: click/tap sends a puff that clears a larger circle for a moment; keyboard users get a focus "lamp" that clears fog around the focused paragraph. Fallback without WebGL: CSS-animated layered semi-transparent noise textures (SVG turbulence) drifting, with a radial-gradient mask following the pointer. Reduced motion: fog is static and very light; text fully legible everywhere; no ripples.

Open Torre di Gelato ↗
068

Torre di Gelato

A sunny one-button arcade game set at the counter of the invented Gelateria Belmonte. A waffle cone stands in a mint holder on a marble counter beneath a blue-and-cream striped awning whose valance is lettered "Torre di Gelato"; between two pastel seaside houses lies a sliver of sea with a sailboat, a promenade railing and a gull. A server's striped sleeve swings a spade overhead, and each tap drops a soft scoop that squashes, ruffles, sticks and makes the whole tower lean, sway and overcorrect, every scoop jiggling a beat behind the one below. Dawdle and the sun melts the lower scoops into drips that run down the cone and pool on the marble. As the tower climbs, the camera leaves the awning behind and rises past washing lines, rooftop cats and aerials, a bell tower whose clock shows the viewer's own time, gulls, a kite, a hot-air balloon, thin clouds, a banner plane, a crescent moon, stars and a small satellite, each with a dashed height marker. Three dropped scoops end the round with a slow, messy cascade onto the counter and a paper receipt listing every flavour in order, with a total and a dry verdict from Signora Belmonte.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A one-button stacking game: scoops of gelato swing in on a server's spade above a cone; tap to drop each scoop; build the tallest tower you can before it topples. The scoops are soft: they squash on landing, sag, stick slightly, wobble as a stack, and begin to melt and drip if the viewer dawdles. Canvas 2D with simple but convincing physics. Concept: an original, sweet-natured arcade game whose charm is in the material behaviour and the flavours. Mood: playful, sunny, seaside holiday. Palette: cream sky/page #FFF1E6, strawberry pink #F7A8B8, pistachio mint #B5E2C4, lemon custard #FFE29A, chocolate/cone brown #8B5E3C (with waffle tan #D9A566), sky blue #7BB7E0 for the awning stripes, shadows and UI type. Pastel, light, flat with soft shading; no outlines except thin brown ones on the cone and awning. Scene: a striped gelateria awning across the top (blue and cream scallops that sway), a marble counter at the bottom, and the waffle cone standing in a holder at centre (crosshatch pattern drawn procedurally). Behind: a simple seaside backdrop in two or three flat tones (promenade railing, a sliver of sea, a gull). As the tower grows, the camera pans up smoothly: the awning is left below and the backdrop changes with height: past balconies with washing lines, rooftops with cats and aerials, a church bell tower, gulls, a kite, a hot-air balloon, thin clouds, an aeroplane towing a banner, and finally stars and a small satellite at absurd heights, each a designed surprise with a height marker ("12 scoops: higher than the awning", "40: bell tower"). Mechanics: a scoop swings left-right above the tower on the spade (pendulum motion whose speed and amplitude increase with height, plus wind gusts indicated by a little flag); tap/click/Space releases it; it falls with gravity. Each scoop is a soft circle body (a ring of ~14 points with pressure and spring constraints, or a simpler squash-and-stretch circle with a spring-driven squash factor and contact patches) that lands on the scoop below. Landing accuracy matters: a well-centred scoop settles; an off-centre one makes the tower lean; the stack is a chain of bodies connected by sticky contact constraints with angular spring-back, so the whole tower sways like a real over-ambitious cone and overcorrects; lean beyond a threshold and the top scoops slide off and splat onto the counter (splat shapes with spreading puddles), ending the round after three dropped scoops (three little spoons as lives). A perfectly centred drop gives a "perfetto" bonus: a sprinkle burst and a short stabilising effect; three in a row adds a wafer or cherry topper. Material rendering: each scoop has the classic ruffled "skirt" at its base (a scalloped edge), a soft highlight, texture flecks per flavour (pistachio pieces, chocolate chips, vanilla seeds, strawberry bits), and squashes to about 80% height on impact, bulging sideways, then recovers with a damped wobble; where two scoops touch, a slight merge fillet. Melting: as time passes, drips form on the lower scoops and run down the cone (teardrop shapes that grow, detach, slide and pool on the counter), faster when the sun (a simple disc in the sky with a face-less warm glow) is out from behind a cloud; melted scoops become slipperier, so speed matters as well as accuracy. Flavours: at least ten with Italian names shown on a small ticket as each arrives: fragola, pistacchio, limone, cioccolato, stracciatella, nocciola, fior di latte, amarena (with cherry swirl), menta, zabaione; plus rare special scoops: a heavy "doppio" double-size scoop, a slippery sorbetto, and a waffle disc that acts as a stabilising platform. Signature moment: the sway. A tower of ten or more soft scoops leaning, wobbling, each scoop jiggling slightly out of phase with the one beneath, drips running, while the next scoop swings overhead: tension and comedy; and the topple, when it comes, is a slow, inevitable, gloriously messy cascade. UI and typography: score as a handwritten gelateria price-board in the corner: scoops stacked, best today (kept in memory for the session), height in "metri di gelato"; titles in a jaunty italic script-like display stack ("Brush Script MT", "Segoe Script", "Snell Roundhand", cursive) paired with a round sans for small text ("Arial Rounded MT Bold"/"Trebuchet MS"); title screen: "Torre di Gelato" lettered across the awning valance, with a three-scoop cone drawn large, and the designed instruction "tap to drop · don't let it lean"; game-over card as a paper receipt listing every flavour stacked in order with a total and a verdict from the invented proprietor, Signora Belmonte ("Fourteen. My nephew does twenty, but he cheats."). Controls: one input everywhere: tap, click, Space or Enter; P pauses; a designed sound toggle (off by default) for soft plops, a bell on perfetto, a sad trombone-less "splat". On load, an attract loop shows scoops being stacked automatically so the first three seconds already have the swaying tower. Reduced motion: no camera sway or jiggle (scoops settle immediately); melting shown as static drips. Responsive: portrait-first; on wide screens the play column is centred with backdrop extending.

Open Strange Attractors — A Collection of Orbits ↗
069

Strange Attractors — A Collection of Orbits

A dark viridian vitrine holds one strange attractor at a time, built from up to 480,000 points of gold dust whose overlapping density, not any drawn line, makes the form: thin outer loops are faint threads, the folded cores glow champagne, and a jade shadow of the same density lies on a graduated floor ring beneath. The page is composed like a museum label on glass: a large Didone name and plate number at top left, the governing equations set in italic serif with live, draggable constants at bottom left, a row of seven miniature specimens at bottom right. The experience is slow and tactile: the specimen turns on its own until taken in hand, a swarm of bright tracers shows which way the flow runs and how quickly neighbours part, and every change of plate or constant makes the whole cloud dissolve and re-settle into its new shape rather than cut.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A generative-art gallery of chaotic dynamical systems, each rendered in WebGL as hundreds of thousands of luminous points tracing a strange attractor in three dimensions, slowly rotating like a specimen of gold dust suspended in a dark green vitrine. The viewer turns each one by hand, changes its parameters, and watches trajectories diverge. Concept: equations as sculpture: fine-grained, additive, jewel-like: not the usual particle network. Mood: contemplative, precious, scientific-romantic. Palette: deep viridian black #06110D background (dark green, not navy or black-purple), gold dust #D9B45B, pale champagne #F5E9C8 for the densest/brightest regions and type, and a muted jade #2E6F5E for axes, annotation lines and secondary type (small jade labels use a lighter tint of the same hue for legibility). Dark page (the additive point rendering requires it), restrained: two hues only, with brightness carrying the form. Rendering: for each attractor, integrate the ODE on the CPU with RK4 at load (and whenever parameters change) for 480,000 steps (220,000 on phones), store positions in a Float32Array vertex buffer (each point jittered a random fraction of a step along its segment so the dust has no beaded pattern), and draw as GL_POINTS with additive blending and tiny point size (about 1.2px, with perspective attenuation) and very low per-point weight so density builds luminance: sparse outer loops are dim gold threads, dense cores glow champagne. The additive accumulation happens in a half-float off-screen target (8-bit where unavailable) and is then developed through a narrow gold–champagne ramp, so density never clips to white. Colour is also steered by a per-point local-speed attribute (slow regions warmer and paler). Depth cue: fog toward jade-black with distance. A second pass draws 30 "live" tracer particles as slightly larger bright points with short fading trails moving along the attractor in real time, so the flow direction is visible; they start as one tight cluster and separate: sensitivity made visible. Thin jade axes with tick marks, a graduated floor ring, projected x/y/z labels, and a ground-plane shadow projection (the attractor's flattened density rendered dimly in jade beneath it, like a shadow on the vitrine floor). Collection (seven systems with their standard equations and parameters): Lorenz (σ = 10, ρ = 28, β = 8/3); Rössler (a = 0.2, b = 0.2, c = 5.7); Aizawa (a = 0.95, b = 0.7, c = 0.6, d = 3.5, e = 0.25, f = 0.1); Thomas' cyclically symmetric attractor (b = 0.208186); Halvorsen (a = 1.89); Dadras (a = 3, b = 2.7, c = 1.7, d = 2, e = 9); Chen–Lee (a = 5, b = −10, c = −3.8). Every displayed formula matches what is actually integrated. Each has: its name in large type, the author as commonly credited (dates only for Lorenz 1963 and Rössler 1976), its equations typeset properly with italic variables, aligned equals signs, stacked fractions and dotted derivatives, the parameter values as adjustable controls, and a two-sentence note. If a parameter choice makes the orbit escape to infinity, a quiet notice says so and the last bounded form stays on show. Layout: the attractor fills the viewport; text is typeset at the margins like a museum label on glass inside a hairline frame with corner ticks: top-left the plate number, name, credit and a live point count; bottom-left the note and the equations in a refined serif italic with the parameters as live numbers that can be dragged horizontally to change (number scrubbing), stepped with small drawn minus/plus glyphs, or reset by double-click; hovering a parameter lights its occurrences in the equations. Bottom-right a slim horizontal index of the seven plates as tiny thumbnails integrated and drawn in the page, with roman numerals, under a one-line interaction hint that fades after first use; top-right minimal controls: tracers on/off, shadow on/off, "new initial condition", "butterfly effect". No audio. Typography: names in a light Didone display (Didot/"Bodoni MT"/Georgia) large; equations in italic serif (Palatino/"Times New Roman" italic) with care for sub/superscripts and dots for time derivatives (ẋ, ẏ, ż); labels in tracked small caps sans in jade. Interaction: drag to rotate (yaw/pitch with inertia; touch supported), wheel/pinch to zoom within limits, double-click/tap to reset; idle auto-rotation resumes after a few seconds. Changing a parameter re-integrates (debounced, computed in time-sliced chunks so the UI stays responsive) and the cloud morphs: the signature moment: when switching plates or parameters, points do not pop: each point interpolates from its old position to its new one over about 1.6–1.8 seconds in the vertex shader (two position attributes and a mix uniform with an eased curve, staggered per point with a small drift and sag mid-flight), so the Lorenz butterfly visibly melts and re-condenses into the Rössler band or a Thomas loop, like gold dust re-settling; interrupted morphs continue from where the dust is. "New initial condition" restarts the orbit from another point so every grain moves yet the same form returns. A "butterfly effect" control launches two tracers 10⁻⁵ apart with a readout of their separation growing exponentially on a tiny log plot, a live least-squares estimate of the Lyapunov exponent λ, and the time at which they have fully parted. Entrance: black-green; then the first attractor is drawn progressively in time order (a draw-range sweep from 0 to N over about 3 seconds, the point count ticking up with it), so the viewer sees the trajectory lay itself down loop by loop into the finished form. Keyboard: left/right arrows or 1–7 change plate; +/− zoom; 0 resets the view; arrow keys on a focused parameter step it. Fallback without WebGL: Canvas 2D rendering of a projected density image accumulated over a few frames into ImageData with the same palette, rotating slowly in steps. Performance: static buffers, uniforms only per frame; DPR capped at 2; point count adapted to device; paused when hidden. Reduced motion: no auto-rotation, entrance sweep or tracers; morphs replaced by cross-fades.

Open Semaphore — Say It with Flags ↗
070

Semaphore — Say It with Flags

A sunny mid-century instructional poster that is also a working instrument. A flat, geometric sailor stands on a signal deck with a rail, a lifebuoy, a coil of rope, the sea horizon, a steamer and one small cloud, holding two diagonally divided red-and-yellow flags. Type a message onto an invented naval message form (S.1320, with TO / FROM / TIME boxes and ruled typewriter lines), press SEND, and the signaller spells it out in correct flag semaphore: arms snapping the shortest way round an eight-point clock with a slight wind-up and overshoot, flags folding edge-on through the move and cracking open with follow-through, the body leaning and bobbing with each position, rests between words, a quick drop between double letters, and the numerals and letters-follow signs inserted automatically. A strip of tiny static glyphs lights up in red in step with the performance while the current character stands beside the figure in huge stencil type. On load the signaller calls attention with both flags and spells HELLO unasked. A second tab turns the page into a reading trainer with a streak counter and a ship's bell, and a reference chart of thirty positions, grouped by the seven circles of the code, lets the viewer play the signaller like an instrument by hovering, tapping or pressing letter keys. Everything is shown as seen by the observer, and says so.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A tool that translates any typed message into flag semaphore, performed by an animated signaller standing on a ship's signal deck. Type a sentence, press send, and the figure spells it out letter by letter with two flags, with correct arm positions, proper rests between words, and the cadence of a trained signaller. It also runs in reverse as a small trainer: the figure signals, the viewer reads. Concept: a legible, joyful code; body language as typography. Accuracy: use the standard flag-semaphore alphabet, defined in the page as pairs of arm directions on the eight-point compass (positions at 45° steps: down, low, out, high, up for each side). The implementer must encode the standard chart carefully: A = right arm low (down-right diagonal on the signaller's right) with left arm down; B = right arm out; C = right arm high; D = right arm up (left down); E = left arm high; F = left arm out; G = left arm low; H = right out + left... : derive every letter from the well-known circular scheme (first circle A–G with one arm at rest, second circle H–N with one arm at "low", and so on, with J, V and the remaining letters at their standard irregular places), and double-check the table against that scheme; include the rest/space position (both flags down in front), the "numerals follow" and "letters follow (J)" signals, attention, and cancel/error (both arms waving). State on the page that the diagram is shown as seen by the observer (mirrored from the signaller's own left and right), and be consistent everywhere. Mood: brisk, nautical, sunny, mid-century instructional poster. Palette: sailcloth white #F6F4EC page, flag red #D62828 and flag yellow #F6BD16 (the classic diagonally divided red-and-yellow flags used at sea), navy #14213D for the figure, type and rules. Light page, flat colour, crisp shapes, a faint canvas weave texture and a thin navy border with rope-twist corners drawn in SVG. The signaller (SVG, flat geometric illustration): a sailor figure in navy with a white cap and a striped collar, standing on a deck with a rail, the sea horizon and one small cloud behind; arms are two-segment limbs pivoting at the shoulder (straight arms with the flag staff as an extension), each holding a square flag divided diagonally red/yellow. Flags are not rigid: they trail the motion: implement follow-through with a simple secondary spring on the flag's rotation and a wave deformation of the flag cloth (animated path with two or three control points) so they snap and flutter at the end of each move, and ripple gently in the wind when held. Arm transitions take the shortest path with an eased, slightly anticipatory motion (cubic-bezier(.5,0,.2,1.2)-like spring), about 380ms at standard speed, with a hold on each letter; double letters get a brief drop to rest between them. Layout: the signaller large on the left/top; on the right/below, the message panel: a text input styled as a signal pad form ("MESSAGE FORM S.1320-like invented naval stationery": ruled lines, boxes for TO / FROM / TIME), the message typed in capitals in a typewriter or stencil-like face; beneath it the message appears as a strip of small static semaphore glyphs (tiny figures or clock-like diagrams, one per character, wrapping in rows) with the current letter highlighted in red as the signaller performs it and the large current letter shown beside the figure in huge stencil type. Controls: SEND (starts/stops), speed (words per minute dial: slow / standard / "yeoman"), LOOP, and a row of canned messages on signal-flag-shaped chips: "ENGLAND EXPECTS THAT EVERY MAN WILL DO HIS DUTY"-style historical quotation is fine but also add playful ones: "SEND MORE TEA", "WE ARE NOT LOST", "PLEASE STOP WAVING BACK". Signature moment: the performance: a whole sentence signalled fluently, flags cracking through each position with follow-through, while the glyph strip lights up in step: it should look like a real person who has done this ten thousand times. On load the signaller raises both flags for "attention", then spells "HELLO" without being asked. Trainer mode ("Read the signal"): the figure signals a random letter or short word; the viewer types what they read; correct answers ring a ship's bell (if sound is on) and advance a streak counter; mistakes show the correct glyph side by side with the guessed one. A reference chart section below shows the whole alphabet as a 6×5 grid of small diagrams with letters, grouped by the "circles" of the system with a short explanation of the logic; hovering/focusing a diagram makes the big signaller take that position, and pressing any letter key on the keyboard (when not typing in the input) does the same instantly: so the viewer can "play" the signaller like an instrument. Typography: bold stencil/condensed caps for headings (a stack such as "Stencil", "Arial Narrow", Impact with tracking) in navy; body in a clean humanist sans ("Gill Sans"/"Trebuchet MS"); title "SEMAPHORE" with each letter sitting under its own tiny flag diagram. Optional sound toggle (off by default): flag snaps (short noise bursts), sea ambience, bell. Reduced motion: arms move directly between positions without flutter; flags static. Touch: large targets; the text input uses the system keyboard. Unsupported characters are skipped with a note; numerals are sent with the numerals sign.

Open Apiary No. 9 — Hill Honey ↗
071

Apiary No. 9 — Hill Honey

A sunlit, nearly monochrome amber landing page for a nine-hive honey producer, built from one material and one shape. A turned beech dipper lowers into the top of the page and lets go of a single thread of honey that falls through the oversized serif headline and coils like a rope onto the first cell of a full-bleed honeycomb. Scrolling makes the comb fill outward from that landing point: each cell rises with a curved meniscus, then most are sealed with a dimpled wax cap while some stay open and glossy. Three seven-cell clearings in the comb hold the three honeys with their hexagonal jars. Bees patrol the cell walls, fly off in arcs and drift toward a resting cursor. Below the comb everything remains hexagonal: a twelve-cell ring calendar, interlocking number cells, a compass and a dance floor for the waggle dance, and an order cell that ends in a pressed wax seal. The voice is first person plural, dry and fond of its bees.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A landing page for an invented small honey producer, "Apiary No. 9", nine hives on a south-facing hillside above the invented village of "Thornaby Coombe". The whole page is built on a hexagonal comb grid: content lives in cells, and honey actually flows: a viscous, amber liquid that pours, stretches, drips and fills cells as the visitor moves through the page. Concept: one material (honey) and one geometry (the hexagon) carried through everything. Mood: sunlit, slow, generous, a little nerdy about bees. Palette: pale beeswax cream-yellow #FFF3C4 as the page ground, honey amber #F2A900 (with a deeper flow tone derived toward #D98A00 for thickness), propolis brown #7A4A00 for secondary type and comb walls, bee-black #1E1A12 for text. Light, warm, nearly monochromatic amber page; no other hues except a single cornflower blue dot on the queen in one illustration (#3B6FB6), as beekeepers mark queens. Honey rendering (signature material): Canvas 2D viscous-fluid look, not a generic particle system: drips are drawn as smooth metaball-like shapes or spline-outlined blobs with a thick bright specular highlight along one side, a darker core where the honey is deep, translucency so comb walls show faintly through, and long thinning threads that stretch between a falling drop and its source before snapping and recoiling. Timing is slow and heavy: custom easing with long ease-in for the fall and a soft settling bulge on landing; pooled honey levels out slowly. Structure: 1) Hero: a wooden honey dipper (drawn in SVG, turned beech with grooves) held at the top centre, turning slowly; honey spirals off it in a thin continuous thread that falls the height of the viewport and coils on itself at the bottom (the liquid rope-coil effect: the thread's landing point circles so it piles in loops). Headline beside it in huge type: "Slow food, made by fast animals." with the brand lockup: a hexagon containing "No. 9". 2) The comb: a large hexagonal grid; scrolling into it, cells fill with honey one after another outward from where the thread lands (each cell's liquid level rises with a meniscus and then gets capped with a pale wax lid: a little dimpled cap drawn on; roughly a quarter of the cells are left open as glossy, unripe honey so the comb stays amber); some cells contain content instead: the three honeys as larger merged cells: "Spring Blossom: hawthorn, apple, dandelion; pale and quick to set", "High Summer: lime tree, bramble, clover; the one for toast", "Ling Heather: thixotropic, jelly-thick, smells of the moor; pressed, not spun": each with a jar drawn in SVG (hexagonal glass jar, paper label, cloth-free plain lid) whose honey colour differs (pale straw, amber, dark red-amber) and tilts sluggishly when hovered, a slow bubble rising. 3) "A year at the hives": a ring calendar of twelve hexagons around a centre, each month with a tiny line illustration and one sentence, the sentence of the chosen month shown in the large centre cell (below the ring on phones); the chosen month's cell fills with honey (January: "They cluster and shiver. We mend frames."; June: "Swarm season. Keep a ladder in the car."). 4) "By the numbers", computed-looking but stated as the apiary's own figures: about 40,000 bees in a summer hive, roughly 9 kg left for the bees each winter, 127 jars last season: each number set large inside a cell, counting up when it enters; a fourth cell holds the queen illustration with her blue dot. 5) The waggle dance: a small interactive: a bee on the comb performs a figure-eight; the visitor drags the "flowers" marker around a compass and the bee's waggle run rotates to the corresponding angle relative to vertical (angle from the sun = angle from vertical), with waggle duration by distance: explained in three plain sentences. 6) Order: jars are few: a simple list with prices in pounds and "sold out until August" tags on two, and a "tell me when the heather is in" field with a custom input; confirmation: a wax seal with a bee pressed into it. Interaction: the pointer leaves a thin trail of honey that thickens if the visitor lingers and drips downward from the cursor after a pause; clicking a filled comb cell uncaps it and a bead of honey wells out and runs down the grid, pooling in the cells below (the cell refills and is recapped after a few seconds); bees (6–10 small drawn bees with blurred wing ovals) wander the comb, walking along cell edges, occasionally flying off in arcs and returning, and they gather near the cursor when it rests. Hover on comb cells raises them slightly with a soft amber inner glow. Typography: a confident, friendly serif for display (Georgia/"Palatino" bold, tight, large) in bee-black; small caps in a geometric sans for labels (Futura/"Century Gothic"/"Trebuchet MS"); numbers in tabular lining figures; the "No. 9" lockup stamped like a hive number stencil. Copy voice: first person plural, observant, dry: "We do not make honey. We collect rent." Entrance: the dipper lowers into frame and the first thread of honey descends through the headline within the first three seconds. Reduced motion: honey thread and fills are shown static (cells already filled), bees stand still, counting numbers appear final. Performance: canvas layers sized to their sections; DPR capped at 2; cap drips; pause off-screen and when hidden. Responsive: hex grid re-tiles for narrow widths (3 columns on phones) with no horizontal overflow.

Open Lumen — Municipal Power Board, Pop. 2,140 ↗
072

Lumen — Municipal Power Board, Pop. 2,140

Lumen is a municipal power dashboard built as a model-railway board: an isometric pastel town of some thirty buildings on a steel-blue plinth, with a wind ridge, a sun field, a weir, a battery barn and a pylon line to the mainland, all wired to a tiny substation. One simulation of a 24-hour day drives both the model and the instruments around it. The board opens empty, buildings spring up district by district, wires string themselves between poles, and yellow dashes begin to run along them at a speed and density that match the kilowatts on each line. As dusk falls the board tints from cream through pink to a dim lavender, windows light house by house, the Bijou's marquee comes on, the wind drops, and the viewer watches the battery barn's flow turn round and the interconnector start importing. Around the model sit a day dial, frequency gauge, segmented battery, a 24-hour stacked supply chart with the demand line, today's mix ring, a carbon-free streak, the mainland account in pounds and a dryly written event log, all in the same flat navy-outlined style.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A dashboard for the electricity grid of a tiny fictional town, "Lumen", drawn as an isometric toy model in SVG: little houses, a bakery, a school, a tram, a cinema, a windmill ridge, a solar field, a small hydro dam and a battery barn, all wired together with visible lines along which power visibly flows. The data (clearly fictional) is simulated in the page over a 24-hour day; the dashboard's charts and the model town are two views of the same simulation. Concept: an infrastructure dashboard with the charm of a model railway. Mood: cheerful, tidy, civic-minded; a town that is proud of its substation. Palette: warm paper white #F6F1EA ground, blush pink #F9C5D1, pale aqua #A8DADC, steel blue #457B9D, sunflower yellow #FFD166 (electricity, lit windows, highlights), ink navy #2B2D42 for outlines and type. Pastel, light, flat isometric shading (each building: top face lightest, left mid, right darkest of one hue), thin navy outlines, no gradients, no glow except the yellow of lit windows at night. The town (isometric SVG, 30° grid, about 30 buildings in five districts on a diamond-shaped base with a river crossing it): Old Town (terraced houses, the bakery with a chimney that works from 3 a.m., the clock tower showing simulation time), Millside (workshops, the laundry, a small factory with saw-tooth roof), Hilltop (school, library, the cinema "The Bijou" with a marquee that lights at dusk), Riverside (allotments, pump house, tram depot; a single tram trundles a loop and draws power while moving), and the Works (substation with tiny transformers, battery barn). Generation: three wind turbines on the ridge (blades rotate according to wind speed), a solar field (panels brighten with the sun; a cloud shadow crossing reduces output), the hydro dam (steady, with a sluice animation), and the interconnector pylon line leading off the board to "the mainland" for import/export. Simulation: time runs at 1 simulated hour per ~6 real seconds (adjustable, and scrubbing via a day dial); demand curve per district with morning and evening peaks, the bakery's night shift, school hours, cinema evenings, tram schedule; supply from sun (bell curve with passing clouds), wind (smooth noise), hydro (flat), battery (charges on surplus, discharges on deficit, with state of charge), import/export balancing the rest. Day–night lighting: the board's ground tint shifts from morning cream through noon white to dusk pink and night navy-blue tint (kept pastel: a dim lavender, not black), windows light up yellow house by house at dusk and go dark at bedtime, streetlamps come on. Signature moment: power flow. Along every wire (thin navy lines on little poles and underground dashed runs), small yellow dashes travel in the direction of flow at a speed and density proportional to the kilowatts on that line; lines reverse direction when the battery switches from charging to discharging or when the town starts exporting; thick trunk lines from generation to the substation pulse. When the evening peak hits and the wind drops, the viewer watches the battery barn's flow reverse and the interconnector begin importing. Clicking any building or generator selects it: it lifts slightly, its wire highlights, and a small card shows its live draw/output, today's energy, and one human detail ("No. 14 Tanner Row: 0.4 kW. Kettle. Again."). Dashboard panels (arranged around/below the model on desktop; stacked on phones; drawn in the same pastel flat style with navy hairlines): a big stacked area chart of the last 24 hours of supply by source against the demand line, with the "now" line advancing; a frequency gauge (50.00 Hz with a needle that wobbles within ±0.05 and dips when a big load switches on); battery state-of-charge as a segmented battery icon with hours remaining; today's mix as a ring ("71% from the hill and the sky"); carbon-free streak counter in hours; import/export tally in pounds, "sold to the mainland: £38.20"; a ticker of events ("17:42 The Bijou's marquee is on. 3.1 kW of civic pride."). Interaction: toggles per district as little knife switches on a drawn switchboard (flip one and that district blacks out: windows dark, a tiny "!" over the bakery, demand drops, the event log grumbles; flip it back); weather controls: wind dial and cloud slider that the viewer can override; "storm" button that trips the interconnector so the town must island on its own generation (load shedding by priority if needed, with the cinema going dark first and the bakery last); a day dial to scrub time; speed ×1/×4/×16; "tonight's big match" event button that creates a half-time kettle surge. Typography: friendly municipal: headings in a rounded or geometric sans (Futura/"Century Gothic"/"Avenir Next"/"Trebuchet MS"), bold, navy, with the town crest (a lightbulb in a shield, SVG); figures in tabular numerals, large; small labels in tracked caps; district names lettered on the board itself in isometric perspective (skewed text along the grid). Entrance: the board is empty for an instant, then buildings pop up district by district with a springy rise (staggered 25ms, overshoot), wires string themselves between poles, and the yellow dashes start to run: the first three seconds. Reduced motion: buildings appear at once, flow shown as static arrows on wires, time advances in steps. Keyboard: the model is a single tab stop with arrow keys moving between buildings in logical district order (Enter reads the meter, Escape closes it); switches, dials and buttons are focusable, all with navy-and-yellow focus rings. Performance: SVG dashes driven by one inherited stroke-dashoffset per wire (three phase-shifted dash layers fade in as the kilowatts rise), CSS animation for pulses, ripples, sluice and steam; pause when hidden.

Open Zoetrope ↗
073

Zoetrope

A darkened Victorian parlour: a brass wall lamp flares up at the upper left, throwing a warm pool over patterned wallpaper and a mahogany table on which stands a slotted drum on a turned pedestal. One automatic flick sets it spinning, and within three seconds the bright slits sliding across the near wall fuse into a cream band in which a row of small black-and-vermilion horses gallops on the spot, dimmer and narrower than the drawings on the paper band lying unrolled on the table in front. As the drum coasts down the figure flickers, breaks into sweeping bars with comet tails of drawing, and finally stands as twelve separate slivers. Nothing is faked: the picture is built each frame by sampling the far-wall strip through the moving slits into a persistence buffer that fades like the eye, so the speed, direction and failure of the illusion all follow from how the viewer spins the drum. A reading glass on a stand shows the central figure enlarged; lifting the slotted lid turns the same drawings into a grey smear; and a drawer in the table holds twelve blank frames for a strip of the viewer's own.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A virtual zoetrope: the Victorian slotted drum that turns a strip of drawings into a moving picture. The viewer spins the drum by dragging; at low speed they see only a blur of slots and drawings sliding past; at the right speed the stroboscopic effect locks and a little figure suddenly comes alive, viewed through the passing slits. The physics of the illusion is simulated honestly: the image seen is the drum's strip sampled through moving slits, so the animation appears, quickens, dissolves, stands still or runs backwards depending on the rate and direction of spin, exactly as in a real one. Concept: recreate the moment of surprise that began cinema. Mood: candlelit parlour, wonder, mahogany and brass. Palette: dark room brown-black #17100B, mahogany drum and stand #6B3F1D (with highlights #8E5A2C), cream paper strip #E9D8B4, vermilion ink #C1272D for the figures' accents and the title, lamp-warm highlight tones derived from the cream. Dark page (a darkened parlour is the concept), lit by a single oil lamp from the upper left that casts a warm pool on the table and gleams on the drum. The apparatus (Canvas 2D): a cylindrical drum seen from slightly above and in front: a wide open-topped cylinder on a turned wooden pedestal with a brass spindle. The outer wall, in front, has 12 evenly spaced vertical slits; through the slits and over the near rim the viewer sees the inside of the far wall, where the paper strip with 12 frames sits. Render the cylinder geometry properly: the near wall as a dark curved band with bright slits moving across it (their spacing compressing toward the edges by cosine projection), the far inner wall visible through them carrying the frames (also projected on the cylinder so frames foreshorten toward the sides), top rim ellipses, the base shadow, and motion blur proportional to angular speed when not strobing. The illusion: compute what is visible per frame of rendering from the true drum angle. Add a "viewing aperture" model: the viewer sees the far wall only through the slits; because slits and pictures move in opposite directions across the line of sight, each slit sweeps a narrow glimpse of one drawing; persistence of vision is simulated by accumulating several sub-frame samples per display frame with decay (or by rendering each glimpsed frame sharp but horizontally compressed, as in a real zoetrope) so that at sufficient speed the sharp, steady, animated figure appears in place, slightly dimmer and thinner than the drawings really are. Below a threshold speed, show blurred streaks. Strips (interchangeable paper bands, each 12 frames, drawn procedurally in the page as simple, elegant silhouettes/line drawings in black and vermilion on cream, with frame numbers and tiny registration marks): 1) a galloping horse built from articulated shapes (body, neck, head, four two-segment legs in a keyframed gait cycle, tail streaming); 2) a juggler keeping three vermilion balls in the air; 3) a leaping frog/acrobat somersaulting over a hoop; 4) a bird in flight (wing cycle); 5) a couple waltzing (two figures rotating); 6) abstract: a bouncing ball that squashes, and a growing-shrinking star: the classic phenakistiscope motifs. The unrolled strip is also shown flat beneath the apparatus as a long paper band with all twelve drawings, so the viewer can compare the static frames with the living figure; the frame currently behind the nearest slit is underlined. Interaction: drag horizontally across the drum (mouse or finger) to flick it: it spins with angular momentum and slows gradually with friction, so the animation speeds, locks, slows and dissolves back into separate pictures: the signature moment is that lock-in, the instant the blur resolves into a galloping horse; a brass crank handle beside the stand can be turned for steady speed (on release a governor holds the speed reached until the drum is braked or grabbed; Enter on the focused crank sets or releases the governor), with a small rpm dial in the table apron that marks the "sweet band" in vermilion; reversing the spin runs the animation backwards; keys: left/right arrows add spin, Space brakes with a felt pad. A toggle "lift the lid" raises the slotted upper ring off the drum on two fine wires: now the viewer sees the strip directly and only smear, proving the slits make the picture. On wide screens a brass reading glass on a stand beside the drum magnifies the central view through the slits, so the lock-in can be seen large. A "draw your own" drawer: a strip of 12 small canvases where the viewer can scribble simple frames with the pointer (with onion-skin of the previous frame), then drops the strip into the drum. Typography: Victorian show-card style, used sparingly: the title "ZOETROPE" in wide-tracked Tuscan-flavoured serif capitals (a stack like "Playbill", "Bodoni MT", Didot, Georgia with heavy weight and generous letterspacing) in vermilion with a thin cream outline, subtitle in italic: "or, the Wheel of Life: turn briskly and look through the slits"; strip names on small paper labels in a handwriting-like italic; a short three-sentence note explaining why it works (the slits act as shutters; each glimpse is too brief to smear; the eye joins them). Optional sound toggle (off by default): the soft whirr and tick of the spindle rising with speed. Entrance: the lamp flares up, the drum is given one automatic flick and the horse gallops within the first three seconds, then coasts down. Reduced motion: the drum does not spin by itself and motion blur is off; a stepper lifts the lid and advances the drum one frame at a time at a slow rate on request. Layout: the scene fills the first screen; the controls sit on the table's front apron beneath it, with the drawing drawer sliding out below.

Open The Kelp Forest of 3n + 1 ↗
074

The Kelp Forest of 3n + 1

A bright, sunlit aquarium tank in which the Collatz conjecture is a living plant. The reverse tree of every path from 1 to 9,000 grows out of a sea-urchin "1" on a sandy floor: thick deep-teal trunks through 2, 4, 8 and 16, thinning and paling into thousands of sea-green hairline fronds that lean and curl because even steps bend one way and odd steps the other. The whole forest sways in a slow travelling swell while garibaldi-orange and teal fish weave through it, bubbles rise and marine snow drifts. The page is set like a museum panel: a bold serif title and three plain sentences in the upper left with the rule drawn as a two-branch sprig, a cream instrument plate with a number field, famous starters and two brass dive-gauge dials, a specimen card with the hailstone plot, and a strip of statistics printed on the sand ending in "counter-examples found: 0". The signature moment is following a number home: one orange thread lighting the whole way down through the forest to 1.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A data visualization of the Collatz conjecture drawn as an underwater kelp forest. Take any whole number: if even, halve it; if odd, triple it and add one; repeat. Every number ever tried ends at 1, and nobody can prove it always will. Here the reverse tree of all these paths (computed in the page for the first several thousand integers) grows up from the sea-floor root "1" as fronds: each step is a short segment that bends one way for an even step and the other way for an odd step, so the tree becomes a mass of swaying, curling, branching kelp. Concept: an unsolved problem that looks like something alive. Mood: sunlit shallows, curiosity, calm. Palette: pale aqua water #E6F4F1 (page ground: bright, light-filled water, not a dark ocean), deep teal #0B525B for mature stalks and type, fresh sea-green #65B891 for young fronds, warm orange #FF9F1C for the highlighted path and the garibaldi-like fish, abyssal ink #06262B for the root, holdfast and smallest text. Light page with soft vertical light shafts (very subtle paler bands drifting) and a sandy floor line. Construction: compute Collatz sequences for n = 1…N (N = 9,000 on desktop, 3,200 on phones) and build the merged tree (each number's parent is its next step). Draw from the root upward: each edge is a segment of length that shrinks slightly with depth; turning angle +a for a step arriving from an even number (n/2 branch) and −b for the odd branch ((n−1)/3), with a ≈ 8–10° and b ≈ 16–20° so the forest leans and curls gracefully (the well-known Collatz "seaweed" look), stroke width proportional to the log of the number of sequences passing through that edge (thick trunks near 1, 2, 4, 8, 16; hairline tips), colour blending from deep teal at the base to sea-green at the tips, slight transparency so overlaps build depth. Sway: every node's angle is perturbed by a slow travelling sine (a current) with amplitude growing toward the tips, so the entire forest undulates like kelp in swell; computed efficiently by re-evaluating angles along cached paths each frame, or by swaying in layers. Interaction: hover/touch near any frond tip or node: the nearest number is selected and its whole path to the root lights in orange with a thicker stroke: the signature moment: one bright thread running down through the forest to 1; a label shows the number, its number of steps ("27 takes 111 steps and climbs as high as 9,232 before coming down"), and a small inline chart of its trajectory (the "hailstone" plot, value against step, in orange on a log scale). A number field ("plant a number") accepts any integer up to about 10^12: its path is computed on the fly (BigInt or safe arithmetic) and drawn as a new orange frond that grows from the root upward segment by segment, joining the existing forest where it meets it; the label reports steps and peak. When a planted frond is longer than the forest, the view eases back to make room for it. Buttons for famous starters: 27, 97, 871, 6171, 837799 (the longest chain under a million: 524 steps). Two sliders drawn as brass dive-gauge dials: "even angle" and "odd angle" re-shape the forest live; a toggle "by height" recolours edges by the peak value of the paths through them. Dragging the water (anywhere without a node) pushes a current that bends fronds away and lets them swing back. Life: a few small fish in orange and teal (simple procedural shapes with tail wag) weave between the stalks following a flow field and scatter from the pointer; bubbles rise from the floor occasionally and fork around fronds; particles of marine snow drift; a sea-urchin-like "1" sits at the holdfast, labelled, with 2, 4, 8, 16 marked up the main trunk and the first fork at 16 → 5 annotated in tiny type ("the first place an odd number joins"). Layout and typography: the forest occupies the full viewport; text is set in the upper left as on a museum aquarium panel: title in a soft, bold serif (Georgia/Palatino bold) in deep teal: "The Kelp Forest of 3n + 1", then three short sentences stating the rule and the open question, with the rule also drawn as a tiny two-branch diagram; statistics strip along the bottom in tabular small caps: numbers planted, longest path so far, highest peak, "counter-examples found: 0". Hints are designed into the panel: "touch a tip to follow it home". Entrance: the forest grows from the root upward over about 2.5 seconds (edges revealed in breadth-first order with a slight spring at each tip) while the light shafts fade in, then begins to sway. Reduced motion: forest drawn complete and still; highlighted paths appear without growth. Performance: geometry cached in typed arrays; edges batched by width/colour buckets; DPR capped at 2; N adapted to device; pause when hidden. Keyboard: the number field and buttons are focusable; arrow keys step the selected number up and down by 1 to compare neighbours.

Open Hell Box — Wood Type You Can Drop ↗
075

Hell Box — Wood Type You Can Drop

A jobbing printer's back room on a cream wall: a red hand-painted HELL BOX sign, a pinned notice from the Foreman, and a steel proofing-press bed framed by wooden furniture. Keys rain end-grain maple sorts onto the bed, each carrying a mirror-reversed letter stained with old ink, and they thud, lean and stack under a small rigid-body engine. Pulling a proof plays the whole press cycle — brayer, sheet, cylinder, peel, flip — and turns the accidental pile into a right-reading letterpress poster with salty ink, slur and deboss, which then flies up to a sagging drying line of pegged, swaying proofs. Overprinting in other inks builds layered chance posters that can be enlarged and saved.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A typographic toy set in a letterpress print shop. Every key the viewer presses drops a big block of wood type onto the bed of a proofing press: a heavy rectangular sort with a letter on its face, in one of several poster styles. The blocks fall, knock into each other, tumble and pile up with rigid-body physics. Then the viewer pulls a proof: the pile, exactly as it lies, is inked and printed onto a sheet of paper as a poster, which is hung on a drying line. Concept: disorder turned into a composition; chance typography with real print texture. Mood: noisy, cheerful, ink-stained; a jobbing printer's back room. Palette: newsprint/proof paper #F2E8D5, poster red #D1352B, press blue #1E3F66, chrome-yellow #F2B134, ink black #1A1A1A; wood tones (end-grain maple #C9A46A, darker #8B6B3F) for the blocks themselves, and a steel-grey press bed. Light page: the press bed is pale steel with the cream wall behind. Blocks: each sort is a rounded-corner rectangle whose width follows the letter (I narrow, M and W wide), rendered with wood grain (thin streaks and growth rings), bevelled edges, a slightly worn ink-stained face in the current ink colour residue, and the letter drawn large, reversed (mirror image, as real type is) on the block face in one of four wood-type styles approximated with system fonts and canvas treatments: a fat slab (Rockwell/"Courier New" bold with a heavy stroke), a condensed gothic (Impact/"Arial Narrow", squeezed), a Tuscan-ish decorative (a Didone/Georgia bold with pointed median spurs grown onto the stems on canvas), and a rounded grotesque ("Arial Rounded MT Bold", round-joined stroke). Three body sizes ("12-line", "8-line", "4-line"). All letters are set as capitals. Nicks, dents and a foundry stamp on some. Physics: a compact custom 2D rigid-body engine for rectangles (position, rotation, linear and angular velocity; gravity; SAT collision detection with clipped two-point manifolds, warm-started sequential impulses, friction and zero restitution so blocks thud rather than bounce; sleeping when at rest), tuned to be stable for piles of 40–60 blocks; walls are the press bed's furniture (wooden side rails) and the bed. Blocks can be dragged and thrown with the pointer (mouse/touch joint), and rotated with the wheel or two fingers while held. "Shake the bed" jolts everything. Performance capped: the oldest blocks are swept off the bed's right edge into the "hell box" (a crate labelled DEAD TYPE where discarded type goes) when the count or the total area of type on the bed exceeds the cap. Typing: any letter, numeral or common punctuation key drops its block from above at a position that advances left-to-right across the bed (a red marker on the top edge shows where the next sort falls), wrapping, with slight random rotation and spin so words land legible-but-drunken; Space advances; Enter starts a new line dropped from higher up; Backspace plucks the last block away with tweezers; an on-screen type case (a compartmented drawer laid A to Z with figures and points, plus Quad, New line and Tweezers) lets the viewer tap sorts, and a text field offers the system keyboard. Style and size selectors are drawn as labelled type-drawer fronts; ink colour as four ink tins with lids off (red, blue, yellow, black). Signature moment: "Pull a proof". A brayer (ink roller) sweeps across the pile leaving glistening ink on the block faces; a sheet of paper lowers over the bed; the press cylinder rolls across with a heavy eased motion and a slight bed shake while the impression shows through the back of the sheet; the sheet peels up and flips over to reveal the print: every letter now reading the right way round, in the chosen ink, at exactly the positions and angles the blocks lay in, with authentic letterpress texture: uneven "salty" ink coverage (noise-masked), heavier ink at one side of each letter, the faint impression of the block edges, slight slur, wood-grain showing through the solids, and a deboss shadow. Multiple pulls in different colours can overprint on the same sheet (multiply blend) if the viewer chooses "print again on this sheet", making layered two- and three-colour chance posters. Finished proofs fly up and are pegged to a drying line strung across the top of the page, swaying slightly; clicking one enlarges it; "save this proof" exports a PNG from canvas. Two house specimens ("HELL BOX" in three colours, "PRINT ANYWAY" in two), printed by the same routine, already hang on the line when the page opens. Entrance: the word "HELLO" drops onto the bed block by block in the first two seconds, each letter in a different face and ink stain, clattering into a leaning row, followed by a smaller "TYPE SOMETHING" in small sorts that land on top of it: the hint is itself made of blocks. Typography of the page chrome: shop-sign lettering for the title "HELL BOX" in condensed caps with a hand-painted feel (slight irregular baseline), small labels in typewriter face; a framed notice in the printer's voice: "Type falls where it likes. Print it anyway."; a job ticket counting sorts on the bed, proofs on the line and sorts gone to hell. Optional sound toggle (off by default): wooden knocks on collisions scaled by impulse (short filtered noise + low thump), roller squelch, the press's rumble. Reduced motion: blocks appear in a settled jumble without falling; the proof appears on the line without the press sequence. Keyboard focus styles on all controls; the page does not trap Tab.

Open Formicarium — Notes on a Colony ↗
079

Formicarium — Notes on a Colony

An open field notebook lies on a dark wooden desk. The left page is written up in a sepia italic hand: a title, two dated entries, a labelled pencil sketch of a worker ant from above, a three-step diagram of the two-scent rule, a live tally and an "Observations" log. The right page is the arena, with a pasted typewritten specimen label, a pressed lime leaf, a coffee ring, a ribbon in the gutter and a green pencil lying across the corner. Up to 1,500 ants pour from a drawn nest hole in a fan, find the waiting sugar within seconds, and stain the paper with green home-scent and rust food-scent that thickens into roads. The viewer adds sugar, draws graphite walls, rubs out roads, drops water and inspects individuals under a 4x loupe, while red-pencil notes with arrows write themselves beside notable events and are entered in the log with timestamps.

↗ Open

Original prompt

A simulation of an ant colony foraging, presented as a naturalist's field notebook spread: the ants run across the paper itself, between pencil sketches and handwritten notes, laying pheromone trails that appear as faint washes of ink which strengthen into roads. The viewer places crumbs of food and obstacles and watches the colony discover, recruit, optimise and re-route. Concept: emergent intelligence as marginalia; a living page. Mood: patient observation, summer afternoon, quiet delight. Palette: notebook paper #F2EAD3 (with ruled feint lines, a centre gutter shadow, a coffee ring and a pressed leaf), sepia ink #2B2118 for ants, sketches and handwriting, rust-red #A44A3F for the food pheromone wash, annotations and the queen's mark, moss green #5B7B5A for the home pheromone wash and leaf fragments. Light page. Simulation (Canvas 2D): 600–1,500 ants (scaled to device) as agents with position, heading and state (searching / returning with food). Two pheromone fields on a coarse grid (typed arrays): "home" pheromone laid by searching ants leaving the nest, "food" pheromone laid by ants carrying food, both evaporating and diffusing slowly. Each ant senses three points ahead (left, centre, right) for the pheromone it is seeking (searchers follow food pheromone; carriers follow home pheromone), steers toward the strongest with some randomness, wanders when it senses nothing, avoids obstacles and page edges, picks up a crumb on reaching food and turns around, drops it at the nest and turns again. From this, trails emerge within seconds of a food discovery, tighten from meanders into near-straight lines, and re-form when blocked. Render pheromones as soft watercolour-like stains on the paper (rust for food trails, green for home trails) using a low-resolution field upscaled with smoothing and multiplied onto the paper, so busy routes become rich ink roads with feathered edges; render ants as tiny three-segment bodies with legs suggested by a two-frame alternating stroke and antennae, oriented along their heading, carriers holding a visible pale crumb or leaf fragment in their jaws; at the nest entrance (a drawn hole with a crater of soil grains) ants disappear and emerge. The notebook: left page mostly "written": a title in careful handwriting-style italic (Georgia/Palatino italic, slightly irregular baseline via per-word rotation): "Formicarium: notes on a colony of Lasius-like garden ants, kept under glass", dated entries, a pencil sketch of an ant in dorsal view with labelled parts (head, mesosoma, petiole, gaster, antenna: correct basic anatomy) drawn in SVG line work with hatching, a small diagram of the two-pheromone rule in three steps, and a tally table that updates live in the writer's hand: "crumbs carried home: 214 · ants out: 630 · longest road: 41 cm". The right page is the open arena where most activity happens, but ants roam across both pages, walk around the sketch and over the handwriting. A ribbon bookmark, page number "37", and a pencil lying across the corner (an obstacle the ants path around). Interaction: click/tap to drop a sugar crumb pile (a cluster of pale granules; each pile holds a finite number of loads and dwindles visibly as it is carried away, then the trail to it fades); drag to draw a pencil line that becomes an obstacle wall (thick graphite stroke; ants treat it as impassable and find the gap or the long way round); a rubber eraser tool removes walls or wipes pheromone (a puddle also washes scent away) (rub out a trail and watch confusion, then recovery: the signature moment: a strong road is cut, ants pile up and scatter, scouts feel their way around, and within half a minute a new road is inked in by the colony); a "drop of water" tool makes a temporary puddle they avoid; a magnifying glass (hold Shift or a toggle) shows a 4× lens with individual ants in detail, antennae waving, exchanging food mouth to mouth when two meet (a brief pause). Controls drawn as items clipped to the notebook edge: sugar spoon, pencil, eraser, pipette, loupe, with handwritten labels; speed (×1, ×3) as a marginal note the viewer taps. Annotations that write themselves: when notable events happen, a short pencil note appears beside them in the margin with a hand-drawn arrow, written out stroke-like (clip reveal left to right): "first scout finds it: 0:14", "road established", "they have abandoned the long way", "traffic jam at the gap". An "Observations" list on the left page accumulates these with timestamps like a real field log. Entrance: the notebook lies open; one crumb pile is already on the right page; ants pour out of the nest hole in a fan and within the first three seconds the first few have reached the food and a faint rust line starts to appear. Typography: handwriting italic for notes, a typewriter face for the pasted specimen label ("COLONY 3 · collected under a paving stone · queen marked red"), tiny printed notebook maker's text in the corner. Reduced motion: ants move at one-third speed with trails pre-established; notes appear without writing animation. Performance: fields at ~160×100 resolution, ants in typed arrays, one path batch per state, DPR capped at 2; pause when hidden. Keyboard: tools selectable with 1–5; arrow keys move a placement cursor, Enter drops.
